# S02 — Complete examination with textbook-grounded option rationales

> Exam content and keyed letters are reproduced unchanged from the frozen export. Only the rationale layer was rewritten. Anesthesia textbooks are the exclusive source of truth.

# Paper 1

## S02-Q001

Which bedside airway-assessment approach most directly estimates the mandibular space available for tongue displacement?

A. Measure the thyromental distance.

B. Repeat Mallampati scoring during pregnancy and labor.

C. Combine Mallampati scoring with other airway assessments.

D. Use the Mallampati score as an indicator of laryngoscopy and intubation difficulty.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks which assessment most directly estimates the mandibular space available for tongue displacement. The thyromental distance is the measurement the cited passage explicitly links to that anatomic space, so A is the best answer. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 764, printed p. 697, passage ID 273626]

**A — correct:** Correct: the thyromental distance directly estimates the mandibular space requested by the lead-in. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 764, printed p. 697, passage ID 273626]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Mallampati scores can change during pregnancy and labor, but this does not make repeat Mallampati scoring the direct estimate of mandibular space asked for here. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 764, printed p. 697, passage ID 273626]

**C — less direct:** Combining assessments is supported because Mallampati alone has false-positive and false-negative results, but C does not identify the requested mandibular-space measurement. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 764, printed p. 697, passage ID 273626]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Mallampati score is associated with laryngoscopy and intubation difficulty, but that is a different prediction from estimating the mandibular space available for tongue displacement. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 764, printed p. 697, passage ID 273626]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient for one best answer. The same textbook passage supports the Mallampati distractors as true background statements while directly identifying thyromental distance for the requested anatomic estimate.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale repeated option claims without stating the stem-relative discriminator.

## S02-Q002

Which finding–implication pairing directly describes the consequence of limited mouth opening during direct laryngoscopy?

A. A beard — difficulty obtaining a mask seal.

B. Limited mouth opening — difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement.

C. Mallampati grade — the relationship among mouth opening, tongue size, and pharyngeal space.

D. A short thyromental distance — limited neck mobility or retrognathia.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the consequence specifically associated with limited mouth opening during direct laryngoscopy. The cited airway-assessment table directly pairs limited mouth opening with difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement, making B the stem-matched pairing. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 28. Airway Management, PDF p. 2302, passage ID 247157]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A beard can make a mask seal difficult, but the stem asks about limited mouth opening during laryngoscopy, not mask-seal difficulty. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 28. Airway Management, PDF p. 2302, passage ID 247157]

**B — correct:** Correct: the cited table directly identifies difficult blade insertion and tongue displacement as the consequence when mouth opening is limited. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 28. Airway Management, PDF p. 2302, passage ID 247157]

**C — true but irrelevant:** A short thyromental distance reflects neck mobility or retrognathia, but it is not the mouth-opening consequence requested by the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 28. Airway Management, PDF p. 2302, passage ID 247157]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Mallampati grade describes mouth opening, tongue size, and pharyngeal space, but it does not give the direct limited-mouth-opening consequence sought in this pairing question. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 28. Airway Management, PDF p. 2302, passage ID 247157]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The cited textbook table directly maps each airway-assessment finding to its implication, and B uniquely matches the limited-mouth-opening finding in the stem.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not distinguish the requested mouth-opening consequence from other true airway-assessment associations.

## S02-Q003

A severely obese patient cannot be positioned head-up and will undergo supine preoxygenation with CPAP. Which documented benefit best supports using 10 cm H2O rather than no positive pressure?

A. Combine CPAP during preoxygenation with PEEP and postinduction ventilation to extend nonhypoxemic apnea from 2 to 3 minutes.

B. Use a 25-degree head-up position rather than supine positioning without positive airway pressure to increase time to desaturation.

C. Ten cm H2O of CPAP during supine preoxygenation produced higher post-intubation PaO2 and less atelectasis.

D. Use 7.5 cm H2O CPAP supine for 3 minutes, which did not change time to desaturation to SpO2 90%.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem specifies supine preoxygenation in severe obesity and asks about 10 cm H2O CPAP versus no positive pressure. The cited study reports that 10 cm H2O CPAP in this exact supine preoxygenation setting produced higher post-intubation PaO2 and less atelectasis, which directly answers the lead-in. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 54. Anesthesia for Bariatric Surgery, PDF p. 2136, printed p. 1796, passage ID 290554]

**A — less direct:** CPAP combined with post-induction PEEP and ventilation increased nonhypoxemic apnea duration, but that is a different intervention comparison and outcome from the 10 cm H2O versus no-pressure question. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 54. Anesthesia for Bariatric Surgery, PDF p. 2136, printed p. 1796, passage ID 290554]

**B — wrong condition:** Head-up preoxygenation improved time to desaturation compared with supine preoxygenation without positive pressure, but the stem states that head-up positioning is unavailable and asks about CPAP. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 54. Anesthesia for Bariatric Surgery, PDF p. 2136, printed p. 1796, passage ID 290554]

**C — correct:** Correct: the cited evidence directly reports higher post-intubation PaO2 and decreased atelectasis with 10 cm H2O CPAP during supine preoxygenation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 54. Anesthesia for Bariatric Surgery, PDF p. 2136, printed p. 1796, passage ID 290554]

**D — wrong timing or magnitude:** The cited 7.5 cm H2O CPAP for three minutes did not change desaturation time; that different pressure and outcome do not negate the documented 10 cm H2O PaO2/atelectasis benefit. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 54. Anesthesia for Bariatric Surgery, PDF p. 2136, printed p. 1796, passage ID 290554]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The textbook passage supplies the exact pressure, positioning, comparator, and outcomes used by the stem.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale repeated study findings without explaining why C matches the specified pressure and outcome.

## S02-Q004

Only 30 seconds are available before induction, so the team is weighing a rapid breathing regimen against longer tidal-volume preoxygenation. Which statement captures the four-breath tradeoff?

A. Eight deep breaths over 1 minute appear to protect against apnea desaturation better than four deep breaths over 30 seconds.

B. Pregnancy-associated reduced functional residual capacity and increased oxygen consumption accelerate hypoxemia during apnea.

C. Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing through a tight-fitting mask is the ideal preoxygenation method.

D. Four maximal breaths over 30 seconds can achieve a similar PaO2 but do not provide the same protection from rapid desaturation.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** With only 30 seconds available, the key distinction is between achieving a similar PaO2 and protecting against rapid desaturation during apnea. The cited pregnancy-anesthesia passage states that four maximal breaths can achieve a similar PaO2 but do not provide the same protection, so D captures the four-breath tradeoff. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 600, printed p. 591, passage ID 272842]

**A — wrong timing or magnitude:** Eight deep breaths over one minute may protect better than four breaths over 30 seconds, but it does not describe the four-breath tradeoff when only 30 seconds are available. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 600, printed p. 591, passage ID 272842]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Pregnancy-related lower FRC and increased oxygen consumption explain why hypoxemia develops more rapidly, but B does not state the four-breath preoxygenation tradeoff asked for. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 600, printed p. 591, passage ID 272842]

**C — less direct:** Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing is described as ideal, but it is not feasible under the stem’s 30-second constraint and does not express the four-breath comparison. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 600, printed p. 591, passage ID 272842]

**D — correct:** Correct: four maximal breaths over 30 seconds can achieve a similar PaO2, but the source explicitly says that equivalent protection from rapid oxyhemoglobin desaturation is not afforded. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 600, printed p. 591, passage ID 272842]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The source directly distinguishes PaO2 achievement from protection against rapid apnea desaturation.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not identify the PaO2-versus-desaturation-protection discriminator.

## S02-Q005

Under the Difficult Airway Society (DAS) 2025 guidelines Plan-A strategy, a hyperangulated videolaryngoscope provides the view but tube delivery remains difficult. Which adjunct is described for use with that blade?

A. Use a flexible bronchoscope as an adjunct with the hyperangulated videolaryngoscope blade.

B. Use direct laryngoscopy rather than videolaryngoscopy as the first-line technique.

C. Use a videolaryngoscope first line to facilitate tracheal intubation.

D. Use an optical stylet as an additional alternative device for intubation.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** evidence_limited

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The frozen export supports the general use and performance of videolaryngoscopy, but the keyed flexible-bronchoscope adjunct is established only by a non-textbook DAS guideline record. Under the textbook-only rule, the keyed adjunct and its exclusions cannot be supported here.

**A — evidence limited:** The keyed flexible-bronchoscope adjunct is not established by the permitted textbook evidence attached to this item.

**B — evidence limited:** The attached textbook evidence contrasts videolaryngoscopy with direct laryngoscopy but does not establish this complete first-line exclusion for the stated DAS Plan-A question.

**C — evidence limited:** The attached textbook evidence supports videolaryngoscope performance generally but does not establish the complete guideline-specific option relationship required here.

**D — evidence limited:** The attached textbook evidence does not establish whether an optical stylet is the appropriate alternative under the named guideline strategy.

**Evidence sufficiency:** Insufficient under textbook-only authority. The frozen packet mixes textbook records with DAS 2025 guideline records, and the exact keyed flexible-bronchoscope adjunct is not supported by a textbook quotation. Requires new textbook retrieval or a separately authorized guideline workflow.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence includes guideline-only support for the key and several exclusions.
- Do not use the guideline citation as textbook authority.

## S02-Q011

A patient's cardiac output falls after induction. Which framework most completely identifies the direct determinants that should be assessed?

A. The relation of preload to stroke volume or cardiac output.

B. Heart rate, preload, myocardial contractility, and afterload.

C. The linear end-systolic pressure-volume relationship.

D. The effect of preload alone on stroke volume.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks for the most complete framework to assess after cardiac output falls. The cited Miller passage identifies four determinants of cardiac output; B is the only option that presents the complete determinant framework rather than one relationship or a pressure-volume representation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 13. Cardiac Physiology, PDF p. 348, printed p. 279, passage ID 282112]

**A — less direct:** Increasing preload can increase stroke volume on the cardiac function curve, but it is one determinant relationship rather than the complete framework requested after cardiac output falls. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 13. Cardiac Physiology, PDF p. 348, printed p. 279, passage ID 282112]

**B — correct:** Correct: it supplies the complete four-factor framework for assessing the direct determinants of cardiac output. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 13. Cardiac Physiology, PDF p. 348, printed p. 279, passage ID 282112]

**C — less direct:** The cardiac function curve relates preload to stroke volume or cardiac output, but that curve is only one component of the broader determinant framework. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 13. Cardiac Physiology, PDF p. 348, printed p. 279, passage ID 282112]

**D — true but irrelevant:** The ESPVR is a valid pressure-volume representation, but it does not provide the direct cardiac-output determinant framework asked for in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 13. Cardiac Physiology, PDF p. 348, printed p. 279, passage ID 282112]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient for the keyed framework, although the frozen quote is brief; the stored claim identifies the four determinants and the alternatives are narrower representations.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not explain why the complete framework is more responsive than each single relationship.

## S02-Q012

When estimating left-ventricular coronary perfusion, why is aortic diastolic rather than systolic pressure used as the upstream pressure term?

A. Because systolic contraction reduces intraventricular pressure and coronary resistance.

B. Because higher aortic pressure lowers afterload and wall tension.

C. Because 70% to 80% of coronary arterial flow occurs during diastole.

D. Because LVEDP replaces aortic pressure during diastole.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks why aortic diastolic pressure is the upstream term for left-ventricular coronary perfusion. Miller states that 70% to 80% of coronary arterial flow occurs during diastole, directly explaining why diastolic rather than systolic aortic pressure is used. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Systolic contraction increases intraventricular pressure and coronary resistance, but that describes an impediment during systole rather than the decisive reason the upstream pressure term is aortic diastolic pressure. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**B — true but irrelevant:** LVEDP is subtracted from aortic diastolic pressure and can impede subendocardial flow, but it is the downstream opposing term, not the reason diastolic aortic pressure is selected as the upstream term. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**C — correct:** Correct: most coronary arterial blood flow occurs during diastole, so aortic diastolic pressure is physiologically relevant to left-ventricular coronary perfusion. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A rise in blood pressure may increase coronary flow while also increasing afterload, wall tension, and oxygen demand, but this does not explain the diastolic upstream-pressure choice. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The key physiologic proportion and the LVEDP relationship are directly present in the frozen textbook passage.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale lacked the diastolic-flow discriminator and did not classify the other true statements as less direct.

## S02-Q013

Across a tissue bed, the pressure difference doubles while vascular resistance remains constant. What happens to blood flow?

A. It is unchanged because only viscosity and vessel length determine resistance.

B. It cannot be determined without mean arterial pressure, right atrial pressure, and cardiac output.

C. It increases sixteenfold because flow follows the fourth power of vessel radius.

D. It doubles because flow is proportional to pressure difference and inversely proportional to resistance.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** With the pressure difference doubled and resistance unchanged, the direct pressure-flow relationship predicts a doubled tissue blood flow. The cited Poiseuille-law passage states that flow is directly proportional to pressure difference and inversely proportional to resistance, making D the complete relationship. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1184, passage ID 313797]

**A — false:** Option A describes a resistance relationship, but resistance is unchanged in this stem; it does not give the flow result when pressure difference doubles. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1184, passage ID 313797]

**B — false:** Option B describes a resistance relationship, but resistance is unchanged in this stem; it does not give the flow result when pressure difference doubles. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1184, passage ID 313797]

**C — false:** Option C describes a resistance relationship, but resistance is unchanged in this stem; it does not give the flow result when pressure difference doubles. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1184, passage ID 313797]

**D — correct:** Correct: it states both proportionalities needed to infer the flow change under the stem’s unchanged-resistance condition. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1184, passage ID 313797]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. D is the only option that directly applies both the changed pressure difference and unchanged resistance.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not apply the stated variable change to the flow relationship.

## S02-Q014

A patient with coronary disease becomes markedly tachycardic and develops ischemic changes despite preserved mean arterial pressure. Which physiological fact best explains the loss of coronary oxygen supply?

A. Seventy to eighty percent of coronary arterial blood flow occurs during diastole.

B. LV coronary perfusion pressure equals aortic diastolic pressure minus LV end-diastolic pressure.

C. Higher blood pressure increases coronary flow while also increasing afterload and oxygen demand.

D. An elevated LV end-diastolic pressure impedes subendocardial blood flow.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Despite preserved mean arterial pressure, marked tachycardia shortens diastole. Miller states that 70% to 80% of coronary arterial blood flow occurs during diastole; that is the physiological fact that best explains reduced coronary oxygen supply in the stated scenario. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**A — correct:** Correct: because most coronary flow occurs during diastole, tachycardia can reduce the time available for coronary oxygen supply even when mean pressure is preserved. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Elevated LVEDP can impede subendocardial blood flow, but the stem’s defining new change is marked tachycardia with preserved mean pressure; B is not the best explanation of that change. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**C — true but irrelevant:** The LV coronary-perfusion formula uses aortic diastolic pressure minus LVEDP, but C is a calculation relationship and does not identify the time-dependent supply fact driving this presentation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A rise in blood pressure can increase coronary flow while also increasing afterload and oxygen demand, but mean arterial pressure is preserved in the stem and D does not explain the tachycardia-related supply loss. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1936, printed p. 1626, passage ID 289609]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient for A as the best explanation. B–D are supported physiologic statements but are less directly responsive to the stem’s tachycardia/diastolic-time change.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not connect tachycardia to the diastolic fraction of coronary blood flow.

## S02-Q015

Under otherwise unchanged conditions, a patient's horizontal ST depression in V5 progresses from 1 mm to 3 mm. Which interpretation is best supported?

A. Both tracings only cross a binary ischemia threshold, so the magnitude adds no information.

B. The larger ST-segment depression indicates more severe myocardial ischemia.

C. The magnitude cannot be interpreted unless lead II is monitored simultaneously.

D. The change identifies the coronary territory but does not indicate severity.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem describes ST depression increasing from 1 to 3 mm and asks for the best interpretation of that progression. The cited passage directly states that the degree of ST-segment depression parallels the severity of myocardial ischemia, so B is the stem-specific interpretation. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 5 Ischemic heart disease, PDF p. 117, passage ID 232760]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The 1-mm ST-change threshold and T-wave inversion are diagnostic features of ischemia, but A does not interpret the increasing magnitude from 1 to 3 mm. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 5 Ischemic heart disease, PDF p. 117, passage ID 232760]

**B — correct:** Correct: the cited textbook directly relates greater ST-segment depression to greater myocardial-ischemia severity, matching the progression in the stem. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 5 Ischemic heart disease, PDF p. 117, passage ID 232760]

**C — true but irrelevant:** V5 reflects a left-ventricular region supplied by the LAD, but identifying the territory does not interpret the change in ST-depression magnitude. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 5 Ischemic heart disease, PDF p. 117, passage ID 232760]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Two-lead monitoring with II and V5 is described as traditional, but monitoring configuration does not explain why the increasing ST depression indicates greater ischemia. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 5 Ischemic heart disease, PDF p. 117, passage ID 232760]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The key claim directly addresses the change in ST-segment-depression magnitude; the other claims are true but answer different questions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not explain the magnitude/severity relationship.

**C — evidence limited:** The keyed aspirin-continuation claim is supported only by an excluded living-web guideline record, not by a permitted textbook quotation; it is not textbook-grounded.

**D — evidence limited:** No permitted textbook evidence in the frozen item establishes the stated adverse-outcome timing relationship; it is not supported here.

**Evidence sufficiency:** Insufficient under the textbook-only rule. The frozen evidence records are all `living_web_guidance`; retrieve textbook evidence or route this explicitly through an authorized guideline workflow before rewriting.

- Do not treat the current keyed letter as textbook-supported.

**C — evidence limited:** No permitted textbook quotation in the frozen item establishes this interruption and resumption interval; it is not supported here.

**D — evidence limited:** The keyed non-user aspirin statement is supported only by an excluded living-web guideline record, not by permitted textbook evidence; it is not textbook-grounded.

## S02-Q018

Which definition encompasses both the systolic and diastolic pathways by which left-ventricular dysfunction produces heart failure?

A. Failure to eject enough blood for metabolic demand or failure to fill without elevated pressure.

B. Failure of a chamber to collect blood effectively at a normal filling pressure.

C. Left-ventricular diastolic dysfunction with ventricular and arterial stiffening.

D. Left-ventricular systolic dysfunction with a reduced ejection fraction.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for the definition spanning both systolic and diastolic pathways. Barash defines heart failure using both inability to eject sufficiently and inability to fill without elevated pressure; A is therefore the complete definition rather than a single phenotype or component. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 12. Cardiac Anatomy and Physiology, PDF p. 841, passage ID 244672]

**A — correct:** Correct: A includes both inadequate ejection and impaired filling at elevated pressure, satisfying the inclusive lead-in. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 12. Cardiac Anatomy and Physiology, PDF p. 841, passage ID 244672]

**B — incomplete:** B defines diastolic function alone and therefore does not encompass the systolic ejection pathway requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 12. Cardiac Anatomy and Physiology, PDF p. 841, passage ID 244672]

**C — incomplete:** C identifies systolic dysfunction in HFrEF but omits the alternative filling pathway and is not the requested general definition. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 12. Cardiac Anatomy and Physiology, PDF p. 841, passage ID 244672]

**D — incomplete:** D lists common HFpEF-associated abnormalities, but it describes a phenotype-associated group rather than a definition covering both pathways. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 12. Cardiac Anatomy and Physiology, PDF p. 841, passage ID 244672]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen textbook definition directly contains both ejection and filling failure.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not distinguish the complete definition from partial pathway or phenotype statements.

## S02-Q019

A patient has clinical heart failure and a left-ventricular ejection fraction of 52%. Using the passage-defined threshold, which phenotype applies?

A. Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.

B. Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction.

C. The phenotype cannot be assigned because diastolic dysfunction occurs in both HFrEF and HFpEF.

D. Heart failure without an ejection-fraction phenotype because filling and ejection can both be impaired.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem supplies an ejection fraction of 52% and directs use of the passage-defined threshold. Stoelting Coexisting Disease states that HFpEF is diagnosed at an ejection fraction of 50% or more, so the stated phenotype is HFpEF. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 10 Heart failure and cardiomyopathies, PDF p. 214, passage ID 233176]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Diastolic dysfunction can occur in both HFrEF and HFpEF, but A does not classify the 52% ejection fraction using the threshold requested. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 10 Heart failure and cardiomyopathies, PDF p. 214, passage ID 233176]

**B — correct:** Correct: 52% is at or above the cited 50% threshold for HFpEF. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 10 Heart failure and cardiomyopathies, PDF p. 214, passage ID 233176]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Structural or functional impairment of filling or ejection defines heart failure generally, but C does not identify the phenotype for the supplied ejection fraction. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 10 Heart failure and cardiomyopathies, PDF p. 214, passage ID 233176]

**D — wrong timing or magnitude:** The cited HFrEF threshold is 40% or less, which does not include the stem’s ejection fraction of 52%. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 10 Heart failure and cardiomyopathies, PDF p. 214, passage ID 233176]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The passage supplies explicit EF thresholds for HFpEF and HFrEF.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale repeated threshold statements without applying 52% to the stated cutoff.

## S02-Q020

In hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, left-ventricular systolic pressure rises after a premature ventricular contraction while arterial pulse pressure falls. Which change best explains this response?

A. An outflow obstruction gradient of at least 30 mm Hg is clinically significant.

B. Dynamic left-ventricular outflow tract obstruction is a hallmark of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

C. The left-ventricular outflow obstruction gradient increases after the premature beat.

D. The hypertrophic-cardiomyopathy murmur becomes louder with Valsalva or standing.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** A premature ventricular contraction is followed in the stem by increased LV systolic pressure and reduced arterial pulse pressure. The cited Chestnut passage directly identifies an increased obstruction gradient after a PVC, which is the best explanation. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 41 Cardiovascular Disease, PDF p. 1084, printed p. 1018, passage ID 275071]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A clinically significant gradient threshold is a severity criterion, not the dynamic post-PVC change described by the stem. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 41 Cardiovascular Disease, PDF p. 1084, printed p. 1018, passage ID 275071]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Dynamic outflow obstruction is a hallmark of HCM, but B does not explain the specific change after a PVC. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 41 Cardiovascular Disease, PDF p. 1084, printed p. 1018, passage ID 275071]

**C — correct:** Correct: the obstruction gradient typically increases after a PVC, matching the stated LV-pressure and arterial-pulse response. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 41 Cardiovascular Disease, PDF p. 1084, printed p. 1018, passage ID 275071]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A louder murmur with Valsalva or standing reflects dynamic obstruction, but it is not the post-PVC response asked for. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 41 Cardiovascular Disease, PDF p. 1084, printed p. 1018, passage ID 275071]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The direct post-PVC gradient statement uniquely matches the observed hemodynamic response.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale repeated options without identifying the post-PVC obstruction-gradient discriminator.

## S02-Q032

In a structured ICU handoff, which intervention is the most direct safety benefit of implementing a formal protocol?

A. The handoff process is intended to be strictly sequential, with monitoring transferred before ventilator transfer, and all phase 1 items completed before information transfer.

B. During phase 2 of the handoff, the anesthesiologist presents patient-specific information including age, weight, medical and surgical history, allergy status, baseline vital signs, pertinent laboratory results, diagnosis, current condition, and vital signs.

C. Using formal, sequential handoff procedures does not increase the duration of the handoff process.

D. Reduce information omission and errors.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for the most direct safety benefit of a formal ICU handoff protocol. Miller explicitly states that implementing such a protocol reduces information omission and errors, which directly answers the safety-benefit question. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1909, printed p. 1599, passage ID 289484]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Sequential transfer of monitoring and ventilator care is part of the process, but it describes protocol order rather than the direct safety benefit asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1909, printed p. 1599, passage ID 289484]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Patient-specific information is presented during phase 2, but B describes handoff content and does not identify the protocol’s direct safety outcome. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1909, printed p. 1599, passage ID 289484]

**C — true but irrelevant:** The protocol does not increase process duration, but that is an efficiency finding rather than the direct safety benefit requested by the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1909, printed p. 1599, passage ID 289484]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller directly states that implementing a handoff protocol reduces information omission and errors, exactly identifying the safety benefit. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1909, printed p. 1599, passage ID 289484]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen passage distinguishes protocol sequence, content, duration, and the direct safety outcome.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The original rationale should distinguish process features from the protocol’s patient-safety outcome.

## S02-Q033

In the text's discrete list of personal factors predisposing to anesthesia error, which set includes production pressure?

A. Expertise or procedural familiarity, distraction, fatigue, and production pressure.

B. Faulty clinical decision-making identified in 54% of reviewed airway medicolegal cases.

C. Distraction, sleep deprivation, fatigue, noise, illness, mental acuity, aging, and team social dynamics.

D. Sleep-deprivation psychomotor changes resembling alcohol or drug intoxication.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the discrete set of personal factors that includes production pressure. Miller’s list pairs expertise or procedural familiarity, distractions, fatigue, and production pressure as factors predisposing to error, so A is the exact set. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 6. Human Behavior and Simulation in Anesthesia, PDF p. 134, printed p. 99, passage ID 281058]

**A — correct:** Correct: the cited Miller list explicitly includes expertise or familiarity, distractions, fatigue, and production pressure as personal factors that can predispose to errors. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 6. Human Behavior and Simulation in Anesthesia, PDF p. 134, printed p. 99, passage ID 281058]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Faulty clinical decision-making in 54% of reviewed airway cases is a medicolegal-review finding, but B is not the requested discrete personal-factor set. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 6. Human Behavior and Simulation in Anesthesia, PDF p. 134, printed p. 99, passage ID 281058]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Distraction, sleep deprivation, fatigue, noise, illness, acuity, aging, and team dynamics can influence performance, but C is a broader influence list and does not supply the discrete set containing production pressure. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 6. Human Behavior and Simulation in Anesthesia, PDF p. 133, printed p. 6, passage ID 281055]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Sleep-deprivation psychomotor effects resembling intoxication are described in Barash, but D is an analogy and not the Miller personal-factor set requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 3. Occupational Health and Well-Being, PDF p. 241, passage ID 243529]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen textbook evidence directly identifies the requested set and distinguishes the other true but differently framed findings.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The wording asks for a discrete list, so the broader performance-factor list is not interchangeable with the keyed set.

## S02-Q034

Which departmental adverse-event reporting policy best supports a nonpunitive learning culture?

A. Discourage departmental adverse-event reporting.

B. Encourage voluntary reporting without threat of punishment.

C. Mandate every report rather than accepting voluntary reporting.

D. Use punitive sanctions to compel reporting.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for a departmental policy supporting a nonpunitive learning culture. Miller directly recommends voluntary adverse-event reporting without threat of punishment, so B supplies both required policy elements. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 5. Quality Improvement in Anesthesia Practice and Patient Safety, PDF p. 122, printed p. 90, passage ID 281004]

**A — false:** Discouraging adverse-event reporting conflicts with Miller’s recommendation to encourage voluntary reporting, so A cannot support the learning culture described in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 5. Quality Improvement in Anesthesia Practice and Patient Safety, PDF p. 122, printed p. 90, passage ID 281004]

**B — correct:** Correct: the cited departmental recommendation is voluntary reporting without the threat of punishment, directly matching a nonpunitive learning culture. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 5. Quality Improvement in Anesthesia Practice and Patient Safety, PDF p. 122, printed p. 90, passage ID 281004]

**C — false:** Mandatory reporting conflicts with Miller’s voluntary-reporting recommendation, so C does not match the nonpunitive culture described. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 5. Quality Improvement in Anesthesia Practice and Patient Safety, PDF p. 122, printed p. 90, passage ID 281004]

**D — false:** Punitive sanctions directly contradict the cited recommendation to report without threat of punishment, so D does not support the requested culture. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 5. Quality Improvement in Anesthesia Practice and Patient Safety, PDF p. 122, printed p. 90, passage ID 281004]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Miller’s frozen passage directly states the policy and supports exclusion of suppression, mandatory coercion, and punitive sanctions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Some frozen evidence records included non-textbook or mismatched supporting claims; this rewrite uses the direct Miller textbook passage only.

## S02-Q035

According to Miller’s Anesthesia, 10th edition, a grade 1–2 hypersensitivity reaction has generalized urticaria or angioedema without hemodynamic compromise. Which treatment relationship is stated?

A. For grade 5 anaphylaxis presenting as cardiopulmonary arrest, advanced cardiac life support methods are used.

B. For grade 4 (severe) anaphylaxis, epinephrine 0.01 mg/kg IM is given, and if there is no response, an epinephrine infusion is started, along with a 1 L crystalloid bolus repeated if no response and vasopressin for persistent hypotension lasting more than 10 minutes.

C. Epinephrine is not needed; antihistamines are used instead.

D. For grade 3 (mild-to-moderate) intraoperative anaphylaxis, epinephrine 0.01 mg/kg IM (maximum 0.5 mg in adults and 0.3 mg in children) is given into the thigh, along with a 0.5 L crystalloid bolus repeated if there is no response.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem defines a grade 1–2 reaction as urticaria or angioedema without hemodynamic compromise. Miller’s treatment table pairs that grade with no epinephrine and antihistamines, whereas the epinephrine and crystalloid regimens belong to more severe grades; C is therefore correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1095, printed p. 920, passage ID 285644]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Advanced cardiac-life-support methods are listed for grade 5 cardiopulmonary arrest, but A describes a different severity grade from the stable grade 1–2 reaction in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1095, printed p. 920, passage ID 285644]

**B — wrong condition:** The epinephrine infusion, larger crystalloid bolus, and vasopressin regimen is the grade 4 treatment, not the no-hemodynamic-compromise grade 1–2 treatment asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1095, printed p. 920, passage ID 285644]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller’s table states that grade 1–2 hypersensitivity without hemodynamic compromise needs no epinephrine and is treated with antihistamines. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1095, printed p. 920, passage ID 285644]

**D — wrong condition:** Intramuscular epinephrine and a 0.5-L crystalloid bolus are listed for grade 3 intraoperative anaphylaxis, but D applies to a more severe presentation than the grade 1–2 reaction described. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1095, printed p. 920, passage ID 285644]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller treatment table places each regimen by grade and directly supports the grade 1–2 answer.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key discriminator is severity and hemodynamic compromise; regimens from grades 3–5 must not be generalized to grade 1–2.

## S02-Q036

During venous air embolism, what pattern is expected when exhaled nitrogen concentration is monitored?

A. A gradual decrease in exhaled nitrogen concentration.

B. No change in exhaled nitrogen concentration.

C. A sudden decrease in exhaled nitrogen concentration.

D. A sudden increase in exhaled nitrogen concentration.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks for the exhaled-nitrogen pattern during venous air embolism. Cote directly lists nitrogen monitoring as showing a sudden increase in nitrogen concentration in exhaled breath, so D is the observed pattern. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**A — wrong timing or magnitude:** A gradual decrease is the opposite of the sudden increase reported for nitrogen monitoring during venous air embolism, so it does not match the textbook pattern. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**B — false:** No change is inconsistent with Cote’s explicit finding of a sudden increase in exhaled nitrogen concentration during venous air embolism and does not match the reported response. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**C — wrong timing or magnitude:** A sudden decrease reverses the direction of the nitrogen-monitoring response stated in the cited passage, which is a sudden increase, and therefore does not match the report. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**D — correct:** Correct: Cote explicitly reports a sudden increase in nitrogen concentration in exhaled breath with nitrogen monitoring during venous air embolism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the direction and abruptness of the nitrogen-monitoring response.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question tests the direction of change; other embolism-monitoring findings in the passage should not be substituted for nitrogen concentration.

## S02-Q037

At the first signs of local-anesthetic systemic toxicity, help has already been summoned. Which immediate airway and oxygenation action should follow?

A. Secure the airway and ventilate with 100% oxygen.

B. Give the 20% lipid-emulsion bolus as the airway intervention.

C. Give a benzodiazepine despite the absence of seizure activity.

D. Summon help for the first time.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Help has already been summoned, so the stem asks for the next immediate airway and oxygenation action in local-anesthetic systemic toxicity. Barash directs securing the airway and ventilating with 100% oxygen, making A the next step. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1721, passage ID 246193]

**A — correct:** Correct: the LAST management passage explicitly states to secure the airway and ventilate with 100% oxygen, matching the requested airway/oxygenation action. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1721, passage ID 246193]

**B — true but irrelevant:** A 20% lipid-emulsion bolus is part of LAST treatment, but it is a separate rescue therapy and not the immediate airway-and-oxygenation action asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1721, passage ID 246193]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Benzodiazepines are listed for halting seizures during LAST, but the stem does not describe seizure activity and asks first for airway and oxygenation management. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1721, passage ID 246193]

**D — incomplete:** Summoning help is an initial LAST action, but the stem states that help has already been summoned; D therefore fails the requested next action. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1721, passage ID 246193]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash management sequence contains the airway, lipid, seizure, and help actions, and the stem fixes the relevant timing.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The phrase ‘help has already been summoned’ makes the sequence discriminator essential.

## S02-Q038

During an acute malignant-hyperthermia crisis, which trigger-related action is required first?

A. Dysrhythmias during malignant hyperthermia treatment should be controlled with lidocaine while avoiding calcium channel blockers.

B. Discontinue halogenated inhaled anesthetics and succinylcholine.

C. Management of malignant hyperthermia includes hyperventilating the patient with 100% oxygen.

D. The initial dantrolene dose for treatment of an acute malignant hyperthermia episode is 2.5 mg/kg.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks which trigger-related action is required first during an acute malignant-hyperthermia crisis. Barash places discontinuation of halogenated inhaled anesthetics and succinylcholine among the immediate trigger-removal actions, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 24. Rare Coexisting Diseases, PDF p. 1866, passage ID 246459]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Lidocaine and avoidance of calcium-channel blockers address dysrhythmia management, but A is not the first trigger-removal action requested by the crisis stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 24. Rare Coexisting Diseases, PDF p. 1866, passage ID 246459]

**B — correct:** Correct: the cited malignant-hyperthermia protocol explicitly says to discontinue triggering agents, including halogenated inhaled anesthetics and succinylcholine. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 24. Rare Coexisting Diseases, PDF p. 1866, passage ID 246459]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Hyperventilation with 100% oxygen is also part of malignant-hyperthermia management, but C addresses oxygenation after identifying the trigger and does not state the requested trigger-removal action. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 24. Rare Coexisting Diseases, PDF p. 1866, passage ID 246459]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Dantrolene 2.5 mg/kg is a treatment action, but the question asks first for removal of triggering agents; D does not answer that trigger-related lead-in. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 24. Rare Coexisting Diseases, PDF p. 1866, passage ID 246459]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash protocol directly states trigger discontinuation and provides the other management actions for comparison.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The options are all protocol actions; the decisive distinction is trigger removal as the requested first trigger-related intervention.

## S02-Q039

A patient refuses a recommended anesthetic after discussing alternatives. Which capacity element is being assessed when asking whether the patient appreciates the disorder and consequences?

A. The final functional element of competence is whether the patient can communicate a choice.

B. One functional element of competence is whether the patient can receive and understand treatment-related information.

C. Insight about the disorder, consequences, and potential treatment options.

D. A further functional element of competence is whether the patient is able to logically compare the risks and benefits of treatment alternatives.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks which capacity element is assessed by asking whether the patient appreciates the disorder and its consequences. Miller’s functional-capacity list names insight about the disorder, consequences, and treatment options, so C is the exact element. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 154, printed p. 117, passage ID 281158]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Communicating a choice is another functional element of competence, but the stem asks about appreciating the disorder and consequences, not communicating the final choice. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 154, printed p. 117, passage ID 281158]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Receiving and understanding treatment-related information is a separate functional element, but it is not the insight element specified by the stem’s appreciation question. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 154, printed p. 117, passage ID 281158]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller identifies insight about the disorder, its consequences, and potential treatment options as the functional element being tested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 154, printed p. 117, passage ID 281158]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Logically comparing risks and benefits is another capacity element, but D does not answer the stem’s specific appreciation/insight wording. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 154, printed p. 117, passage ID 281158]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage lists the functional elements distinctly and directly names insight as the requested element.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The item tests discrimination among closely related functional capacity elements.

## S02-Q040

A capacitated patient refuses treatment because other values outweigh the proposed medical outcome. Which interpretation is appropriate?

A. The majority of patients referred for capacity evaluations are found to have decision-making capacity.

B. Capacity assessment tools have been shown to be more likely to classify a female or non-White patient as lacking capacity.

C. Functional capacity for decision-making must be judged separately from the perceived quality of the decision itself.

D. Treatment refusal may reflect values rather than impaired decision-making capacity.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem says the patient is capacitated and refuses because other values outweigh the proposed medical outcome. Miller states that treatment refusal may reflect prioritization of other values rather than a capacity problem, so D is the appropriate interpretation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 153, printed p. 116, passage ID 281155]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The majority finding among referred capacity evaluations is a general referral observation, but it does not interpret this capacitated patient’s values-based refusal. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 153, printed p. 116, passage ID 281155]

**B — true but irrelevant:** The cited disparity in classification by some capacity tools is an assessment-bias observation, but it does not explain the values-based refusal described in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 153, printed p. 116, passage ID 281155]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Separating functional capacity from the perceived quality of a decision is an important principle, but C states the general rule rather than the stem-specific interpretation that values can explain refusal. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 153, printed p. 116, passage ID 281155]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller explicitly says treatment refusal may simply reflect prioritization of other values above medical outcomes rather than impaired decision-making capacity, matching the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 7. Ethical Aspects of Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 153, printed p. 116, passage ID 281155]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the values-based explanation and provides the contextual principles represented by the distractors.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem explicitly supplies capacity, so refusal should not be equated with a poor outcome preference or incapacity.

## S02-Q050

After a large acute myocardial infarction, a patient develops pulmonary edema, cool extremities, low cardiac output, and increased systemic vascular resistance. Which shock type best integrates these findings?

A. Cardiogenic shock.

B. Distributive shock.

C. Obstructive shock.

D. Hypovolemic shock.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient has acute myocardial infarction, pulmonary edema, cool extremities, low cardiac output, and increased SVR. Barash characterizes cardiogenic shock by heart failure with low cardiac output and increased SVR, so A integrates the findings. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash identifies cardiogenic shock as heart failure characterized by low cardiac output and increased systemic vascular resistance, matching this post-infarction picture. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**B — wrong condition:** Distributive shock is associated with reduced SVR and often increased cardiac output, which does not match the low-output, high-SVR findings in the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**C — wrong condition:** Obstructive shock is due to obstruction of forward flow such as tension pneumothorax, tamponade, or pulmonary embolism; this does not match the post-infarction failure pattern described here. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Hypovolemic shock can also have low cardiac output and increased peripheral resistance, but the acute infarction and pulmonary edema point to cardiac pump failure rather than hemorrhagic volume loss. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly contrasts cardiogenic, distributive, obstructive, and hypovolemic shock profiles.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Low cardiac output is not by itself unique; the infarction, pulmonary edema, and high-SVR context identify cardiogenic shock.

## S02-Q051

A patient in cardiogenic shock has pulmonary edema and cool extremities. Which hemodynamic profile is expected?

A. Low cardiac output with increased peripheral resistance from hemorrhage.

B. Low cardiac output with increased systemic vascular resistance.

C. Reduced SVR with often increased cardiac output in sepsis.

D. Obstruction to forward flow from tension pneumothorax, tamponade, or pulmonary embolism.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem identifies cardiogenic shock and asks for its expected hemodynamic profile. Barash directly characterizes it by low cardiac output and increased systemic vascular resistance, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Low output with increased peripheral resistance can describe hypovolemic shock from hemorrhage, but A does not give the cardiogenic profile in this stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**B — correct:** Correct: cardiogenic shock from heart failure is characterized by low cardiac output and increased systemic vascular resistance. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**C — wrong condition:** Reduced SVR with often increased cardiac output is the distributive/septic profile, not the low-output cardiogenic profile asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**D — wrong condition:** Obstruction to forward flow defines obstructive shock, whereas the stem identifies cardiogenic pump failure; D describes a different shock mechanism. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4825, passage ID 251734]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly states the cardiogenic profile and contrasts the other shock categories.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Cardiogenic and hypovolemic shock can both have low output and high resistance; the stem’s cardiac-failure context is decisive.

## S02-Q052

According to Miller’s Anesthesia, 10th edition, septic shock remains hypotensive after fluid. Which first-line vasopressor relationship is stated?

A. The initial septic-shock MAP target is 65 mm Hg.

B. Vasopressin or epinephrine may be added as a second agent when needed.

C. Norepinephrine is the recommended first-line vasopressor when fluid alone fails.

D. Within three hours, 30 mL/kg intravenous crystalloid is recommended for sepsis hypotension.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question specifies septic shock that remains unstable after fluid administration and asks for the first-line vasopressor. Miller states that norepinephrine is the recommended first-line choice when fluid alone fails, so C is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3053, printed p. 2560, passage ID 295145]

**A — true but irrelevant:** An initial MAP target of 65 mm Hg is a stated septic-shock target, but A does not identify the first-line vasopressor requested by the stem. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 36 Infection, PDF p. 951, printed p. 885, passage ID 274481]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Vasopressin or epinephrine may be added as a second agent when needed, but B describes adjunctive therapy after first-line treatment rather than the first-line drug. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 36 Infection, PDF p. 951, printed p. 885, passage ID 274481]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller explicitly states that when fluid alone cannot restore hemodynamic stability, norepinephrine is the recommended first-line vasopressor of choice. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3053, printed p. 2560, passage ID 295145]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Thirty mL/kg crystalloid within three hours is a fluid-resuscitation recommendation, but the stem says fluid alone has failed and asks for the vasopressor relationship. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 36 Infection, PDF p. 951, printed p. 885, passage ID 274481]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller evidence directly answers first-line vasopressor selection; Chestnut evidence supports why MAP, second-agent, and initial-fluid options are not the requested relationship.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The frozen item includes several true sepsis-management statements; fluid failure and first-line status distinguish C.

## S02-Q053

A patient remains hypotensive despite norepinephrine during septic shock. Which mechanism explains the action of an adjunctive vasopressin infusion?

A. The described low-dose vasopressin regimen is 0.03 U/min.

B. A randomized trial found no mortality difference when vasopressin and norepinephrine were added to open-label therapy.

C. Low-dose vasopressin can raise blood pressure in vasopressor-refractory septic shock.

D. Direct vascular smooth-muscle constriction through V1-receptor stimulation.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient is vasopressor-refractory and the question asks for the mechanism of adjunctive vasopressin. Chestnut states that vasopressin directly constricts vascular smooth muscle through V1-receptor stimulation, so D gives the mechanism rather than a dose or outcome. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1359, printed p. 1293, passage ID 276299]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The dose of low-dose vasopressin is reported as 0.03 U/min, but A is a regimen detail and does not answer the requested receptor mechanism. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1359, printed p. 1293, passage ID 276299]

**B — true but irrelevant:** The randomized trial found no mortality difference, but B is an outcome finding and does not explain how vasopressin raises vascular tone. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1359, printed p. 1293, passage ID 276299]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Low-dose vasopressin can raise pressure in refractory shock, but C states the clinical effect rather than the direct receptor-mediated mechanism asked for. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1359, printed p. 1293, passage ID 276299]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut explicitly states that vasopressin causes direct vascular smooth-muscle constriction via V1-receptor stimulation. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1359, printed p. 1293, passage ID 276299]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage contains the mechanism, dose, pressure effect, and trial result, allowing a mechanism-specific selection.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The item asks mechanism; dose, blood-pressure effect, and mortality are related but nonmechanistic findings.

## S02-Q054

An ARDS trial compares 6 mL/kg and 12 mL/kg predicted-body-weight ventilation. What comparison was reported?

A. The ARMA study compared lung-protective ventilation with 12 mL/kg predicted-body-weight ventilation.

B. Low-tidal-volume ventilation has become standard of care for ARDS.

C. The landmark study reduced mortality from 40% to 30%.

D. Lung-protective ARDS ventilation uses 6 mL/kg with plateau pressure below 30 cm H2O.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks what comparison the ARMA trial reported between 6 and 12 mL/kg predicted-body-weight ventilation. Miller states that lung-protective 6 mL/kg ventilation was compared with 12 mL/kg, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3049, printed p. 2556, passage ID 295124]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller’s description of the landmark ARMA study says lung-protective ventilation was compared with ventilation at 12 mL/kg predicted body weight. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3049, printed p. 2556, passage ID 295124]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Low-tidal-volume ventilation becoming standard care is a later practice consequence, but B does not state the ARMA comparison asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3049, printed p. 2556, passage ID 295124]

**C — true but irrelevant:** The 40% to 30% mortality reduction is a reported ARMA outcome, but C answers outcome rather than which tidal-volume comparator was used. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3049, printed p. 2556, passage ID 295124]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Six mL/kg with plateau pressure below 30 cm H2O describes the lung-protective arm, but D omits the 12 mL/kg comparator that the lead-in asks about. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3049, printed p. 2556, passage ID 295124]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage states the comparator, intervention settings, mortality result, and standard-of-care consequence.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Several options accurately quote ARMA details; the lead-in specifically asks for the comparison.

## S02-Q055

Which statement defines the effect of auto-PEEP specifically in terms of end-expiratory lung volume?

A. Management may include more expiratory time and a lower tidal volume or respiratory rate.

B. End-expiratory lung volume remains larger than it would be without auto-PEEP.

C. Dynamic hyperinflation may reduce venous return.

D. Nonzero end-expiratory flow indicates incomplete emptying.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks specifically how auto-PEEP changes end-expiratory lung volume. Miller states that end-expiratory lung volumes are larger than in the absence of auto-PEEP, so B is the direct physiologic definition. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287087]

**A — true but irrelevant:** More expiratory time and lower tidal volume or respiratory rate are management responses, but A does not define the requested volume change. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287087]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that auto-PEEP leaves end-expiratory lung volumes larger than they would be without auto-PEEP. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287087]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Dynamic hyperinflation may reduce venous return, but C states a consequence rather than the requested end-expiratory-volume relationship. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287087]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Nonzero end-expiratory flow indicates incomplete emptying, but D is a waveform clue and does not state the resulting larger end-expiratory volume. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287087]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the volume relationship and supplies the related mechanisms, consequences, and management.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in requests the volume definition rather than a management step, consequence, or diagnostic waveform clue.

## S02-Q056

What does the ratio VD/VT represent?

A. Respiratory rate divided by tidal volume.

B. Tidal volume divided by physiologic dead-space volume.

C. Physiologic dead-space volume divided by tidal volume.

D. Minute ventilation divided by tidal volume.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The ratio VD/VT asks for dead-space volume relative to tidal volume. Stoelting and Cote both identify VD/VT as the ratio of physiologic dead space to tidal volume, so C is correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1766, passage ID 314687] [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 16 Cardiac physiology and pharmacology, PDF p. 788, passage ID 186129]

**A — false:** Respiratory rate divided by tidal volume is not the VD/VT relationship; the cited texts identify VD/VT as dead-space volume divided by tidal volume. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1766, passage ID 314687]

**B — wrong timing or magnitude:** Tidal volume divided by physiologic dead-space volume reverses the requested ratio, so B does not match the VD/VT definition. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 16 Cardiac physiology and pharmacology, PDF p. 788, passage ID 186129]

**C — correct:** Correct: VD/VT is physiologic dead-space volume divided by tidal volume, as stated in the cited Stoelting and Cote passages. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1766, passage ID 314687]

**D — false:** Minute ventilation divided by tidal volume is not VD/VT; the cited definition uses physiologic dead-space volume in the numerator. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 12. Respiratory Physiology and Pathophysiology, PDF p. 326, printed p. 261, passage ID 282017]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting and Cote passages directly define the ratio; Miller’s passage distinguishes minute ventilation from the requested expression.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The principal trap is inversion of the ratio or substitution of minute ventilation for dead-space volume.

## S02-Q057

A septic patient develops dysfunction in neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic systems. Which score summarizes these domains?

A. SIRS features can occur in trauma, surgery, burns, and pancreatitis.

B. Sepsis is life-threatening organ dysfunction from dysregulated host response, often involving MODS.

C. Sepsis organ dysfunction includes hypotension, hypoxemia, altered mental status, or oliguria.

D. The SOFA score assesses neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic function.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem lists dysfunction across neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic systems and asks for the score covering those domains. Miller states that SOFA numerically assesses exactly these six functions, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3052, printed p. 2559, passage ID 295141]

**A — true but irrelevant:** SIRS features can occur in trauma, surgery, burns, and pancreatitis, but A describes nonspecific inflammatory features rather than the six-domain score asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3052, printed p. 2559, passage ID 295141]

**B — true but irrelevant:** The sepsis definition and association with MODS provide disease context, but B does not name the score that summarizes the listed organ domains. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3052, printed p. 2559, passage ID 295141]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Hypotension, hypoxemia, altered mental status, and oliguria are examples of organ dysfunction, but C lists findings rather than the six-domain scoring system. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3052, printed p. 2559, passage ID 295141]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller states that SOFA uses a 0–4 assessment of neurologic, cardiovascular, respiratory, renal, liver, and hematologic function. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 79. Critical Care Anesthesiology, PDF p. 3052, printed p. 2559, passage ID 295141]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly names all six SOFA domains and distinguishes SIRS, sepsis/MODS, and individual dysfunction findings.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The domain list is the discriminator; the distractors provide related sepsis concepts but not the requested score.

## S02-Q058

An intubated ICU patient has fluctuating inattention and disorganized thinking. Which bedside tool is designed to screen for ICU delirium?

A. CAM-ICU is a structured screening instrument for ICU delirium diagnosis.

B. Light sedation and daily awakening trials can shorten ventilation and ICU stay.

C. Reorientation, day-night maintenance, and noise reduction can reduce delirium and confusion.

D. ICU delirium is distinguished by acute onset, fluctuation, inattention, disorganized thinking, and altered consciousness.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The intubated ICU patient has fluctuating inattention and disorganized thinking, and the lead-in asks for a bedside delirium-screening tool. Barash identifies CAM-ICU as a structured screening instrument for ICU delirium, so A is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4859, passage ID 251801]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash explicitly identifies the Confusion Assessment Method for the ICU (CAM-ICU) as a structured screening instrument available for ICU delirium diagnosis. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4859, passage ID 251801]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Light sedation and daily awakening trials can improve ICU outcomes, but B is a management practice and not the bedside delirium-screening tool requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4859, passage ID 251801]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Reorientation, day-night maintenance, and noise reduction can reduce delirium and confusion, but C describes prevention/supportive care rather than a screening instrument. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4859, passage ID 251801]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Acute onset, fluctuation, inattention, disorganized thinking, and altered consciousness are delirium characteristics, but D describes diagnostic features and not the named tool. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4859, passage ID 251801]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly identifies CAM-ICU and distinguishes screening from diagnostic features and management practices.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The item asks for an instrument, not a delirium feature or prevention strategy.

## S02-Q068

Under ASA Standard I, what personnel requirement applies during general, regional, and monitored anesthesia care?

A. Modify anesthesia based on clinical observations and patient responses to surgery or drugs.

B. Qualified personnel must be continuously present.

C. Emphasize frequent measurements and clinical judgment.

D. Allow extenuating circumstances to influence monitoring applicability or accuracy.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** ASA Standard I concerns who must be present during general, regional, and monitored anesthesia care. Barash states that qualified personnel must be present in the operating room to monitor continuously, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2094, passage ID 246835]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Modifying anesthesia based on observations and patient responses is a monitoring purpose, but A does not state Standard I’s personnel-presence requirement. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2094, passage ID 246835]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash states that qualified personnel must be present and monitor the patient continuously during general, regional, and monitored anesthesia care. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2094, passage ID 246835]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Frequent measurements and clinical judgment are emphasized in current standards, but C concerns monitoring practice rather than the required qualified-personnel presence. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2094, passage ID 246835]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Extenuating circumstances can affect monitoring applicability or accuracy, but D does not answer who must be continuously present under Standard I. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2094, passage ID 246835]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly states Standard I’s personnel requirement and identifies the other monitoring principles as separate provisions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for personnel, while several distractors describe monitoring purposes or limitations.

## S02-Q069

A pulse-oximeter site loses a reliable pulsatile signal during peripheral vasoconstriction. Which response directly addresses that problem?

A. Evaluate for pediatric endobronchial intubation despite no tube-position concern.

B. Rely on supplemental oxygen to make desaturation appear sooner during apnea.

C. Assess another vascular bed for satisfactory pulsatile perfusion.

D. Use pulse oximetry to confirm that ventilation is adequate.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Peripheral vasoconstriction reduces pulsatile perfusion at the current oximeter site. Barash states that another vascular bed may need to be tried until satisfactory pulsatile perfusion is found, so C directly addresses the problem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2103, passage ID 246851]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Pediatric endobronchial intubation is a different pulse-oximetry application, but A does not address loss of a pulsatile peripheral signal. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2103, passage ID 246851]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Supplemental oxygen can delay visible desaturation during apnea, but B does not restore the pulsatile signal needed for oximeter operation. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2103, passage ID 246851]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash recommends trying several vascular beds when necessary until a site with satisfactory pulsatile perfusion is found. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2103, passage ID 246851]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Pulse oximetry is a poor indicator of ventilation, but D states a limitation of interpretation and not the site-selection response to absent pulsatility. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2103, passage ID 246851]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly links pulse-oximeter reliability to pulsatility and recommends another vascular bed when perfusion is poor.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The problem is signal acquisition from poor pulsatile perfusion, not interpretation of oxygenation or ventilation.

## S02-Q070

Why can oscillometric cuff results differ between devices even when cuffs are used across age groups?

A. The cuff inflates above systolic pressure and gradually deflates while oscillations are measured.

B. Oscillometry can satisfy evaluation of arterial pressure and heart rate at least every five minutes.

C. Automated oscillometry is usually accurate and versatile in anesthetized patients.
