D. Proprietary formulas differ between devices.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks why oscillometric results can differ between devices despite use across age groups. Barash explains that monitors refine estimates using proprietary empirical formulas, so D identifies the source of inter-device variation. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2122, passage ID 246882]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Inflating above systolic pressure and gradually deflating while measuring oscillations describes how the cuff operates, but it does not explain why devices disagree. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2122, passage ID 246882]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Five-minute arterial-pressure and heart-rate evaluation is a monitoring standard, but B does not explain inter-device formula differences. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2122, passage ID 246882]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Automated oscillometry is usually accurate and versatile in anesthetized patients, but C describes general performance and not the reason results vary by device. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2122, passage ID 246882]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash states that proprietary empirical formulas are used to refine oscillometric estimates, causing results not to be consistent from device to device. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2122, passage ID 246882]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly addresses cuff sizing, measurement mechanics, standards, accuracy, and proprietary formulas.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks about device-to-device variation, not the general method or monitoring interval.

## S02-Q071

Which equation correctly defines Fick cardiac output when oxygen consumption and the arteriovenous oxygen difference are known?

A. Fick cardiac output equals oxygen consumption divided by arteriovenous oxygen difference.

B. The Fick principle was first described by Adolf Fick in 1870.

C. Estimate cardiac output using impedance cardiography and pulse-contour analysis.

D. Measure oxygen consumption by collecting exhaled gases and measuring their volume and oxygen concentration.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The Fick principle determines cardiac output from oxygen consumption divided by the arteriovenous oxygen difference. Stoelting states that exact relationship, so A is the correct equation. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 313836]

**A — correct:** Correct: Stoelting states that cardiac output is estimated by dividing oxygen consumption by the arteriovenous oxygen difference. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 313836]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Fick’s historical attribution to Adolf Fick in 1870 is true, but B does not give the equation requested by the lead-in. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 313836]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Impedance cardiography and pulse-contour analysis are alternative cardiac-output methods, but C does not state the Fick equation asked for. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 313836]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Measuring exhaled gases can determine oxygen consumption, but D describes one input measurement rather than the requested cardiac-output equation. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 313836]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly gives the Fick equation and related historical, alternative-method, and measurement details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The item asks for the equation, not its history, alternative techniques, or measurement of one component.

## S02-Q072

An arterial waveform’s fast-flush response is being assessed after transducer setup. What does the test evaluate?

A. Damping coefficient is determined from the heights of adjacent oscillation peaks.

B. A fast-flush test evaluates natural frequency and damping coefficient of an arterial-pressure monitoring system.

C. Natural frequency is determined from the period of an oscillation cycle.

D. Air bubbles degrade dynamic response and produce spurious pressure readings.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** A fast-flush test is used to characterize the dynamic response of an arterial-pressure monitoring system. Miller identifies natural frequency and damping coefficient as the measured properties, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 32. Cardiovascular Monitoring, PDF p. 1206, printed p. 1010, passage ID 286182]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Damping coefficient is determined from adjacent peak heights, but A gives one calculation rather than what the fast-flush test evaluates overall. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 32. Cardiovascular Monitoring, PDF p. 1206, printed p. 1010, passage ID 286182]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller describes clinical measurement of both natural frequency and damping coefficient using square-wave fast-flush artifacts. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 32. Cardiovascular Monitoring, PDF p. 1206, printed p. 1010, passage ID 286182]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Natural frequency can be determined from one oscillation period, but C identifies a component calculation and not the complete fast-flush test evaluation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 32. Cardiovascular Monitoring, PDF p. 1206, printed p. 1010, passage ID 286182]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Air bubbles can alter monitoring behavior, but D gives a system artifact rather than the natural-frequency/damping evaluation performed by fast flush. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 32. Cardiovascular Monitoring, PDF p. 1206, printed p. 1010, passage ID 286182]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly identifies both dynamic-response properties and the related individual measurements and artifact.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks what the test evaluates as a system, not how one property is calculated.

## S02-Q073

The monitor shows a CVP waveform synchronized with the ECG. Which event corresponds to the v wave?

A. Atrial fibrillation causes an absent a wave.

B. The x descent represents right-atrial relaxation.

C. The v wave represents rapid right-atrial filling.

D. The y descent represents early ventricular filling after tricuspid-valve opening.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks which CVP waveform event is the v wave. Stoelting identifies the v wave as rapid right-atrial filling, so C is correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1179, passage ID 313791]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Atrial fibrillation can produce an absent a wave, but A concerns the a wave and does not identify the v-wave event asked for. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1179, passage ID 313791]

**B — true but irrelevant:** The x descent represents right-atrial relaxation, but B describes a descent rather than the v wave requested. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1179, passage ID 313791]

**C — correct:** Correct: Stoelting states that the v wave represents rapid filling of the right atrium. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1179, passage ID 313791]

**D — true but irrelevant:** The y descent represents early ventricular filling after tricuspid opening, but D is a y-descent event and not the v-wave event asked for. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 14. Circulatory Physiology, PDF p. 1179, passage ID 313791]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting waveform passage directly maps a wave, x descent, v wave, and y descent.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The distractors are true descriptions of other CVP components; the waveform label v is decisive.

## S02-Q074

At the beginning of expiration, which normal capnogram phase is sampled first?

A. Phase C-D is the alveolar expiratory plateau and is normally almost horizontal.

B. A sharp upstroke occurs when CO2-containing gas reaches the sampling site.

C. During inspiration, the capnogram has a steep downstroke back to baseline.

D. The first capnogram phase samples anatomic dead space and is normally devoid of CO2.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** At the beginning of expiration, the first capnogram phase samples gas from anatomic dead space before alveolar CO2 reaches the sampling site. Barash states that this gas is normally CO2-free, so D is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2108, passage ID 246858]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The C-D phase is the nearly horizontal alveolar plateau, but A describes a later phase rather than the first expiratory sample. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2108, passage ID 246858]

**B — true but irrelevant:** The sharp upstroke occurs when CO2-containing gas reaches the sampling site, but B describes phase B-C after the initial dead-space phase. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2108, passage ID 246858]

**C — true but irrelevant:** The steep downstroke occurs during inspiration, but C is an inspiratory event and not the first expiratory phase asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2108, passage ID 246858]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash states that phase A-B is the initial expiratory stage, samples anatomic dead-space gas, and is normally devoid of CO2. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2108, passage ID 246858]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash capnogram passage directly maps all phases and identifies the first expiratory sample.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The timing phrase ‘beginning of expiration’ distinguishes phase A-B from the later upstroke, plateau, and inspiratory downstroke.

## S02-Q075

Immediately after induction, ETCO2 falls near zero and the waveform disappears. Which option gives the appropriately broad differential diagnosis?

A. Tube malposition, severe hypotension, pulmonary embolism, cardiac arrest, or circuit disconnection.

B. Tube malposition alone.

C. Circuit disconnection alone.

D. Cardiac arrest alone.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** A near-zero ETCO2 with an absent waveform immediately after induction can reflect several airway, circulatory, thromboembolic, arrest, and equipment causes. Barash lists these collectively, so A is the appropriately broad differential. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2109, passage ID 246860]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash lists tube malposition, sudden severe hypotension, massive pulmonary embolism, cardiac arrest, and disconnection or sampling-line disruption as causes. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2109, passage ID 246860]

**B — incomplete:** Tube malposition is one possibility, but B is incomplete because the cited differential also includes circulatory, thromboembolic, arrest, and equipment causes. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2109, passage ID 246860]

**C — incomplete:** Circuit disconnection is one possibility, but C omits the airway, hypotension, pulmonary-embolism, and cardiac-arrest causes listed by Barash. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2109, passage ID 246860]

**D — incomplete:** Cardiac arrest is one possibility, but D is incomplete and does not include the other urgent physiologic and mechanical causes in the textbook differential. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 26. Commonly Used Monitoring Techniques, PDF p. 2109, passage ID 246860]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage gives the broad differential and explicitly identifies each narrower option as only one possible cause.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The safety point is to avoid anchoring on a single cause when an absent ETCO2 waveform has multiple life-threatening explanations.

## S02-Q076

A deeply blocked patient has no TOF responses but is being assessed with posttetanic stimulation. What pattern indicates complete peripheral-muscle block?

A. During moderate block, the first TOF response appears and posttetanic facilitation increases.

B. In complete peripheral-muscle block, no response occurs to TOF, tetanic, or posttetanic stimulation.

C. During deep block, TOF responses are absent but posttetanic facilitation is present.

D. A posttetanic count of 1 corresponds to very deep block.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient has no TOF responses and is being assessed for complete peripheral block. Miller states that complete peripheral-muscle block produces no response to TOF, tetanic, or posttetanic stimulation, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1461, printed p. 1216, passage ID 287363]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A first TOF response with increasing posttetanic facilitation describes moderate block, but A does not describe complete peripheral-muscle block. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1461, printed p. 1216, passage ID 287363]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that complete peripheral-muscle block produces no response to TOF, tetanic, or posttetanic stimulation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1461, printed p. 1216, passage ID 287363]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Absent TOF with posttetanic facilitation describes less pronounced deep block, but C is not the complete-block pattern asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1461, printed p. 1216, passage ID 287363]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A posttetanic count of 1 corresponds to very deep block, but D is a grading detail and does not define complete block with no response to any stimulation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1461, printed p. 1216, passage ID 287363]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage distinguishes complete, deep, very deep, and moderate block patterns.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Posttetanic facilitation differentiates deep from complete block; complete block has no response to any stimulation.

## S02-Q086

During controlled ventilation, PaCO2 is increased while cerebral perfusion pressure is held constant. Which cerebral-flow response is supported?

A. The PaO2 response is a threshold phenomenon rather than continuous.

B. A maintained PaCO2 permits CBF to increase when PaO2 falls sufficiently.

C. Cerebral blood flow rises continuously as PaCO2 increases.

D. Cerebral blood flow responds to lactic-acid hydrogen ions by increasing.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks how cerebral blood flow changes as PaCO2 rises. Stoelting states that cerebral blood flow increases in response to increased PaCO2, with a continuous rise, so C is correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 3. Neurophysiology, PDF p. 291, passage ID 312541]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The PaO2 response is a threshold phenomenon, but A concerns oxygen response rather than the PaCO2-driven cerebral-flow response asked for. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 3. Neurophysiology, PDF p. 291, passage ID 312541]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Maintained PaCO2 permits CBF to increase when PaO2 falls sufficiently, but B describes an oxygen-threshold interaction and not the direct PaCO2 relationship in the stem. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 3. Neurophysiology, PDF p. 291, passage ID 312541]

**C — correct:** Correct: Stoelting identifies a continuous increase in cerebral blood flow in response to increasing PaCO2. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 3. Neurophysiology, PDF p. 291, passage ID 312541]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Lactic-acid hydrogen ions can also increase CBF, but D gives an alternate acid stimulus rather than the PaCO2 response being tested. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 3. Neurophysiology, PDF p. 291, passage ID 312541]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage distinguishes PaCO2, PaO2, and other-hydrogen-ion effects on cerebral blood flow.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The manipulated variable is PaCO2; oxygen-threshold and lactic-acid statements are related but not the requested response.

## S02-Q087

After severe head injury, cerebral autoregulation is absent. Which quantitative relationship between cerebral blood flow and cerebral perfusion pressure is expected?

A. CBF is fixed at approximately 50 mL/100 g/min.

B. CBF remains constant as CPP changes.

C. CBF is determined only by whether normotension is present.

D. CBF varies proportionally with CPP.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** With absent autoregulation, cerebral blood flow becomes pressure-passive. Miller states that CBF varies in proportion to CPP in this condition, so D is the quantitative relationship requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290257]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Approximately 50 mL/100 g/min is a normal-flow reference value, but A does not describe how CBF changes with CPP when autoregulation is absent. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290257]

**B — false:** Constant CBF would require preserved autoregulation; Miller describes the absent-autoregulation curve as pressure-passive, so B does not fit the stated condition. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290257]

**C — incomplete:** Normotension alone does not define the pressure-passive relationship; Miller notes subnormal CBF can occur during normotension after head injury, so C is incomplete. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290257]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller states that absent autoregulation creates a pressure-passive condition in which CBF varies in proportion to CPP. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290257]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the proportional CBF/CPP relationship and provides normal-flow and normotension contrasts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Absent autoregulation changes the question from a plateau relationship to pressure-passive proportionality.

## S02-Q088

An intracranial mass expands after cerebrospinal-fluid compensation is exhausted. Which volume-conservation principle explains why ICP now rises?

A. An increase in one intracranial-volume component must be offset by a decrease in another.

B. The relevant principle is that normal ICP is 5 to 15 mm Hg.

C. Normal compensation remains unlimited despite continued mass expansion.

D. Every small intracranial-volume increase causes the same large ICP rise even before compensation is exhausted.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Once cerebrospinal-fluid compensation is exhausted, the governing Monro-Kellie volume principle is that an increase in one intracranial component must be offset by a decrease in another to prevent an ICP rise. A states that principle directly. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 286, printed p. 275, passage ID 233461]

**A — correct:** Correct: Stoelting states that any increase in one intracranial-volume component must be offset by a decrease in another to prevent increased ICP. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 286, printed p. 275, passage ID 233461]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Normal ICP of 5–15 mm Hg is a reference range, but B does not explain why ICP rises after compensatory volume reserve is exhausted. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 286, printed p. 275, passage ID 233461]

**C — false:** Compensation is not unlimited: Stoelting states that eventually even a small volume change can cause a large ICP change, so C contradicts the described exhausted reserve. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 286, printed p. 275, passage ID 233461]

**D — false:** The large ICP response occurs after compensatory reserve is exhausted, not with every small increase before exhaustion; D does not fit the volume-compensation curve. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 286, printed p. 275, passage ID 233461]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage states the volume-conservation principle, normal compensation, normal ICP, and later steep pressure response.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem deliberately places the patient beyond the compensatory portion of the intracranial-volume curve.

## S02-Q089

During prolonged controlled hyperventilation for craniotomy, which time-dependent cerebral response should be anticipated?

A. PaCO2 should increase after retractors are removed when dural closure permits.

B. During prolonged hyperventilation, brain pH and CBF return toward normal over 8 to 12 hours despite persistent arterial hypocapnia.

C. CPP should be maintained normal or high-normal during most intracranial procedures.

D. Hypocapnia may be used as an adjunct to brain relaxation during craniotomy.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the response after prolonged controlled hyperventilation. Miller states that brain pH and CBF return toward normal over 8–12 hours even while arterial hypocapnia persists, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290255]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Allowing PaCO2 to increase after retractors are removed is a later management step, but A does not state the time-dependent adaptation during prolonged hyperventilation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290255]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that brain pH and CBF return toward normal over 8–12 hours despite persistent decreased arterial PaCO2 and systemic alkalosis. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290255]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Maintaining normal or high-normal CPP is a procedural goal, but C does not answer the time-dependent response to prolonged hyperventilation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290255]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Hypocapnia may be used as an adjunct to brain relaxation, but D describes an indication and not what happens after prolonged exposure. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2077, printed p. 1746, passage ID 290255]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the 8–12-hour cerebral adaptation and the related hyperventilation management points.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The discriminator is time course: cerebral pH and CBF normalize toward baseline while arterial hypocapnia persists.

## S02-Q090

During sitting craniotomy, which paired precordial-Doppler and capnography findings support venous air embolism?

A. Absence of air on echocardiography with a precipitous ETCO2 decrease.

B. A continuous windmill Doppler murmur with a sudden decrease in exhaled nitrogen.

C. A continuous windmill Doppler murmur with a precipitous decrease in ETCO2.

D. A continuous windmill Doppler murmur with a precipitous increase in ETCO2.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the paired precordial-Doppler and capnography findings of venous air embolism. Cote directly lists a continuous windmill murmur with a precipitous decrease in ETCO2, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**A — false:** Venous air embolism is associated with detecting air rather than its absence, and the key Doppler finding is a windmill murmur; A does not match both findings. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**B — wrong timing or magnitude:** A windmill murmur is appropriate, but B reverses the capnography direction: Cote reports a precipitous decrease in ETCO2, not a nitrogen finding as the paired second result. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote pairs precordial Doppler’s continuous windmill murmur with a precipitous decrease in end-tidal carbon dioxide during venous air embolism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**D — wrong timing or magnitude:** The Doppler murmur is appropriate, but D reverses the reported ETCO2 direction; the cited passage states a precipitous decrease, not increase. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 33 Plastic and reconstructive surgery, PDF p. 1457, passage ID 189360]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly pairs the Doppler and ETCO2 findings and distinguishes nitrogen monitoring and echocardiography.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Both components and the direction of ETCO2 change are required; the distractors invert or substitute one component.

## S02-Q096

In a term pregnant patient, which combined physiologic change most increases risk of rapid desaturation during apnea?

A. A 25-degree back-table elevation can make laryngoscopy easier and aid preoxygenation.

B. FRC is the primary oxygen reservoir during apnea.

C. Left uterine displacement minimizes aortocaval compression during induction.

D. Decreased FRC and increased oxygen requirement result in rapid desaturation during apnea.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Pregnancy decreases FRC while increasing oxygen requirement, reducing reserve and causing rapid desaturation during apnea. Chestnut states this combined relationship directly, so D is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 769, printed p. 702, passage ID 273646]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Back-table elevation can aid laryngoscopy and preoxygenation, but A does not state the paired physiologic cause of rapid desaturation. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 769, printed p. 702, passage ID 273646]

**B — true but irrelevant:** FRC is the primary oxygen reservoir during apnea, but B does not state why pregnancy depletes that reserve rapidly. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 769, printed p. 702, passage ID 273646]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Left uterine displacement minimizes aortocaval compression, but C addresses circulation and not rapid apnea-related desaturation. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 769, printed p. 702, passage ID 273646]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut explicitly states that decreased FRC and increased oxygen requirement in pregnancy result in rapid oxygen desaturation during apnea. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 769, printed p. 702, passage ID 273646]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly states the paired pregnancy physiology and related airway/obstetric facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The keyed answer requires both decreased FRC and increased oxygen consumption/requirement.

## S02-Q097

During maternal hypotension, which uteroplacental-flow property makes fetal perfusion pressure-dependent?

A. Uterine and placental blood flow have minimal autoregulation and depend on cardiac output and uterine perfusion pressure.

B. Term uterine blood flow is about 700 mL/min, approximately 10% of cardiac output.

C. Maternal hypotension from hypovolemia, aortocaval compression, or anesthetic resistance reduction decreases perfusion pressure.

D. Extreme hypocapnia below PaCO2 20 mmHg can reduce uterine blood flow.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Uterine and placental blood flow have minimal autoregulation and depend on cardiac output and uterine perfusion pressure. Stoelting states that relationship directly, so A explains why maternal hypotension threatens fetal perfusion. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 45. Maternal and Fetal Physiology and Pharmacology, PDF p. 2704, passage ID 316128]

**A — correct:** Correct: Stoelting states that uterine and placental flow depend on maternal cardiac output and are directly related to uterine perfusion pressure. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 45. Maternal and Fetal Physiology and Pharmacology, PDF p. 2704, passage ID 316128]

**B — true but irrelevant:** About 700 mL/min or 10% of cardiac output is a term-flow magnitude, but B does not explain the pressure-dependent property asked for. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 45. Maternal and Fetal Physiology and Pharmacology, PDF p. 2704, passage ID 316128]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Hypovolemia, aortocaval compression, and reduced resistance can lower perfusion pressure, but C lists causes rather than the flow property requested. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 45. Maternal and Fetal Physiology and Pharmacology, PDF p. 2704, passage ID 316128]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Extreme hypocapnia can reduce uterine flow, but D is a specific CO2 effect and not the baseline pressure-dependence explanation. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 45. Maternal and Fetal Physiology and Pharmacology, PDF p. 2704, passage ID 316128]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly states the flow-pressure relationship and distinguishes magnitude, causes, and hypocapnia.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks for a governing vascular property rather than a cause or measurement.

## S02-Q098

Which paired change in alveolar ventilation and PaCO2 is expected during normal pregnancy?

A. Only pH and bicarbonate change; alveolar ventilation and PaCO2 do not.

B. Alveolar ventilation increases by at least 30%, lowering PaCO2 to approximately 28–32 mm Hg.

C. The principal change is a 20% increase in functional residual capacity.

D. Alveolar ventilation falls because obesity or supine positioning increases functional residual capacity.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Normal pregnancy increases alveolar ventilation by at least 30% and produces chronic respiratory alkalosis with PaCO2 approximately 28–32 mm Hg. Chestnut states both changes together, so B is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 394, printed p. 369, passage ID 271858]

**A — false:** Pregnancy does change alveolar ventilation and PaCO2; Chestnut documents the ventilation increase and respiratory alkalosis, so A does not fit the documented physiology. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 394, printed p. 369, passage ID 271858]

**B — correct:** Correct: Chestnut states that alveolar ventilation rises by 30% or more and PaCO2 falls to 28–32 mm Hg by mid-pregnancy. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 394, printed p. 369, passage ID 271858]

**C — false:** FRC diminishes by approximately 20% as the uterus expands, rather than increasing; C reverses the documented change. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 394, printed p. 369, passage ID 271858]

**D — false:** Obesity or supine positioning can further decrease FRC, but D incorrectly says alveolar ventilation falls because FRC increases and does not describe normal pregnancy physiology. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 394, printed p. 369, passage ID 271858]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly supports the ventilation/PaCO2 pair and related pH/FRC changes.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The keyed option combines the magnitude of ventilation increase with the resulting PaCO2 range.

## S02-Q099

Which combined spinal–epidural feature best explains its role in labor analgesia?

A. Intrathecal fentanyl or sufentanil with bupivacaine provides 90–120 minutes of analgesia with minimal motor block.

B. Continuous epidural analgesia may maintain analgesia after spinal injection.

C. Combined spinal–epidural analgesia combines rapid spinal onset with epidural flexibility and longer duration.

D. Adding local anesthetic is necessary for satisfactory analgesia during active labor.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Combined spinal–epidural analgesia is defined by integrating rapid spinal onset with the flexibility and longer duration of a continuous epidural. Barash states this directly, so C is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3491, passage ID 249250]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Intrathecal opioid with bupivacaine can provide 90–120 minutes with minimal motor block, but A describes the spinal component and not the defining combined-technique role. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3491, passage ID 249250]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Continuous epidural analgesia may maintain analgesia after spinal injection, but B provides only maintenance detail and not the complete combined advantage. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3491, passage ID 249250]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash states that CSE combines rapid, reliable spinal onset with the flexibility and longer duration associated with continuous epidural technique. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3491, passage ID 249250]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Local anesthetic may be necessary during active labor, but D concerns drug composition rather than the combined onset and duration rationale. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3491, passage ID 249250]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly states the CSE rationale and related component details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for integration of spinal onset and epidural flexibility/duration.

## S02-Q100

A hemodynamically stable woman with combined spinal–epidural labor analgesia asks whether ambulation improves labor or neonatal outcomes compared with bed rest. Which trial summary is most accurate?

A. Ambulation shortened labor and reduced oxytocin augmentation.

B. The comparative outcome is determined only by indirect maternal effects on the fetus.

C. The comparative outcome is determined only by direct fetal drug transfer.

D. Ambulation showed no advantage in labor progress or outcome and no differences in oxytocin use, analgesia satisfaction, or Apgar scores.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Randomized trials comparing ambulation with bed rest during first-stage labor with neuraxial analgesia found no advantage in labor progress or outcome and no differences in oxytocin use, satisfaction, or Apgar scores. Chestnut reports that complete summary, so D is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 23 Epidural and Spinal Analgesia: Anesthesia for Labor and Vaginal Delivery, PDF p. 544, printed p. 520, passage ID 272579]

**A — false:** The trials did not demonstrate shorter labor or reduced oxytocin augmentation with ambulation, so A reverses the reported finding. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 23 Epidural and Spinal Analgesia: Anesthesia for Labor and Vaginal Delivery, PDF p. 544, printed p. 520, passage ID 272579]

**B — false:** Maternal effects can affect the fetus indirectly, but B’s word “only” excludes direct transplacental effects and is therefore an overrestriction. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 23 Epidural and Spinal Analgesia: Anesthesia for Labor and Vaginal Delivery, PDF p. 544, printed p. 520, passage ID 272579]

**C — false:** Transplacental drug transfer can affect the fetus directly, but C’s word “only” excludes indirect maternal effects and is therefore an overrestriction. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 23 Epidural and Spinal Analgesia: Anesthesia for Labor and Vaginal Delivery, PDF p. 544, printed p. 520, passage ID 272579]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut reports no ambulation advantage in labor progress or outcome and no differences in oxytocin augmentation, analgesia satisfaction, or Apgar scores. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 23 Epidural and Spinal Analgesia: Anesthesia for Labor and Vaginal Delivery, PDF p. 544, printed p. 520, passage ID 272579]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly reports the trial comparison and all listed outcomes.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The keyed answer is a multi-outcome null finding, not a mechanism claim.

## S02-Q101

A healthy laboring patient requests epidural analgesia early in labor. Which timing principle should guide initiation?

A. Maternal request is sufficient justification to initiate labor epidural analgesia; timing should not depend on arbitrary cervical dilation.

B. Lumbar epidural analgesia is a safe and effective mainstay of labor pain relief.

C. The epidural block can be made denser and prolonged if operative delivery is required.

D. Preventing accidental intravascular or intrathecal local-anesthetic administration is paramount for epidural safety.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks when early labor epidural analgesia may be initiated. Miller states that maternal request is sufficient justification and timing need not depend on arbitrary cervical dilation, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291169]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller states that maternal request alone justifies labor epidural initiation and timing should not depend on arbitrary cervical dilation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291169]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Epidural analgesia is a safe and effective mainstay, but B describes general value rather than the timing principle asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291169]

**C — true but irrelevant:** The block can be made denser for operative delivery, but C describes versatility and does not answer when to initiate analgesia. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291169]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Preventing intravascular or intrathecal administration is a safety priority, but D does not address the requested initiation timing. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291169]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the maternal-request timing principle and the other epidural features.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question tests timing justification, not safety, versatility, or general efficacy.

## S02-Q102

For cesarean delivery, which neuraxial feature most directly favors spinal over epidural anesthesia when rapid dense surgical anesthesia is needed?

A. Spinal anesthesia is usually administered as a single-injection, single-shot procedure.

B. Spinal anesthesia provides rapid, dense, generally more profound neuroblockade than epidural anesthesia for cesarean delivery.

C. Only a small amount of local anesthetic is needed to establish functional spinal blockade.

D. Spinal anesthesia permits visual confirmation of needle placement through cerebrospinal-fluid visualization and is technically easier than epidural anesthesia.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Rapid dense surgical anesthesia is the relevant discriminator for cesarean delivery. Chestnut states that spinal anesthesia has rapid onset and generally more profound neuroblockade than epidural anesthesia, so B is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 591, printed p. 582, passage ID 272795]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Single-shot administration describes how spinal is usually delivered, but A does not give the rapid dense advantage sought by the lead-in. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 591, printed p. 582, passage ID 272795]

**B — correct:** Correct: Chestnut directly states that spinal anesthesia provides rapid, dense, typically more profound neuroblockade than epidural anesthesia for cesarean delivery. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 591, printed p. 582, passage ID 272795]

**C — true but irrelevant:** A small local-anesthetic amount establishes spinal blockade, but C is a dose feature and not the rapid dense neuroblockade comparison. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 591, printed p. 582, passage ID 272795]

**D — true but irrelevant:** CSF visualization and technical simplicity are spinal advantages, but D does not state the profound rapid block comparison requested. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 591, printed p. 582, passage ID 272795]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly compares spinal and epidural onset and depth and supplies related spinal features.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem specifies rapid dense surgical anesthesia, selecting the comparative block-depth statement.

## S02-Q103

According to Miller’s Anesthesia, 10th edition, which combined strategy is recommended to prevent spinal hypotension during cesarean delivery?

A. Use phenylephrine as the vasopressor of choice for treatment, with prophylactic infusion increasingly supported.

B. Account for phenylephrine's potential reflex maternal slowing of the heart rate and decreased cardiac output.

C. Use prophylactic phenylephrine infusion with crystalloid co-loading and left lateral uterine displacement.

D. Use fluid co-loading alone, which has limited efficacy in consistently preventing postspinal hypotension.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The prevention strategy must combine vasopressor prophylaxis, fluid co-loading, and uterine displacement. Miller’s consensus summary recommends exactly that combination, so C is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2261, printed p. 1901, passage ID 291187]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Phenylephrine is the vasopressor of choice and prophylactic infusion is supported, but A omits co-loading and uterine displacement required by the combined strategy. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2261, printed p. 1901, passage ID 291187]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Reflex slowing and decreased cardiac output are phenylephrine considerations, but B is an adverse-effect detail rather than the recommended prevention bundle. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2261, printed p. 1901, passage ID 291188]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller reports prophylactic phenylephrine infusion combined with crystalloid co-loading and left lateral uterine displacement. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2261, printed p. 1901, passage ID 291187]

**D — incomplete:** Fluid co-loading alone has limited efficacy and is typically combined with a vasopressor, so D omits the key prophylactic components. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2261, printed p. 1901, passage ID 291187]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller passages directly support the combined prophylaxis and explain why fluid alone or isolated drug details are incomplete.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The item asks for a combined prevention bundle, not a single agent or adverse effect.

## S02-Q104

A pregnant patient requires general anesthesia for cesarean delivery. Which interpretation best distinguishes volatile-anesthetic MAC from the dose needed to produce hypnosis?

A. Interpret the pregnancy-associated MAC decrease as a decreased inhaled-anesthetic requirement.

B. One study found no difference in bispectral-index values between pregnant cesarean and nonpregnant gynecologic groups.

C. Pregnancy lowers volatile-anesthetic MAC by up to 40%.

D. Although pregnancy may lower MAC, it does not necessarily lower the volatile-anesthetic dose required for hypnosis.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question distinguishes MAC from actual hypnosis dosing. Chestnut reports that pregnancy may lower MAC without decreasing volatile-anesthetic requirements, so D is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 2 Physiologic Changes of Pregnancy, PDF p. 19, printed p. 28, passage ID 270165]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Clinicians have interpreted lower MAC as lower inhaled-anesthetic requirement, but A reports an interpretation that the cited study did not support. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 2 Physiologic Changes of Pregnancy, PDF p. 19, printed p. 28, passage ID 270165]

**B — true but irrelevant:** No BIS difference was found between groups, but B is a study observation and does not state the MAC-versus-hypnosis dose distinction. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 2 Physiologic Changes of Pregnancy, PDF p. 19, printed p. 28, passage ID 270165]

**C — true but irrelevant:** MAC may be up to 40% lower in pregnancy, but C gives the MAC change alone and does not answer whether hypnosis dose is reduced. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 2 Physiologic Changes of Pregnancy, PDF p. 19, printed p. 28, passage ID 270165]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut states that although pregnancy may decrease MAC, it does not decrease volatile-anesthetic requirements for hypnosis. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 2 Physiologic Changes of Pregnancy, PDF p. 19, printed p. 28, passage ID 270165]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly distinguishes the MAC decrease from volatile-anesthetic hypnosis requirement.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key is the distinction between MAC as a measured endpoint and dose required for hypnosis.

## S02-Q114

During prolonged prone orthopedic surgery, which positioning practice best protects the patient?

A. Repeatedly verify that the eyes are free of pressure, the airway is secure, and head weight rests only on bony facial prominences.

B. Support the forehead and malar regions with a horseshoe headrest while maintaining reasonable airway access.

C. Fully lock all articulating head-support brackets to prevent complications if the head drops.

D. Use pin fixation to avoid direct pressure on the face.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Prolonged prone surgery requires repeated verification of eye pressure, airway security, and support on bony facial prominences. Miller states that complete positioning must be frequently checked, so A is the best protective practice. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1121, printed p. 939, passage ID 285762]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller explicitly recommends frequent checks that the eyes are pressure-free, the airway is secure, and head weight rests only on bony facial prominences. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1121, printed p. 939, passage ID 285762]

**B — true but irrelevant:** A horseshoe headrest can support the forehead and malar regions with airway access, but B is one support device and omits the repeated safety verification asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1121, printed p. 939, passage ID 285762]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Locking articulating supports prevents a dropped head, but C addresses hardware security and not the full eye/airway/facial-support check. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1121, printed p. 939, passage ID 285762]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Pin fixation avoids direct facial pressure, but D is a different fixation method and does not state the requested practice for this prone orthopedic case. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1121, printed p. 939, passage ID 285762]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller positioning passage directly supports A and distinguishes headrest, bracket, and pin alternatives.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key is a repeated integrated check rather than a single head-support device.

## S02-Q115

During limb surgery under a regional anesthetic that is otherwise adequate, when can tourniquet pain emerge?

A. A thigh tourniquet is routinely inflated during total knee arthroplasty to reduce blood loss and provide a bloodless field.

B. Tourniquet pain may occur after 60 minutes despite an adequate regional anesthetic.

C. Nerve injury after tourniquet inflation is attributed to ischemia combined with mechanical trauma.

D. Deflate the tourniquet for 30 minutes of reperfusion when prolonged inflation is required.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks when tourniquet pain can occur despite an otherwise adequate regional anesthetic. Miller states that it may emerge after 60 minutes, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 60. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 2353, printed p. 1976, passage ID 291649]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A thigh tourniquet is routinely used for TKA to reduce blood loss and provide a bloodless field, but A does not answer the timing of tourniquet pain. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 60. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 2353, printed p. 1976, passage ID 291649]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that tourniquet pain may occur after 60 minutes despite a regional anesthetic adequate for the operation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 60. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 2353, printed p. 1976, passage ID 291649]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Ischemia combined with mechanical trauma is attributed to nerve injury, but C addresses a different complication from the pain timing asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 60. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 2353, printed p. 1976, passage ID 291649]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Thirty minutes of reperfusion may reduce neural ischemia during prolonged inflation, but D is a management strategy and not when tourniquet pain emerges. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 60. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 2353, printed p. 1976, passage ID 291649]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states pain timing and distinguishes tourniquet use, nerve injury, and reperfusion management.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem specifies regional anesthesia adequacy, making the 60-minute pain timing the discriminator.

## S02-Q116

When setting a pneumatic tourniquet for extremity surgery, which pressure principle best limits ischemic and pressure-related injury while maintaining the surgical field?

A. Most clinicians limit tourniquet inflation to a maximum of 1.5–2 hours.

B. In children, tourniquet pressure may be set 50 mm Hg above Doppler-measured arterial occlusion pressure.

C. The minimum pressure that maintains ischemic conditions should be sought to limit tourniquet injury.

D. An alternative pediatric setting is 20 mm Hg above arterial occlusion pressure.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The pressure should be high enough to maintain the bloodless field but no higher than necessary. Cote states that the minimum pressure maintaining ischemic conditions should be sought to limit tourniquet injury, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 30 Orthopedic and spine surgery, PDF p. 1293, passage ID 188640]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Limiting inflation to 1.5–2 hours is a duration principle, but A does not address the minimum safe inflation pressure asked for. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 30 Orthopedic and spine surgery, PDF p. 1293, passage ID 188640]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Fifty mm Hg above Doppler occlusion pressure is a pediatric setting, but B is a specific alternative and not the general pressure-minimization principle. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 30 Orthopedic and spine surgery, PDF p. 1293, passage ID 188640]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote recommends seeking the minimum pressure that maintains ischemic conditions to limit tourniquet injury. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 30 Orthopedic and spine surgery, PDF p. 1293, passage ID 188640]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Twenty mm Hg above occlusion pressure is another pediatric setting, but D does not state the general minimum-pressure principle sought by the question. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 30 Orthopedic and spine surgery, PDF p. 1293, passage ID 188640]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the minimum-pressure principle and distinguishes duration and pediatric settings.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The requested principle is pressure minimization; pediatric numeric settings are contextual distractors.

## S02-Q117

In exsanguinating trauma managed with damage-control resuscitation, which outcome interpretation of a balanced 1:1:1 blood-product strategy versus 1:1:2 is best supported?

A. Damage-control resuscitation supplies clotting factors through plasma transfusion and direct platelet administration.

B. Tissue injury and endogenous anticoagulants can produce sustained coagulation abnormalities during large-volume resuscitation.

C. Viscoelastic monitoring can guide goal-directed treatment of trauma-induced coagulopathy.

D. Balanced 1:1:1 blood-product resuscitation improves 3-hour hemostasis and reduces 24-hour exsanguination deaths, although it does not improve overall mortality versus 1:1:2.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the outcome interpretation of 1:1:1 versus 1:1:2 balanced resuscitation. Chestnut reports improved 3-hour hemostasis and fewer 24-hour exsanguination deaths but no overall mortality improvement, so D is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1346, printed p. 1280, passage ID 276236]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Plasma and platelets supply clotting factors, but A describes the mechanism of balanced resuscitation and not the comparative outcomes requested. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1346, printed p. 1280, passage ID 276236]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Tissue injury and endogenous anticoagulants can sustain coagulation abnormalities, but B is pathophysiology and not the 1:1:1 versus 1:1:2 outcome result. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1346, printed p. 1280, passage ID 276236]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Viscoelastic monitoring can guide goal-directed treatment, but C describes monitoring rather than the trial’s comparative hemostasis and mortality findings. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1346, printed p. 1280, passage ID 276236]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut reports better 3-hour hemostasis and fewer 24-hour exsanguination deaths with 1:1:1, without improved overall mortality at 24 hours or 30 days versus 1:1:2. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1346, printed p. 1280, passage ID 276236]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly supplies the comparative outcomes and related coagulopathy concepts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key preserves both the early hemostasis benefit and absence of overall mortality benefit.

## S02-Q118

After high cervical spinal cord injury, hypotension and bradycardia persist despite no convincing evidence of major hemorrhage. Which shock phenotype is most consistent with the supplied physiology?

A. Neurogenic shock causes bradycardia and vasodilation from loss of central supraspinal sympathetic control.

B. Biochemical, vascular, and electrolyte changes can worsen spinal-cord lesions for up to 3 days.

C. Clinically significant cervical cord injury can occur without visible skeletal injury.

D. Spinal shock refers to loss of reflexes and is distinct from neurogenic shock.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** High cervical spinal-cord injury with persistent hypotension and bradycardia without major hemorrhage is the autonomic cardiovascular pattern of neurogenic shock. Miller attributes it to loss of supraspinal sympathetic control causing vasodilation and bradycardia, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 60. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 2356, printed p. 1979, passage ID 291661]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller states that high cervical SCI can cause neurogenic shock with bradycardia and vasodilation from loss of central supraspinal sympathetic control. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 60. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 2356, printed p. 1979, passage ID 291661]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Biochemical, vascular, and electrolyte changes can worsen SCI lesions for up to three days, but B describes secondary injury and not the shock phenotype. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2418, printed p. 2029, passage ID 291972]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Cervical injury can occur without visible skeletal injury, but C addresses diagnosis and not hypotension with bradycardia from sympathetic loss. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2418, printed p. 2029, passage ID 291972]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Spinal shock refers to loss of reflexes, but D is a neurologic terminology distinction and does not explain the autonomic cardiovascular findings in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2418, printed p. 2029, passage ID 291972]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passages directly support neurogenic shock and distinguish secondary injury, occult injury, and spinal shock.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Bradycardia and vasodilation without hemorrhage identify autonomic neurogenic shock.

## S02-Q124

Which four-factor infant respiratory profile best explains rapid desaturation during anesthetic apnea?

A. Increased FRC, larger low-resistance airways, a mature sustained hypoxic response, and low oxygen consumption.

B. Reduced FRC, smaller high-resistance airways, an immature biphasic hypoxic response, and high oxygen consumption.

C. Reduced FRC, smaller high-resistance airways, a mature sustained hypoxic response, and low oxygen consumption.

D. Increased FRC, larger low-resistance airways, an immature hypoxic response, and high oxygen consumption.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Infants desaturate rapidly because anesthesia reduces FRC, their airways are smaller and higher resistance, hypoxic ventilatory response is immature, and oxygen consumption is high. Stoelting supports the complete four-factor profile in B. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1793, passage ID 314735]

**A — false:** The infant profile is not increased FRC, large low-resistance airways, mature hypoxic response, and low oxygen use; Stoelting describes the opposite pattern. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1793, passage ID 314735]

**B — correct:** Correct: Stoelting describes anesthetic FRC reduction, proportionately smaller higher-resistance airways, a biphasic immature hypoxic response, and higher neonatal oxygen consumption. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1793, passage ID 314735]

**C — false:** Infant hypoxic response is immature and oxygen consumption is high, so C reverses two key features and does not fit rapid desaturation. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1793, passage ID 314735]

**D — false:** Infants have reduced rather than increased FRC and smaller rather than larger airways; D does not match the textbook profile. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1793, passage ID 314735]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly supplies all four physiologic factors.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The correct option is a conjunction; reversing any of the four factors changes the explanation for rapid desaturation.

## S02-Q125

Compared with adults, what volatile-anesthetic potency relationship should be anticipated in children?

A. Neonates have relatively larger volumes of distribution and lower clearances for most drugs.

B. Allometric scaling is more accurate for pediatric drug dose requirements than relying on a mg/kg calculation.

C. Volatile-anesthetic MAC is generally higher in children.

D. The neonatal airway differs from the adult airway in position, glottic shape, and vocal-cord angle.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Compared with adults, children generally require greater volatile-anesthetic concentration for the same MAC endpoint. Miller directly states that volatile-agent MAC is higher in children, so C is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2709, printed p. 72, passage ID 293413]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Neonatal distribution and clearance differ from adults, but A concerns pharmacokinetics and not volatile-agent potency. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2709, printed p. 72, passage ID 293413]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Allometric scaling may improve pediatric drug-dose estimation, but B addresses dosing methodology rather than volatile-anesthetic MAC. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2709, printed p. 72, passage ID 293413]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller states that the MAC of volatile anesthetic agents is higher in children. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2709, printed p. 72, passage ID 293413]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Neonatal airway anatomy differs in position and shape, but D is an airway-anatomy statement and not a volatile-potency relationship. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2709, printed p. 72, passage ID 293413]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states higher pediatric MAC and contrasts pharmacokinetic and airway facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks volatile-anesthetic potency, not general pediatric dosing or airway anatomy.

## S02-Q126

A neonate receives a highly protein-bound anesthetic. Which pharmacokinetic consequence should be anticipated?

A. Neonatal protein concentrations reach adult levels by the first year of life.

B. Clearance per kilogram is greater in a toddler than in an older child.

C. Caffeine and ceftriaxone can displace bilirubin from plasma proteins and increase kernicterus risk in sick neonates.

D. Reduced neonatal protein levels increase the free fraction and effect of highly protein-bound drugs.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Reduced neonatal plasma proteins increase the unbound fraction of highly protein-bound anesthetics and therefore their effect. Miller states this pharmacokinetic consequence directly, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2715, printed p. 2272, passage ID 293436]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Protein concentrations reaching adult levels by one year is a time-course fact, but A does not state the immediate free-fraction consequence in the neonate. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2715, printed p. 2272, passage ID 293436]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Greater toddler clearance per kilogram is a developmental comparison, but B does not explain highly protein-bound drug effect in a neonate. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2715, printed p. 2272, passage ID 293436]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Caffeine and ceftriaxone can displace bilirubin and increase kernicterus risk, but C concerns bilirubin binding rather than the anesthetic’s free fraction. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2715, printed p. 2272, passage ID 293436]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller states that reduced neonatal protein levels give highly bound drugs a higher free fraction and greater drug effect. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2715, printed p. 2272, passage ID 293436]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly supplies the free-fraction effect and related developmental and bilirubin-binding facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem asks for anesthetic pharmacokinetics arising from reduced protein binding, not clearance or bilirubin displacement.

## S02-Q127

A neonate receiving morphine has greater weight-based respiratory-depression risk than an adult. Which immature barrier feature specifically explains this risk?

A. Incomplete neonatal blood–brain-barrier function permits greater morphine brain penetration and respiratory depression.

B. Codeine has approximately one-tenth morphine potency, a separate opioid comparison.

C. Morphine is frequently used for postoperative analgesia in children, irrespective of age.

D. Neonatal morphine clearance is low but reaches adult levels by six to twelve months.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Neonatal morphine causes greater weight-based respiratory depression because the immature blood–brain barrier permits easy brain penetration. Miller states that mechanism directly, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 22. Opioids, PDF p. 696, printed p. 579, passage ID 283713]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller links greater neonatal morphine respiratory depression to easy brain penetration through incomplete blood–brain barriers. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 22. Opioids, PDF p. 696, printed p. 579, passage ID 283713]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Codeine has about one-tenth morphine potency, but B is a separate opioid comparison and does not explain neonatal morphine penetration. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2718, printed p. 2275, passage ID 293449]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Morphine is frequently used postoperatively in children, but C describes use frequency and not the immature-barrier mechanism. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2718, printed p. 2275, passage ID 293449]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Low neonatal clearance reaching adult levels by 6–12 months is a pharmacokinetic time course, but D does not provide the barrier explanation asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2718, printed p. 2275, passage ID 293449]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller passages directly support the blood–brain-barrier mechanism and distinguish potency, use, and clearance facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The requested discriminator is immature CNS barrier penetration, not opioid potency or clearance.

## S02-Q128

When selecting a cuffed or uncuffed tube for a young child, which comparative statement about subglottic airway-trauma risk is supported?

A. Cuffed tubes have lower subglottic-trauma risk than uncuffed tubes because they improve CO2 sampling and reduce pollution.

B. Cuffed versus uncuffed tubes have no documented increase in subglottic airway trauma.

C. Uncuffed tubes have greater subglottic-trauma risk than cuffed tubes whenever acceptable leak pressures are used.

D. Cuffed tubes have greater subglottic-trauma risk than uncuffed tubes because uncuffed tubes were historically preferred.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks whether cuffed tubes increase subglottic airway trauma compared with uncuffed tubes. Miller states that no data document increased trauma with cuffed versus uncuffed tubes, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2726, printed p. 2283, passage ID 293486]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Cuffed tubes may improve CO2 sampling and reduce pollution, but A does not state the documented trauma comparison. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2726, printed p. 2283, passage ID 293486]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that there is no data documenting increased subglottic airway trauma when cuffed rather than uncuffed tubes are used. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2726, printed p. 2283, passage ID 293486]

**C — false:** Miller does not state that uncuffed tubes have greater trauma whenever acceptable leak pressures are used; airway trauma can occur with uncuffed tubes, but the comparison does not support C. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2726, printed p. 2283, passage ID 293486]

**D — false:** Historical preference for uncuffed tubes does not establish greater cuffed-tube trauma, and Miller reports no documented increase with cuffed tubes; D does not follow. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2726, printed p. 2283, passage ID 293486]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the trauma comparison and related historical, leak, and CO2-sampling facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The item tests documented trauma evidence, not historical preference or other cuff advantages.

## S02-Q129

During pediatric emergence, which multi-factor physiologic profile best explains rapid desaturation?

A. Hypoventilation, airway obstruction, bronchospasm, aspiration, and atelectasis can reduce oxygenation during emergence.

B. Active upper-respiratory infection increases airway reactivity, atelectasis, and secretions, worsening desaturation.

C. General anesthesia reduces FRC and hypoxic/hypercapnic drive while altering hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction.

D. Shivering increases oxygen consumption two- to fivefold and can worsen emergence desaturation.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Emergence hypoxia is accelerated when anesthesia inhibits hypoxic and hypercapnic drive, reduces FRC, and alters hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction. Cote identifies that complete physiologic profile in C, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 45 The post anesthesia care unit and beyond, PDF p. 1942, passage ID 191560]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Hypoventilation, obstruction, bronchospasm, aspiration, and atelectasis can cause hypoxemia, but A is a broad differential and not the multi-factor emergence physiology asked for. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 45 The post anesthesia care unit and beyond, PDF p. 1942, passage ID 191560]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Upper-respiratory infection can worsen desaturation through reactivity, atelectasis, and secretions, but B is a comorbidity profile rather than the general emergence mechanism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 45 The post anesthesia care unit and beyond, PDF p. 1942, passage ID 191560]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote states that anesthesia inhibits hypoxic and hypercapnic drive, reduces FRC, and alters hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, accelerating emergence hypoxia. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 45 The post anesthesia care unit and beyond, PDF p. 1942, passage ID 191560]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Shivering can raise oxygen consumption two- to fivefold, but D describes a worsening factor and not the primary multi-factor emergence physiology. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 45 The post anesthesia care unit and beyond, PDF p. 1942, passage ID 191560]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly distinguishes the emergence mechanism from other hypoxemia causes and exacerbating factors.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The keyed option is the multi-factor physiologic explanation, not a broad differential or single aggravating factor.

## S02-Q130

A child develops upper-airway obstruction during emergence. Which clinical pattern supports laryngospasm rather than bronchospasm?

A. Nebulized levalbuterol or metaproterenol in oxygen can resolve postoperative bronchospasm.

B. Prolonged expiration or wheezing during forced vital-capacity expiration can reveal airway resistance.

C. Preoperative spirometric evidence of increased airway resistance predicts postoperative bronchospasm risk.

D. Laryngospasm produces stridor, suprasternal and supraclavicular retractions, and paradoxical rocking chest movements.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for clinical signs supporting laryngospasm rather than bronchospasm. Barash lists stridor, suprasternal and supraclavicular retractions, and paradoxical rocking chest movements, so D is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 43. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3755, passage ID 249742]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Nebulized levalbuterol or metaproterenol can resolve postoperative bronchospasm, but A is treatment for a different process and not laryngospasm signs. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 54. Postanesthesia Recovery, PDF p. 4583, passage ID 251302]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Prolonged expiration or wheezing can reveal airway resistance, but B describes bronchospasm physiology rather than the upper-airway laryngospasm pattern. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 54. Postanesthesia Recovery, PDF p. 4583, passage ID 251301]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Preoperative increased airway resistance predicts postoperative bronchospasm risk, but C is a risk prediction and not the laryngospasm examination pattern. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 54. Postanesthesia Recovery, PDF p. 4583, passage ID 251301]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash identifies stridor, suprasternal/supraclavicular retractions, and paradoxical rocking chest movements as clinical signs of laryngospasm. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 43. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3755, passage ID 249742]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly distinguish laryngospasm signs from bronchospasm signs, treatment, and risk prediction.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The decisive distinction is upper-airway stridor/retractions/rocking rather than lower-airway wheeze or resistance.

## S02-Q131

In a severely hypoxemic neonate with pulmonary hypertension, which combined intervention may decrease pulmonary vascular resistance?

A. Use hyperventilation to induce respiratory alkalosis together with 100% oxygen.

B. Ventilate with small tidal volumes and low inflating pressures to avoid a contralateral pneumothorax.

C. Design the anesthetic to minimize sympathetic discharge, which may exacerbate pulmonary hypertension.

D. Use nitric oxide or high-frequency oscillatory ventilation during surgical repair.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The severely hypoxemic neonate has pulmonary hypertension, and the question asks for a combined intervention that lowers pulmonary vascular resistance. Cote recommends hyperventilation to respiratory alkalosis with 100% oxygen, so A is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 658, passage ID 185551]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote states that hyperventilation producing respiratory alkalosis and 100% oxygen may be administered to decrease pulmonary vascular resistance. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 658, passage ID 185551]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Small tidal volumes and low inflating pressures reduce contralateral pneumothorax risk, but B does not give the requested pulmonary-vascular-resistance intervention. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 658, passage ID 185551]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Minimizing sympathetic discharge may limit pulmonary hypertension, but C is an anesthetic-design principle and not the combined oxygen/alkalosis intervention. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 658, passage ID 185551]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Nitric oxide and high-frequency oscillatory ventilation have been used during repair, but D does not state the specific combined intervention asked for. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 658, passage ID 185551]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the hyperventilation/oxygen intervention and related pulmonary and anesthetic measures.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks for one combined PVR-lowering intervention rather than any supportive strategy.

## S02-Q132

A newborn becomes hypoxemic and acidotic while fetal shunt pathways remain open. Which change can reverse the circulation to right-to-left shunting?

A. Increased afterload closes the foramen-ovale flap valve and reverses ductal shunt direction.

B. Increased pulmonary vascular reactivity in response to hypoxia and acidosis may precipitate reversal to right-to-left shunting.

C. Delay cord clamping until respirations begin to improve cardiac filling and pulmonary blood flow.

D. Expect extrapulmonary and intrapulmonary shunting to persist for some time after birth.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Hypoxia and acidosis increase pulmonary vascular reactivity and can reverse fetal circulation to right-to-left shunting. Cote describes this ‘flip-flop’ mechanism directly, so B is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 33, passage ID 182585]

**A — false:** Cote does not support increased afterload as the mechanism here; the stem’s hypoxia/acidosis trigger increases pulmonary vascular reactivity and reverses flow. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 33, passage ID 182585]

**B — correct:** Correct: Cote states that increased pulmonary vascular reactivity in response to hypoxia and acidosis may precipitate reversal to right-to-left shunting. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 33, passage ID 182585]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Delayed cord clamping until respirations may improve cardiac filling and pulmonary flow, but C does not explain hypoxia/acidosis-induced reversal of shunting. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 33, passage ID 182585]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Fetal-channel and intrapulmonary shunting can persist after birth, but D describes persistence and not the acute reversal mechanism in the stem. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 33, passage ID 182585]
