**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the hypoxia/acidosis reactivity mechanism and related transitional-circulation facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key is an acute trigger for reversal, not normal persistence or cord-management physiology.

## S02-Q142

A patient with insulin-treated diabetes and a creatinine of 2.3 mg/dL is being evaluated before noncardiac surgery. Which statement correctly identifies the predictor set used by the Revised Cardiac Risk Index (RCRI)?

A. Cardiac complications rise as the number of RCRI risk factors increases.

B. An RCRI score of 0 carries approximately 0.4% perioperative cardiovascular risk.

C. RCRI predictors are high-risk surgery, ischemic heart disease, congestive heart failure, cerebrovascular disease, insulin-dependent diabetes, and creatinine above 2 mg/dL from chronic kidney disease.

D. The ACS NSQIP and MICA are other validated risk-prediction tools.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the Revised Cardiac Risk Index predictor set. Barash lists high-risk surgery, ischemic heart disease, heart failure, cerebrovascular disease, insulin-dependent diabetes, and chronic kidney disease with creatinine above 2 mg/dL, so C is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1762, passage ID 246272]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Risk rises with increasing RCRI factors, but A states the score’s trend and not the complete predictor set requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1762, passage ID 246272]

**B — true but irrelevant:** An RCRI score of zero carries about 0.4% risk, but B gives a result estimate rather than the predictor list. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1762, passage ID 246272]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash’s six RCRI predictors are high-risk surgery, ischemic heart disease, CHF, cerebrovascular disease, insulin-dependent diabetes, and chronic kidney disease with creatinine above 2 mg/dL. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1762, passage ID 246272]

**D — true but irrelevant:** ACS NSQIP and MICA are validated alternative tools, but D does not identify the RCRI predictors asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1762, passage ID 246272]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly lists the six predictors and related risk/tool facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for predictors, not score-associated risk or alternative tools.

## S02-Q143

A patient with unstable angina is being considered for elective noncardiac surgery. Which statement best describes the appropriate preoperative priority?

A. For high-risk surgery with multiple risk factors and poor functional capacity, noninvasive stress testing is reasonable if it will change management.

B. Further-testing decisions depend on the interaction of clinical risk factors, surgery-specific risk, and functional capacity.

C. Routine noninvasive stress-test screening is not recommended for low-risk noncardiac surgery.

D. Patients with unstable coronary syndromes should be identified, receive appropriate consultation, and have treatment instituted before elective surgery.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Unstable angina is an unstable coronary syndrome, so elective surgery requires identification, consultation, and treatment before proceeding. Barash states that priority directly, making D correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1763, passage ID 246274]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Stress testing may be reasonable for selected high-risk patients if it changes management, but A does not address the unstable coronary syndrome priority. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1769, passage ID 246284]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Further-testing decisions do depend on clinical, surgical, and functional factors, but B is a testing principle and not the required treatment-before-elective-surgery priority. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1763, passage ID 246274]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Routine stress testing is not recommended for low-risk surgery, but C concerns low-risk patients and does not address unstable angina. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1769, passage ID 246284]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash says patients with unstable coronary syndromes should be identified, appropriately consulted, and treated before elective surgery. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1763, passage ID 246274]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly distinguish unstable-syndrome treatment priority from selective stress-testing principles.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The unstable clinical condition overrides routine additional-testing discussion.

## S02-Q144

A patient with known chronic lung disease is being evaluated before a planned thoracic operation. Which statement best describes the role of pulmonary function testing in this setting?

A. Pulmonary function tests complement preoperative evaluation and risk stratification in patients with known risk factors, preexisting lung disease, or planned thoracic surgery.

B. Active smoking, diffuse wheezing, and productive cough may predict pulmonary complications better than spirometric tests.

C. A thorough history and physical examination remains the cornerstone of preoperative pulmonary assessment.

D. The value of routine preoperative pulmonary function testing remains controversial.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Known lung disease and planned thoracic surgery are indications for pulmonary function testing as a complement to assessment and risk stratification. Miller states that role directly, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 12. Respiratory Physiology and Pathophysiology, PDF p. 327, printed p. 262, passage ID 282020]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller states that PFTs complement evaluation and risk stratification in patients with risk factors, preexisting lung disease, or planned thoracic surgery. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 12. Respiratory Physiology and Pathophysiology, PDF p. 327, printed p. 262, passage ID 282020]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Smoking, wheeze, and productive cough may predict complications better than spirometry, but B does not state the role of PFTs in this high-risk setting. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 2 Anesthetic considerations for obstructive lung disease, PDF p. 40, printed p. 29, passage ID 232415]

**C — true but irrelevant:** History and physical examination remain the cornerstone, but C does not exclude the complementary role of PFTs in known disease or thoracic surgery. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 12. Respiratory Physiology and Pathophysiology, PDF p. 327, printed p. 262, passage ID 282020]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Routine testing’s value remains controversial, but D does not answer its targeted use in a patient with known risk factors and planned thoracic surgery. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 2 Anesthetic considerations for obstructive lung disease, PDF p. 40, printed p. 29, passage ID 232415]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller and Stoelting passages directly distinguish targeted PFT use from routine testing and clinical assessment.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The patient’s known disease and thoracic operation make targeted testing distinct from routine screening.

## S02-Q145

A 68-year-old with stable symptoms is scheduled for intermediate-risk noncardiac surgery. The team agrees that an abnormal preoperative stress-test result would not change medical therapy or the decision to proceed. Which principle should guide additional cardiovascular testing?

A. Functional status determines the need for further cardiac workup in patients at elevated perioperative MACE risk.

B. Preoperative cardiovascular testing should not be performed when its results would not change perioperative management.

C. Patients with 4 to more than 10 METs of exercise capacity may proceed without further cardiac testing.

D. Further testing decisions incorporate clinical and surgery-specific risk factors.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** If an abnormal test would not change medical therapy or the decision to proceed, additional cardiovascular testing has no management consequence. Barash explicitly says not to perform it in that circumstance, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1763, passage ID 246274]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Functional status can guide workup in elevated-risk patients, but A is broader than and does not override the stem’s no-management-change condition. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 40. Anesthesia for Vascular and Endovascular Surgery, PDF p. 3403, passage ID 249087]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash states that preoperative cardiovascular testing should not be performed when results will not change perioperative management. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1763, passage ID 246274]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Moderate-to-excellent exercise capacity may permit proceeding without further testing, but C is an exercise-capacity pathway and not the stated management-impact principle. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 40. Anesthesia for Vascular and Endovascular Surgery, PDF p. 3403, passage ID 249087]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Clinical and surgery-specific risk factors enter testing decisions, but D does not state the decisive rule that testing must change management. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1763, passage ID 246274]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly states the management-impact rule and related risk/functional principles.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem explicitly says the result would not change management, making the testing decision straightforward.

## S02-Q146

According to Barash's Clinical Anesthesia, 9th edition, a patient with type 1 diabetes is fasting before surgery. Which insulin plan is supported?

A. Insulin-pump patients may continue the pump for short operations or switch to intravenous insulin for longer or major operations.

B. Patients should arrive early and have blood glucose checked on arrival and hourly if surgery is delayed.

C. During fasting, continue basal insulin in type 1 diabetes to prevent ketoacidosis and hold usual rapid- or short-acting insulin unless hyperglycemia requires correction.

D. A slow dextrose infusion can prevent hypoglycemia while a fasting patient receives basal insulin.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Type 1 diabetes requires basal insulin during fasting to prevent ketoacidosis, while usual rapid or short-acting insulin is held unless correction is needed. Barash states that complete plan, so C is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1781, passage ID 246307]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Pump continuation for short cases or IV insulin for longer cases is a pump-management option, but A does not state the fasting basal/short-acting plan. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1781, passage ID 246307]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Early arrival and glucose checks are monitoring logistics, but B omits the insulin distinction that prevents ketoacidosis during fasting. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1781, passage ID 246307]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash directs continuation of basal insulin during fasting and holding rapid/short-acting insulin unless hyperglycemia requires correction. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1781, passage ID 246307]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Slow dextrose can prevent hypoglycemia during fasting basal-insulin management, but D gives an adjunct and not the core type 1 insulin plan. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 47. Endocrine Function, PDF p. 4056, passage ID 250301]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support the type 1 insulin plan and related pump, glucose-check, and dextrose details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Basal insulin continuation is the safety discriminator during fasting.

## S02-Q147

A patient with pheochromocytoma is being prepared for anesthesia and develops an arrhythmia requiring treatment. Which blockade sequence is essential before adding a beta blocker?

A. Nicardipine is an alternative preoperative approach for pheochromocytoma.

B. Pheochromocytoma preparation includes alpha blockade and intravascular volume repletion in norepinephrine-dominant disease.

C. Phenoxybenzamine is commonly started at 10 mg orally twice daily and titrated upward.

D. Beta-blockade for pheochromocytoma-related arrhythmias must be preceded by effective alpha-adrenergic blockade to prevent paradoxical hypertension.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Adding beta blockade before alpha blockade in pheochromocytoma can cause paradoxical hypertension. Chestnut states that effective alpha blockade must precede beta blockade for arrhythmias, so D is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 43 Endocrine Disorders, PDF p. 1140, printed p. 1074, passage ID 275320]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Nicardipine is an alternative preparation approach, but A does not state the required alpha-before-beta sequence. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 43 Endocrine Disorders, PDF p. 1140, printed p. 1074, passage ID 275320]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Alpha blockade and volume repletion are preparation elements, but B does not answer the sequence before treating an arrhythmia with beta blockade. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 43 Endocrine Disorders, PDF p. 1140, printed p. 1074, passage ID 275320]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Phenoxybenzamine dosing is a preparation detail, but C does not state why alpha blockade must precede beta blockade. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 43 Endocrine Disorders, PDF p. 1140, printed p. 1074, passage ID 275320]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut states that beta antagonists for arrhythmias must follow effective alpha blockade to prevent paradoxical hypertension. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 43 Endocrine Disorders, PDF p. 1140, printed p. 1074, passage ID 275320]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly states the sequence and related preparation alternatives.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The sequence, rather than the choice or dose of alpha agent, is the key safety point.

## S02-Q148

According to A Practice of Anesthesia for Infants and Children, 7th edition (Coté et al.), a patient with hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal-axis suppression is undergoing a physiologically stressful procedure. Which perioperative steroid plan is supported?

A. Patients with hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal-axis suppression or high adrenal-insufficiency risk require stress-dose hydrocortisone for physiologically stressful procedures, with perioperative redosing and postoperative tapering.

B. The postoperative steroid taper should be planned according to the patient and procedure.

C. Stress-dose steroids may not be required for minor low-stress procedures if daily replacement continues.

D. Patients taking low-dose prednisone for less than 3–4 weeks are unlikely to have hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal-axis suppression.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Diagnosed HPAA suppression or high adrenal-insufficiency risk during a physiologically stressful procedure requires stress-dose hydrocortisone, redosing, and postoperative taper. Cote states that plan directly, so A is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 25 Essentials of endocrinology, PDF p. 1136, passage ID 187875]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote recommends hydrocortisone at induction with intraoperative redosing and a postoperative taper for HPAA suppression or high risk during physiologic stress. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 25 Essentials of endocrinology, PDF p. 1136, passage ID 187875]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Postoperative tapering is planned according to patient and procedure, but B omits the stress-dose indication and perioperative regimen asked for. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 25 Essentials of endocrinology, PDF p. 1136, passage ID 187875]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Minor low-stress procedures may not require stress doses if replacement continues, but C is a different low-stress condition from this physiologically stressful procedure. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 25 Essentials of endocrinology, PDF p. 1136, passage ID 187875]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Low-dose prednisone for a short course is unlikely to suppress HPAA, but D describes low-risk exposure and not this known/high-risk suppression scenario. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 25 Essentials of endocrinology, PDF p. 1136, passage ID 187875]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly supports stress dosing, redosing, tapering, and low-risk exceptions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The physiologic stress and HPAA-risk qualifiers determine whether the stress-dose pathway applies.

## S02-Q149

In a patient with chronic kidney disease at risk for perioperative acute kidney injury, which prevention principle is most appropriate?

A. GFR is the best measure of overall renal function.

B. Perioperative AKI prevention depends on maintaining adequate renal perfusion and avoiding nephrotoxins.

C. AKI is classically divided into prerenal, intrarenal, and postrenal causes.

D. Perioperative AKI risk factors include advanced age, CKD, sepsis, diabetes, hypovolemia, anemia, chronic liver disease, and heart failure.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for prevention of perioperative AKI in CKD. Stoelting directly states that prevention hinges on adequate renal perfusion and avoiding nephrotoxins, so B is correct. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 21 Renal disease, PDF p. 447, passage ID 234141]

**A — true but irrelevant:** GFR is the best overall renal-function measure, but A is an assessment fact and not the AKI-prevention principle requested. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 21 Renal disease, PDF p. 447, passage ID 234141]

**B — correct:** Correct: Stoelting states that AKI prevention hinges on maintaining adequate renal perfusion and avoiding nephrotoxins. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 21 Renal disease, PDF p. 447, passage ID 234141]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Prerenal, intrarenal, and postrenal categories describe AKI etiology, but C does not state how to prevent perioperative AKI. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 21 Renal disease, PDF p. 447, passage ID 234141]

**D — true but irrelevant:** The listed conditions are AKI risk factors, but D identifies risk rather than the prevention measures asked for. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 21 Renal disease, PDF p. 447, passage ID 234141]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly distinguishes prevention, measurement, etiology, and risk factors.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks prevention, not risk identification or AKI classification.

## S02-Q150

A dialysis-dependent patient is scheduled for elective surgery. Which complete perioperative plan is best?

A. Dialyze on the operative morning, sample potassium immediately, and use nondominant upper-extremity veins for access.

B. Dialyze immediately before surgery, supplement potassium from the first postdialysis sample, and preserve access veins.

C. Dialyze the day before, check morning electrolytes after equilibration, optimize volume, and preserve potential fistula veins.

D. Dialyze the day before, omit morning electrolytes, and use potential fistula veins for routine blood draws.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** For elective surgery in a dialysis-dependent patient, Miller recommends dialysis the day before, morning electrolyte checks after equilibration, volume optimization, and preservation of fistula-access veins. C contains the complete plan. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 1009, printed p. 847, passage ID 285215]

**A — false:** Same-day dialysis and immediate potassium sampling do not allow equilibration, and using access veins threatens fistula preservation; A does not fit the supported plan. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 1009, printed p. 847, passage ID 285215]

**B — false:** Immediate preoperative dialysis and early potassium supplementation risk misinterpretation before equilibration, so B does not follow Miller’s day-before plan. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 1009, printed p. 847, passage ID 285215]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller recommends day-before dialysis, morning electrolyte assessment after equilibration, and avoiding venipuncture in potential fistula-access veins. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 1009, printed p. 847, passage ID 285215]

**D — false:** Omitting morning electrolytes and using potential fistula veins for draws contradicts the supported monitoring and access-preservation plan, so D is incomplete and unsafe. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 1009, printed p. 847, passage ID 285215]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly supplies timing, equilibration, electrolyte checks, volume, and access preservation.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The complete answer requires all four elements; day-before dialysis alone is insufficient.

## S02-Q151

A patient with advanced chronic kidney disease is scheduled for a painful operation and will need opioid analgesia. Which statement specifically addresses opioid-metabolite accumulation and should guide selection?

A. Gabapentinoids are renally excreted and must be dose adjusted based on creatinine clearance.

B. Postoperative urinary retention is a frequent cause of failure to void after surgery.

C. Contrast media, nephrotoxic antibiotics, and NSAIDs can exacerbate underlying kidney disease.

D. Meperidine and morphine should be avoided in renal compromise because renally excreted metabolites can accumulate to toxic levels.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks specifically about opioid-metabolite accumulation in renal compromise. Miller states that meperidine and morphine metabolites can accumulate to toxic levels, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2173, printed p. 1829, passage ID 290736]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Gabapentinoid renal dose adjustment is important, but A does not address the opioid-metabolite accumulation asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2173, printed p. 1829, passage ID 290736]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Urinary retention is a postoperative complication, but B does not concern renal excretion of opioid metabolites. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2173, printed p. 1829, passage ID 290736]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Nephrotoxic medications can worsen kidney disease, but C is a nephrotoxin warning and not the opioid-selection statement requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2173, printed p. 1829, passage ID 290736]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller recommends avoiding meperidine and morphine in renal compromise because renally excreted metabolites, including normeperidine and morphine glucuronides, can accumulate to toxic levels. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2173, printed p. 1829, passage ID 290736]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly supports opioid avoidance and related renal-risk facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The requested issue is metabolite accumulation, not general nephrotoxin avoidance or urinary retention.

## S02-Q152

A child with chronic liver disease is being assessed before surgery using a PELD/MELD-style hepatic-reserve score. Which statement correctly describes what the score estimates?

A. PELD and MELD scoring systems assign a score reflecting probability of mortality within the next 3 months.

B. Interpret the PELD/MELD score as the portal vein's approximately 70% contribution to hepatic blood flow.

C. Interpret the PELD/MELD score as the liver's eight structural segments, each with its own arterial, portal, venous, and biliary structures.

D. Interpret the PELD/MELD score as its component variables—age, growth failure, serum albumin, bilirubin, and INR—rather than as a mortality estimate.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** PELD and MELD are hepatic-reserve scores estimating mortality probability over the next three months. Cote states that purpose directly, so A is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 29 Organ transplantation, PDF p. 1218, passage ID 188287]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote states that PELD and MELD assign a score reflecting probability of mortality within the next three months. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 29 Organ transplantation, PDF p. 1218, passage ID 188287]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Portal flow of about 70% is a hepatic vascular fact, but B does not describe what the PELD/MELD score estimates. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 28 Essentials of hepatology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 188230]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Eight liver segments describe anatomy, but C is not the mortality estimate represented by PELD/MELD. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 28 Essentials of hepatology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 188229]

**D — incomplete:** Age, growth failure, albumin, bilirubin, and INR are PELD variables, but D incorrectly substitutes component variables for the score’s mortality-estimate purpose. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 29 Organ transplantation, PDF p. 1218, passage ID 188287]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Cote passages directly distinguish score purpose from component variables and liver anatomy/flow.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks what the score estimates, not how it is constructed.

## S02-Q153

A patient with portal hypertension and delayed gastric emptying is scheduled for transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt (TIPS) placement and cannot reliably remain still during the procedure. Which statement specifically describes a benefit of general anesthesia for this case?

A. Sedation is commonly used to facilitate TIPS placement.

B. General anesthesia for TIPS can limit patient movement, control diaphragmatic excursion, and reduce aspiration risk.

C. Volume resuscitation may be necessary in patients with recent variceal bleeding.

D. TIPS decompresses portal hypertension in the setting of esophageal varices or intractable ascites.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient cannot remain still and has aspiration risk during TIPS. Barash states that general anesthesia limits movement, controls diaphragmatic excursion, and reduces aspiration risk, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 46. The Liver: Surgery and Anesthesia, PDF p. 3966, passage ID 250142]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Sedation is commonly used for TIPS, but A does not state the general-anesthesia benefits relevant to inability to remain still and aspiration risk. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 46. The Liver: Surgery and Anesthesia, PDF p. 3966, passage ID 250142]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash states that general anesthesia limits movement, controls diaphragmatic excursion, and reduces aspiration risk during TIPS. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 46. The Liver: Surgery and Anesthesia, PDF p. 3966, passage ID 250142]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Volume resuscitation may be needed after recent variceal bleeding, but C is a hemodynamic preparation issue and not a general-anesthesia benefit. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 46. The Liver: Surgery and Anesthesia, PDF p. 3966, passage ID 250142]

**D — true but irrelevant:** TIPS decompresses portal hypertension for varices or ascites, but D states the procedural indication rather than the anesthesia benefit asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 46. The Liver: Surgery and Anesthesia, PDF p. 3966, passage ID 250142]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly states the general-anesthesia benefit and related TIPS facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The inability to remain still and aspiration risk make the anesthesia-benefit option decisive.

## S02-Q154

Which pair names the liver's two vascular inflow sources?

A. At any given time, the liver contains approximately 13% of circulating blood volume.

B. During the neonatal period, liver function is immature and clearance of most xenobiotics is poor.

C. The liver receives blood from two sources: the portal vein and the hepatic artery.

D. The portal vein accounts for approximately 70% of hepatic blood flow.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the liver’s two vascular inflow sources. Cote states that blood arrives through the portal vein and hepatic artery, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 28 Essentials of hepatology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 188229]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The liver contains about 13% of circulating blood volume, but A is a storage-volume fact and not an inflow-source pair. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 28 Essentials of hepatology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 188230]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Immature neonatal clearance is a developmental pharmacology fact, but B does not identify the two hepatic inflows. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 28 Essentials of hepatology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 188230]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote identifies the portal vein and hepatic artery as the liver’s two blood-flow sources. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 28 Essentials of hepatology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 188229]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Portal vein contributes about 70% of hepatic flow, but D gives a proportion and not the requested pair of inflow vessels. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 28 Essentials of hepatology, PDF p. 1207, passage ID 188230]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote hepatology passage directly identifies both sources and the related liver facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks sources, not flow proportion, blood volume, or neonatal metabolism.

## S02-Q155

According to Miller’s Anesthesia, 10th edition, a patient develops fever, flank pain, and dark urine shortly after a transfusion begins. What immediate action is required when a hemolytic transfusion reaction is suspected?

A. Maintain urine output at a minimum of 75–100 mL/h with fluids, mannitol, and possibly furosemide.

B. Determine platelet count, prothrombin time, partial thromboplastin time, and serum fibrinogen level.

C. Assay urine and plasma hemoglobin concentrations.

D. Stop the transfusion immediately when a hemolytic transfusion reaction is suspected.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Fever, flank pain, and dark urine shortly after starting transfusion suggest a hemolytic reaction. Miller’s ordered response begins by stopping the transfusion immediately, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 45. Patient Blood Management: Transfusion Therapy, PDF p. 1703, printed p. 1425, passage ID 288492]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Maintaining urine output is a subsequent supportive step, but A is not the immediate first action when a hemolytic reaction is suspected. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 45. Patient Blood Management: Transfusion Therapy, PDF p. 1703, printed p. 1425, passage ID 288492]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Coagulation studies are part of the evaluation, but B is later investigation and not the immediate transfusion-stop action. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 45. Patient Blood Management: Transfusion Therapy, PDF p. 1703, printed p. 1425, passage ID 288492]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Urine and plasma hemoglobin assays help evaluate hemolysis, but C is diagnostic testing after the immediate stop decision. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 45. Patient Blood Management: Transfusion Therapy, PDF p. 1703, printed p. 1425, passage ID 288492]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller’s first listed action for suspected hemolytic transfusion reaction is to stop the transfusion. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 45. Patient Blood Management: Transfusion Therapy, PDF p. 1703, printed p. 1425, passage ID 288492]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller protocol orders transfusion cessation before urine-output support and laboratory evaluation.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The word ‘immediate’ makes the sequence, not the later workup, decisive.

## S02-Q156

Which plan combines avoidance of the principal perioperative sickling triggers with individualized transfusion decision factors?

A. Avoid dehydration, hypothermia, hypoxia, and acidosis; individualize transfusion by baseline hemoglobin, prior complications, disease severity, and surgical stress.

B. Tourniquets should be avoided if possible to avoid metabolic acidosis and regional hypoxemia.

C. All anesthetic techniques are acceptable for patients with sickle-cell disease.

D. Transfusions are administered to achieve a hemoglobin concentration greater than 9 g/dL before surgery.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Sickle-cell perioperative protection requires avoiding dehydration, hypothermia, hypoxia, and acidosis while individualizing transfusion decisions by baseline hemoglobin, prior complications, disease severity, and surgical stress. Miller states this combined strategy, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1098, printed p. 923, passage ID 285661]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller lists avoidance of dehydration, hypothermia, hypoxia, and acidosis and those individualized transfusion factors. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1098, printed p. 923, passage ID 285661]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Avoiding tourniquets may limit regional hypoxemia and acidosis, but B is one precaution and not the combined trigger/transfusion strategy requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1098, printed p. 923, passage ID 285661]

**C — true but irrelevant:** All anesthetic techniques may be acceptable, but C does not address trigger avoidance or individualized transfusion decisions. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1098, printed p. 923, passage ID 285661]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A preoperative hemoglobin above 9 g/dL is a transfusion target in selected cases, but D omits trigger avoidance and the individualized decision factors. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1098, printed p. 923, passage ID 285661]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly supports the combined trigger-avoidance and transfusion decision strategy.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question requires both perioperative trigger mitigation and individualized transfusion selection.

## S02-Q157

According to the current Thrombosis Canada Clinical Guide: Warfarin, a patient taking warfarin needs emergency reversal before urgent surgery. Which regimen is recommended?

A. Warfarin should be interrupted for most surgery or procedures, with bridging reserved for a small selected high-risk group.

B. For emergency warfarin reversal, administer intravenous vitamin K together with a four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate.

C. There is no role for subcutaneous vitamin K.

D. When emergency reversal is not required but the INR is very elevated, oral vitamin K can be used.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Emergency warfarin reversal before urgent surgery requires rapid factor replacement plus vitamin K. Barash states that emergency reversal uses PCC, with four-factor PCC preferred and vitamin K replacement, so B is the textbook-supported answer; the frozen web evidence is not used. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1355, passage ID 245564]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Interruption and selective bridging describe routine perioperative management, but A does not provide the emergency reversal regimen requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1355, passage ID 245564]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash states that immediate surgery or major bleeding requires PCC for emergency reversal, with four-factor PCC generally preferred and vitamin K replacement required. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1355, passage ID 245564]

**C — true but irrelevant:** No role for subcutaneous vitamin K is a route-specific point, but C does not state the emergency PCC-plus-IV-vitamin-K regimen. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1355, passage ID 245564]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Oral vitamin K can be used when emergency reversal is not required, but D describes a nonemergency situation unlike this urgent-surgery stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1355, passage ID 245564]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The frozen evidence was living-web guidance; Barash directly supports emergency PCC reversal and vitamin K replacement.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence was non-textbook guidance; the new Barash passage is the source of truth for emergency reversal.

## S02-Q174

Two nondepolarizing neuromuscular blockers are compared at equipotent doses. Which statement correctly relates potency measurements to their expected onset?

A. Efficacy refers to the position of the concentration-response curve on the y-axis, whereas potency refers to relative drug concentration for a particular response.

B. Efficacy measures the intrinsic ability of a drug to produce a physiologic or clinical effect.

C. For nondepolarizing neuromuscular-blocking drugs, potency is expressed by ED50 or ED95, and low potency predicts rapid onset whereas high potency predicts slow onset.

D. For an equipotent dose, a low-potency drug has more molecules available to diffuse from capillary to the neuromuscular junction.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** For nondepolarizing blockers, potency is quantified by ED50/ED95 and inversely predicts onset speed. Stoelting states that low potency predicts rapid onset and high potency slow onset, so C is correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 12. Neuromuscular-Blocking Drugs and Reversal Agents, PDF p. 1046, passage ID 313603]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Efficacy and potency have the described graph meanings, but A is a general pharmacology definition and not the NMBD onset relationship. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 2. Basic Principles of Pharmacology, PDF p. 216, passage ID 312431]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Efficacy measures intrinsic effect-producing ability, but B does not address ED50/ED95 or onset speed. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 2. Basic Principles of Pharmacology, PDF p. 216, passage ID 312431]

**C — correct:** Correct: Stoelting expresses NMBD potency as ED50 or ED95 and states that low potency predicts rapid onset while high potency predicts slow onset. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 12. Neuromuscular-Blocking Drugs and Reversal Agents, PDF p. 1046, passage ID 313603]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A low-potency drug can have more molecules at an equipotent dose, but D offers the explanatory detail and not the requested potency/onset statement. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 12. Neuromuscular-Blocking Drugs and Reversal Agents, PDF p. 1046, passage ID 313603]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting passages directly support potency definitions, efficacy distinctions, molecule explanation, and onset relationship.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The keyed statement combines the potency measurement with its inverse onset relationship.

## S02-Q175

A patient receives a propofol infusion for a 4-hour operation, whereas another patient receives propofol continuously for several days in the ICU. Which recovery statement is most accurate after the infusions are stopped?

A. Remifentanil context-sensitive half-time is independent of infusion duration and is estimated at about 4 minutes.

B. Context-sensitive half-time is the time for plasma drug concentration to decline by 50% after terminating an infusion of a particular duration.

C. As infusion duration increases, context-sensitive half-time increases for all drugs, a phenomenon not described by elimination half-life.

D. For propofol infusions lasting up to 8 hours, context-sensitive half-time is less than 40 minutes and is minimally influenced by infusion duration at surgical durations, whereas prolonged ICU infusions can produce prolonged effects after discontinuation.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Propofol has a short context-sensitive half-time at surgical infusion durations but prolonged effects after days of ICU infusion. Stoelting states both duration-dependent contexts, so D is correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 5. Intravenous Sedatives and Hypnotics, PDF p. 541, passage ID 312883]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Remifentanil’s context-sensitive half-time is about 4 minutes and infusion-duration independent, but A concerns another drug and not propofol recovery. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 7. Opioid Agonists and Antagonists, PDF p. 768, passage ID 313211]

**B — true but irrelevant:** The definition of context-sensitive half-time is correct, but B does not apply it to the two propofol infusion durations in the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 30. Monitored Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 2509, passage ID 247509]

**C — false:** Context-sensitive half-time does not increase uniformly for all drugs; propofol is minimally duration-sensitive at surgical hours but prolonged after ICU days, so C does not fit. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 5. Intravenous Sedatives and Hypnotics, PDF p. 541, passage ID 312883]

**D — correct:** Correct: Stoelting states propofol context-sensitive half-time is under 40 minutes up to 8 hours and minimally duration-influenced at surgical durations, but prolonged ICU infusions can prolong effects. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 5. Intravenous Sedatives and Hypnotics, PDF p. 541, passage ID 312883]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting and Barash passages directly support the definition, propofol duration contrast, and remifentanil comparison.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The two infusion contexts—hours in surgery versus days in ICU—are the decisive discriminator.

## S02-Q176

When estimating the half-life of a drug undergoing first-order elimination, which graphical method is supported?

A. A series of concentration-time points on a semilogarithmic graph can determine half-life when the kinetics are first-order exponential.

B. Context-sensitive half-time is the time for plasma concentration to decrease by 50% after an infusion ends.

C. For first-order processes, a constant fraction rather than a constant amount is removed per unit time, and after five half-lives the process is almost 97% complete.

D. If concentration decreases by one-half in 100 minutes, the half-life is 100 minutes.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** For first-order exponential kinetics, half-life can be determined from concentration-time points plotted on a semilogarithmic graph. Cote states that graphical method directly, so A is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 176, passage ID 183299]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote states that a series of concentration-time points on a semilogarithmic graph determines half-life for first-order exponential kinetics. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 176, passage ID 183299]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Context-sensitive half-time is the time for concentration to fall 50% after an infusion, but B is an infusion concept and not the first-order graphical method. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 176, passage ID 183299]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Constant-fraction removal and near-97% completion after five half-lives describe first-order behavior, but C does not state the requested graphical method. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 745, passage ID 244508]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A one-half concentration fall in 100 minutes illustrates a half-life, but D is a single calculation and not the supported semilogarithmic method. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 176, passage ID 183299]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the graphical method and related half-life concepts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for a method, not a definition or numerical illustration.

## S02-Q177

A highly blood-soluble inhaled anesthetic is producing a slow induction. Which statement captures both the strategy that speeds induction and its safety limitation?

A. A high initial inspired partial pressure offsets uptake and accelerates induction by increasing the rate of rise of alveolar partial pressure.

B. Increasing inspired partial pressure above the maintenance target can offset high blood solubility and speed induction, but sustained high inspired partial pressure risks anesthetic overdose.

C. When blood solubility is low, minimal anesthetic must dissolve before equilibration and induction is rapid.

D. High blood solubility slows the rate at which alveolar and arterial partial pressures rise and makes induction slow.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** High blood solubility slows the alveolar/arterial rise; overpressure can speed induction but sustained high inspired partial pressure risks overdose. Stoelting states both elements, so B is correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 398, passage ID 312693]

**A — true but irrelevant:** High initial inspired pressure can offset uptake and accelerate induction, but A omits the safety limitation of sustained high inspired pressure requested in the paired statement. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 389, passage ID 312682]

**B — correct:** Correct: Stoelting describes overpressure above maintenance as a way to speed induction despite high solubility, while warning that sustained high inspired pressure causes overdose. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 398, passage ID 312693]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Low blood solubility permits rapid equilibration and induction, but C describes the opposite solubility condition from the slow induction in the stem. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 398, passage ID 312693]

**D — true but irrelevant:** High solubility does slow PA and Pa rise and induction, but D states only the problem and omits the speed-up strategy and overdose limitation. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 398, passage ID 312693]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passages directly support high/low solubility, overpressure acceleration, and overdose risk.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The answer must contain both the induction strategy and its safety limitation.

## S02-Q178

A patient with an air-filled intracranial cavity is scheduled to receive nitrous oxide. The surrounding cavity wall is noncompliant. What pressure effect should be anticipated?

A. The magnitude of nitrous-oxide-related volume or pressure increase is influenced by nitrous-oxide partial pressure, blood flow to the cavity, and duration of administration.

B. Nitrous oxide entering an air-filled cavity surrounded by a compliant wall, such as a pneumothorax, causes the gas space to expand.

C. Nitrous oxide entering an air-filled cavity surrounded by a noncompliant wall, such as the middle ear or cerebral ventricles, increases intracavitary pressure.

D. Nitrous oxide can increase middle-ear pressure when Eustachian-tube patency is compromised, and this mechanism has been attributed to tympanic-membrane rupture.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** A noncompliant air-filled cavity cannot accommodate expansion, so nitrous oxide diffusing into it raises intracavitary pressure. Stoelting identifies the middle ear and cerebral ventricles as examples, making C correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 402, passage ID 312700]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Partial pressure, blood flow, and duration influence magnitude, but A does not identify the noncompliant-wall pressure effect asked for. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 402, passage ID 312700]

**B — true but irrelevant:** A compliant cavity such as pneumothorax expands when nitrous oxide enters, but B describes the opposite wall-compliance condition from the stem. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 401, passage ID 312699]

**C — correct:** Correct: Stoelting states that nitrous oxide entering a noncompliant air-filled cavity such as the middle ear or cerebral ventricles increases intracavitary pressure. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 402, passage ID 312700]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Middle-ear pressure can rise with impaired Eustachian-tube patency and has been linked to membrane rupture, but D gives a specific consequence rather than the general cavity-wall principle. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 403, passage ID 312701]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting passages directly distinguish compliant expansion, noncompliant pressure rise, magnitude factors, and middle-ear consequences.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Wall compliance is the decisive variable in the stem.

## S02-Q179

Nitrous oxide is stopped abruptly at the end of anesthesia. Which diffusion-related change can lower arterial oxygen tension during recovery?

A. Nitrous-oxide outpouring into alveoli is greatest during the first 1–5 minutes after discontinuation at the end of anesthesia.

B. Negative middle-ear pressure may develop after nitrous oxide discontinuation and lead to serous otitis.

C. Filling the lungs with oxygen at the end of anesthesia helps prevent arterial hypoxemia from nitrous-oxide dilution of PAO2.

D. Abrupt discontinuation of nitrous oxide reverses pressure gradients, causing high-volume nitrous-oxide outpouring into alveoli that dilutes PAO2 and lowers PaO2.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Abrupt nitrous-oxide discontinuation reverses the blood-to-alveolus gradient; high-volume outpouring dilutes alveolar oxygen and lowers PaO2. Stoelting states that diffusion-hypoxia mechanism directly, so D is correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 414, passage ID 312719]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Outpouring is greatest during the first 1–5 minutes, but A gives timing and not the pressure-gradient mechanism causing hypoxemia. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 415, passage ID 312720]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Negative middle-ear pressure and serous otitis can follow discontinuation, but B is an ear consequence and not the arterial oxygen mechanism asked for. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 403, passage ID 312701]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Filling lungs with oxygen helps prevent dilution hypoxemia, but C describes prevention and not the diffusion-related change producing the fall in PaO2. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 415, passage ID 312720]

**D — correct:** Correct: Stoelting states that abrupt discontinuation reverses gradients, sends nitrous oxide into alveoli, dilutes PAO2, and decreases PaO2. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 4. Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 414, passage ID 312719]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting passages directly support diffusion hypoxia and its timing, prevention, and ear-related effects.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks the mechanism of arterial hypoxemia, not timing or preventive oxygen administration.

## S02-Q180

During total intravenous anesthesia with propofol and an opioid, which dosing and monitoring principle best supports adequate hypnosis, antinociception, hemodynamic control, and timely recovery?

A. During TIVA, profound opioid–propofol synergy permits greater doses of a short-context-sensitive-half-time opioid with reduced propofol without compromising recovery time, while dosing regimens and plasma-concentration assessment support antinociceptive, hypnotic, and hemodynamic control.

B. Combining propofol and remifentanil can produce strikingly synergistic severe respiratory depression.

C. Opioids combined with benzodiazepines have a consistent and marked negative effect on respiratory responsiveness.

D. During monitored anesthesia care, coadministered sedatives should be given in cautious incremental small boluses or titrated infusions because coadministration increases serious adverse-event risk.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** During TIVA, profound opioid–propofol synergy permits more short-context-sensitive-half-time opioid with less propofol without delaying recovery, while dosing and concentration assessment support control. Miller states that complete principle, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 22. Opioids, PDF p. 716, printed p. 599, passage ID 283797]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller describes profound opioid–propofol synergy, short context-sensitive half-time opioid selection, reduced propofol, and dosing/concentration assessment for TIVA control and recovery. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 22. Opioids, PDF p. 716, printed p. 599, passage ID 283797]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Propofol and remifentanil can produce striking synergistic respiratory depression, but B is a respiratory-risk observation and not the broader TIVA dosing/recovery principle. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 23. Intravenous Drug Delivery Systems, PDF p. 772, printed p. 1, passage ID 284078]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Opioid–benzodiazepine combinations can impair respiratory responsiveness, but C concerns another sedative combination and not propofol TIVA optimization. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 30. Monitored Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 2533, passage ID 247553]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Cautious incremental sedation reduces monitored-care adverse-event risk, but D concerns MAC administration rather than TIVA synergy and recovery. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 30. Monitored Anesthesia Care, PDF p. 2522, passage ID 247532]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly supports the TIVA strategy; frozen Barash/Miller passages distinguish related respiratory and MAC concerns.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for the integrated TIVA dosing, monitoring, hemodynamic, and recovery principle rather than an isolated respiratory effect.

## S02-Q181

A patient with limited cardiovascular reserve requires intravenous induction. Which statement best characterizes propofol's onset, recovery, and cardiovascular effects?

A. Intravenous anesthetics rapidly distribute to highly perfused, vessel-rich tissues.

B. Propofol has rapid onset and rapid recovery after a bolus because of redistribution, but induction doses can decrease systemic vascular resistance, blood pressure, and cardiac output.

C. Propofol has antiemetic properties.

D. Prolonged propofol infusion can cause propofol infusion syndrome.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient has limited cardiovascular reserve, so the relevant propofol profile combines rapid bolus recovery with dose-related hemodynamic depression. Barash describes redistribution recovery and reductions in SVR, blood pressure, and cardiac output, making B correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 19. Intravenous Anesthetics, PDF p. 1477, passage ID 245779]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Rapid distribution to vessel-rich tissues helps explain onset, but A does not address propofol’s recovery and cardiovascular effects in this limited-reserve patient. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 19. Intravenous Anesthetics, PDF p. 1466, passage ID 245761]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash describes rapid onset and redistribution recovery after bolus, while induction doses decrease SVR, blood pressure, cardiac output, and stroke volume. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 19. Intravenous Anesthetics, PDF p. 1477, passage ID 245779]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Propofol’s antiemetic property is a useful benefit, but C does not address the cardiovascular limitation central to the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 19. Intravenous Anesthetics, PDF p. 1465, passage ID 245759]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Prolonged infusion can cause PRIS, but D concerns extended infusion toxicity and not the bolus onset/recovery and hemodynamic effect asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 19. Intravenous Anesthetics, PDF p. 1466, passage ID 245761]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support propofol redistribution, rapid recovery, hemodynamic depression, antiemesis, and PRIS.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Limited cardiovascular reserve makes the dose-dependent induction hypotension clinically salient.

## S02-Q182

A child with severe cardiovascular instability requires induction, and the team is weighing hemodynamic stability against adrenal effects. Which statement best describes etomidate's tradeoff?

A. Etomidate can cause pain and myoclonic movements when injected intravenously.

B. Etomidate is indicated for induction in children with sepsis, cardiac instability, cardiomyopathy, or hypovolemic shock.

C. Etomidate provides marked cardiovascular stability but may suppress adrenal steroid synthesis after induction.

D. Ketamine is useful for children with cardiovascular instability, especially in hypovolemic states.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Etomidate is selected for cardiovascular stability, but the tradeoff is adrenal steroid suppression after induction. Cote states both features, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 84, passage ID 182823] [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 85, passage ID 182824]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Pain and myoclonus are etomidate adverse effects, but A omits the cardiovascular-stability/adrenal-suppression tradeoff asked for. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 85, passage ID 182824]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Etomidate may be indicated in these unstable children, but B gives an indication rather than the requested physiologic tradeoff. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 84, passage ID 182823]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote describes marked cardiovascular stability together with possible suppression of adrenal steroid synthesis after induction. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 84, passage ID 182823] [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 85, passage ID 182824]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Ketamine is useful in unstable or hypovolemic children, but D describes another induction agent and not etomidate’s tradeoff. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 85, passage ID 182824]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Cote passages directly support etomidate’s stability, adrenal, adverse-effect, indication, and ketamine comparisons.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question requires both benefit and limitation, not either feature alone.

## S02-Q183

A neonate requires an opioid infusion whose clearance is not dependent on immature hepatic or renal pathways. Which statement correctly describes remifentanil clearance?

A. Clearance expressed per kilogram reaches its zenith at 2 years of age and decreases subsequently.

B. Clearance is immature in infancy.

C. Allometry alone is insufficient to predict clearance in neonates and infants for most drugs.

D. Remifentanil clearance depends on nonspecific blood esterases, which are mature at birth.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The neonate needs an opioid whose clearance is independent of immature hepatic or renal pathways. Cote states that remifentanil is metabolized by nonspecific blood esterases mature at birth, so D is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 180, passage ID 183320]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Clearance per kilogram peaks at about two years, but A is a developmental clearance curve and not remifentanil’s enzyme-independent pathway. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 180, passage ID 183320]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Clearance is immature for many drugs in infancy, but B does not apply to remifentanil’s mature-at-birth blood-esterase metabolism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 180, passage ID 183320]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Allometry alone may be insufficient for many neonatal drugs, but C is a general modeling limitation and not the specific remifentanil clearance mechanism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 180, passage ID 183320]

**D — correct:** Correct: Cote states that remifentanil depends on nonspecific blood esterases, which are mature at birth. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 180, passage ID 183320]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states remifentanil’s esterase metabolism and contrasts it with general pediatric clearance.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem specifically asks for an organ-independent clearance mechanism.

## S02-Q184

A patient has moderate postoperative pain in the recovery unit. Which plan best applies an opioid-sparing multimodal analgesia strategy while maintaining adequate analgesia?

A. Postoperative pain management should focus on multimodal analgesia using nonopioid analgesics, neuraxial or regional techniques, and opioids titrated to effect.

B. Patient-controlled epidural or intravenous analgesia may benefit patients with the developmental capability to use those devices.

C. The Society for Pediatric Anesthesia supports PCA as a safe and efficacious pediatric opioid-delivery option.

D. Use no analgesic-delivery plan because no universal pain-measurement tool exists.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Moderate PACU pain calls for a multimodal plan combining nonopioids, neuraxial/regional techniques, and titrated opioids. Barash states that complete approach, so A is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 43. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3781, passage ID 249789]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash recommends PACU multimodal analgesia with nonopioids, neuraxial/regional techniques, and opioids titrated to effect. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 43. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3781, passage ID 249789]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Patient-controlled techniques may benefit developmentally capable patients, but B is a delivery option and not the complete multimodal PACU plan requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 43. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3781, passage ID 249789]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Pediatric PCA is supported as safe and efficacious, but C does not address the broader moderate-pain multimodal strategy. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 43. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3781, passage ID 249789]

**D — false:** Lack of one universal pain tool does not justify withholding analgesia; D does not follow Barash’s explicit multimodal PACU recommendation. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 43. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3781, passage ID 249789]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly supports the multimodal plan and related PCA and measurement points.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The absence of a universal measurement tool does not remove the need for a structured analgesic plan.

## S02-Q195

An epidural is planned for surgery requiring a defined thoracic sensory level while limiting excessive cephalad spread. Which dose-and-site principle should guide placement?

A. Hyperbaric local anesthetic flows with gravity toward dependent spinal areas.

B. Choose epidural dose and insertion site to control block spread: small 5–10 mL doses produce a band around the injection site, whereas lumbar injection spreads more cephalad than caudad, so the planned surgical level must be selected accordingly.

C. A smaller epidural dose produces relatively greater spread per dermatome than a larger dose.

D. Height, weight, BMI, and vertebral-column length have no clinically significant effect on subarachnoid spread.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The planned thoracic sensory level must be controlled by epidural dose and insertion site. Barash states that 5–10 mL produces a band around the site and lumbar injection spreads more cephalad, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2844, passage ID 248099] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2844, passage ID 248098]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Gravity-dependent spread describes hyperbaric spinal anesthetic, but A is not the epidural dose/site principle in this stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2837, passage ID 248090]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash identifies patient variables, dose, and injection site as determinants; small 5–10 mL doses form a band and lumbar injection spreads more cephalad than caudad. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2844, passage ID 248098] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2844, passage ID 248099]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Smaller doses produce greater spread per dermatome, but C gives a dose-efficiency relationship and not the site-plus-dose level-control principle. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2844, passage ID 248099]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Height, weight, BMI, and vertebral length may not significantly affect subarachnoid spread, but D concerns spinal rather than epidural placement and does not answer the planned level. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2841, passage ID 248094]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support epidural site/dose spread and distinguish spinal gravity and patient-variable statements.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The operation’s thoracic level makes epidural insertion site and spread direction decisive.

## S02-Q196

According to the current Thrombosis Canada Clinical Guide: DOACs: Perioperative Management, a patient taking rivaroxaban is scheduled for spinal or epidural anesthesia. Which interruption schedule applies to this high-bleed-risk procedure?

A. Resume therapeutic-dose rivaroxaban cautiously after the procedure because the dose is therapeutic.

B. Take the last rivaroxaban dose 2 days before the procedure, skipping 1 dose.

C. Take the last rivaroxaban dose 3 days before the procedure, skipping 2 doses.

D. Do not interrupt rivaroxaban for the procedure because neuraxial anesthesia is a minimal-bleed-risk intervention.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Spinal or epidural anesthesia is a high-bleed-risk neuraxial procedure. Newly retrieved Lange textbook evidence recommends stopping rivaroxaban 72 hours before neuraxial block, equivalent to 3 days and skipping 2 doses, so C is correct. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 45 Spinal, Epidural, & Caudal Blocks, PDF p. 1810, passage ID 279169]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Cautious therapeutic-dose resumption is a postoperative issue, but A does not state the pre-neuraxial interruption interval asked for. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 45 Spinal, Epidural, & Caudal Blocks, PDF p. 1810, passage ID 279169]

**B — wrong timing or magnitude:** Two days and one skipped dose is the low/moderate-bleed-risk schedule, not the 72-hour high-risk neuraxial interval in the stem. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 45 Spinal, Epidural, & Caudal Blocks, PDF p. 1810, passage ID 279169]

**C — correct:** Correct: Lange states rivaroxaban should be discontinued 72 hours before neuraxial block, which is 3 days and skipping 2 doses. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 45 Spinal, Epidural, & Caudal Blocks, PDF p. 1810, passage ID 279169]

**D — false:** Neuraxial anesthesia is specifically treated as high bleed risk, so D does not fit the required interruption schedule. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 45 Spinal, Epidural, & Caudal Blocks, PDF p. 1810, passage ID 279169]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The frozen evidence was living-web guidance; Lange 7e directly supports the 72-hour neuraxial interruption.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence was non-textbook guidance; new Lange evidence is used exclusively for the rewrite.

## S02-Q197

Immediately after an epidural injection, a patient reports a metallic taste and tinnitus and then develops neurologic or cardiovascular deterioration. Which complication best explains this presentation?

A. Expected prevention of local-anesthetic toxicity through incremental injection and a marker-containing test dose.

B. Postdural-puncture headache after lumbar puncture, which is less frequent with a smaller-gauge needle.

C. High spinal block after an epidural dose enters the intrathecal space, causing respiratory compromise and unconsciousness.

D. Neuraxial local-anesthetic systemic toxicity from epidural absorption or unrecognized intravascular injection.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Metallic taste and tinnitus immediately after epidural injection are early local-anesthetic toxicity symptoms, with possible neurologic/cardiovascular deterioration. Barash attributes neuraxial LAST to epidural absorption or unrecognized intravascular injection, so D is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2866, passage ID 248130]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Incremental injection and a marker-containing test dose reduce toxicity risk, but A describes prevention rather than the toxicity already presenting. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2866, passage ID 248130]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Postdural-puncture headache follows lumbar puncture and does not match immediate tinnitus/metallic taste with neurologic or cardiovascular decline. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 31. Ambulatory Anesthesia, PDF p. 2569, passage ID 247624]

**C — true but irrelevant:** An intrathecal epidural dose can cause high spinal respiratory compromise, but C does not explain the classic early LAST symptoms after epidural injection. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 45. Anesthesia and Obesity, PDF p. 3884, passage ID 249987]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash states that neuraxial LAST can follow epidural absorption or unrecognized intravascular injection and range from tinnitus/metallic taste to seizures and arrest. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2866, passage ID 248130]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly supports LAST mechanism and symptoms and distinguishes prevention, headache, and high-spinal alternatives.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Immediate sensory symptoms followed by neurologic/cardiovascular decline are the LAST discriminator.

## S02-Q198

Three days after prolonged epidural catheterization, a patient develops severe localized backache, fever, and new leg weakness. Which evaluation and treatment pathway is most appropriate?

A. Suspect epidural abscess, obtain prompt MRI, and arrange urgent decompression/laminectomy when neurologic signs are present, with directed antibiotics.

B. Start directed antibiotics for a routine 4–6 weeks without tailoring therapy or considering neurologic decompression.

C. Treat the presentation as a post-neuraxial hematoma without prioritizing infectious evaluation or MRI.

D. Identify Staphylococcus aureus as the likely organism and defer imaging or decompression until cultures are finalized.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Severe backache, fever, and new weakness after prolonged epidural catheterization suggest epidural abscess. Chestnut recommends prompt MRI, directed antibiotics, and urgent laminectomy when neurologic signs occur, so A is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 830, printed p. 763, passage ID 273916] [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 830, printed p. 763, passage ID 273917] [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 831, printed p. 764, passage ID 273919]

**A — correct:** Correct: Chestnut links prolonged catheterization, severe backache, fever, MRI diagnosis, neurologic progression, prompt laminectomy, and directed antibiotics. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 830, printed p. 763, passage ID 273916] [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 830, printed p. 763, passage ID 273917] [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 831, printed p. 764, passage ID 273919]

**B — false:** Antibiotics are mandatory, but duration is case-by-case and usually 4–6 weeks without complications; B incorrectly omits tailoring and neurologic decompression. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 831, printed p. 764, passage ID 273919]

**C — wrong condition:** Fever and progressive weakness favor infectious abscess; treating this as hematoma without MRI or infectious evaluation does not fit the stem. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 830, printed p. 763, passage ID 273917]

**D — incomplete:** S. aureus is common, but D delays MRI and decompression until cultures, omitting the urgent neurologic pathway required by the new weakness. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 31 Neurologic Complications of Pregnancy and Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 830, printed p. 763, passage ID 273917]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Chestnut passages directly support diagnosis, imaging, decompression, antibiotics, organism, and duration.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- New neurologic weakness changes the urgency from diagnostic evaluation alone to prompt decompression.

## S02-Q199

Before needle advancement for a peripheral nerve block, which complete practice combines a systematic ultrasound survey with color-Doppler vessel identification?

A. Small mono-fascicular nerves may be difficult to identify and can resemble small blood vessels.

B. Systematically scan the area with ultrasound and use color Doppler to identify vascular structures before needle advancement.

C. After negative aspiration, local-anesthetic spread around the brachial plexus confirms appropriate needle placement.

D. Power Doppler is more sensitive and less angle dependent than traditional color Doppler.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Before advancing a block needle, systematic ultrasound and color Doppler identify vessels and reduce vascular-puncture risk. Miller states that color Doppler should identify vascular structures, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1581, printed p. 1315, passage ID 287931]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Small nerves can resemble vessels, but A is an identification challenge and not the complete pre-needle Doppler survey requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1573, printed p. 1307, passage ID 287908]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller recommends systematic ultrasound scanning and color Doppler identification of vascular structures before needle advancement. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1581, printed p. 1315, passage ID 287931]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Spread after negative aspiration can confirm placement, but C is a post-injection check and does not prevent encountering an unrecognized vessel before advancement. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1581, printed p. 1315, passage ID 287931]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Power Doppler is more sensitive and less angle-dependent, but D compares Doppler technologies and does not state the systematic vessel survey. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1573, printed p. 1307, passage ID 287907]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller passages directly support color Doppler vessel identification, systematic scanning, nerve-vessel confusion, and post-aspiration confirmation.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The temporal phrase ‘before needle advancement’ makes vessel mapping the key safety action.

## S02-Q200

A patient with limited respiratory reserve needs analgesia for shoulder surgery, and preservation of postoperative hemidiaphragmatic function is important. Which regional strategy is best supported?

A. Choose supraclavicular block as fully phrenic-sparing because its phrenic palsy incidence is lower than interscalene block.

B. Use posterior suprascapular plus axillary nerve block for major shoulder surgery because it provides superior analgesia to interscalene block.
