C. Use a low-volume superior trunk block for effective analgesia with preserved hemidiaphragmatic function; a selective anterior suprascapular block is a noninferior alternative to interscalene block for ambulatory arthroscopy.

D. Choose traditional interscalene block because its historical ipsilateral diaphragmatic paresis rate was 100%.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Limited respiratory reserve makes hemidiaphragmatic preservation central. Barash states that low-volume superior-trunk block preserves function with effective analgesia and that anterior suprascapular block is noninferior for ambulatory arthroscopy, so C is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4375, passage ID 250901]

**A — false:** Supraclavicular block has clinically significant phrenic palsy and may need larger volumes, so A does not support calling it fully phrenic-sparing. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4375, passage ID 250901]

**B — false:** Posterior suprascapular plus axillary block is inferior to interscalene for major shoulder analgesia, so B does not provide the claimed superior strategy. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4375, passage ID 250902]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash describes low-volume superior-trunk block as effective while preserving hemidiaphragmatic function and anterior suprascapular block as noninferior to interscalene for ambulatory arthroscopy. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4375, passage ID 250901]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Historical interscalene paresis was 100%, but D ignores the stem’s need to preserve postoperative hemidiaphragmatic function and is not the best current strategy. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4374, passage ID 250899]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support phrenic risk, superior-trunk preservation, suprascapular alternatives, and interscalene history.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Respiratory reserve makes phrenic/hemidiaphragmatic preservation the decisive clinical discriminator.

## S02-Q201

Which statement best summarizes how ultrasound improves the safety and performance of a pediatric or adult peripheral nerve block?

A. Ultrasound-guided peripheral nerve blocks can reduce local-anesthetic doses and the number of punctures.

B. Ultrasound guidance can shorten block onset time and prolong sensory-block duration.

C. Ultrasound guidance gradually replaces landmark techniques and nerve-stimulation localization approaches in pediatric regional anesthesia.

D. Ultrasound-guided regional techniques visualize targeted nerves and spaces and the spread of injected local anesthetic, improving block safety; block performance also depends on understanding the relationships among nerves, bone, and vessels.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the broad ultrasound-guided block safety/performance summary. Miller identifies visualization of nerves, spaces, injectate spread, improved safety, and anatomic nerve-bone-vessel relationships, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 74. Regional Anesthesia in Children, PDF p. 2813, printed p. 74, passage ID 293948]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Ultrasound can reduce dose and punctures, but A gives selected benefits and not the complete visualization/anatomy/safety summary requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 74. Regional Anesthesia in Children, PDF p. 2813, printed p. 74, passage ID 293948]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Shorter onset and longer sensory duration may occur, but B is an outcome comparison and not the broad block-performance principle asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 74. Regional Anesthesia in Children, PDF p. 2813, printed p. 74, passage ID 293948]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Ultrasound gradually replaces older localization approaches, but C describes adoption and not how visualization and anatomic understanding improve safety and performance. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 74. Regional Anesthesia in Children, PDF p. 2813, printed p. 74, passage ID 293948]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller states that ultrasound visualizes targeted nerves/spaces and local-anesthetic spread, improves safety, and requires understanding nerve, bone, and vessel relationships. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 74. Regional Anesthesia in Children, PDF p. 2813, printed p. 74, passage ID 293948]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly supports the complete ultrasound safety/performance summary and its component benefits.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The keyed option integrates visualization with anatomic understanding rather than listing one isolated ultrasound benefit.

## S02-Q202

A peripheral block is incomplete, and the ultrasound image shows a small structure that could be either the target nerve or a nearby artery. Which troubleshooting plan is most appropriate before further injection?

A. When a block is incomplete, rescan dynamically to identify the nerve, use color Doppler because nearby arteries can mimic it, and target injectate immediately before the relevant nerve branch.

B. Generally, 10–15 mL of local anesthetic is used for this block, although volumes as low as 5 mL can be successful.

C. At the traditional C6 interscalene level, ipsilateral phrenic nerve block and diaphragmatic paresis are inevitable.

D. After negative aspiration, local-anesthetic spread around the brachial plexus confirms appropriate needle placement.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** An incomplete block with a structure that may be nerve or artery requires dynamic rescanning, color Doppler, and targeted injectate placement. Miller gives that troubleshooting sequence, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1581, printed p. 1315, passage ID 287933]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller advises dynamic identification of the nerve, color Doppler because arteries can mimic it, and injectate targeting immediately before the relevant branch. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1581, printed p. 1315, passage ID 287933]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Typical 10–15 mL volume and possible 5 mL success are dosing details, but B does not resolve whether the imaged structure is nerve or artery. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1581, printed p. 1315, passage ID 287931]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Phrenic block at the traditional C6 level is an interscalene complication, but C does not troubleshoot the uncertain structure or incomplete block. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1581, printed p. 1315, passage ID 287931]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Post-aspiration spread can confirm placement, but D occurs after the key vessel-identification problem and does not prevent injection into a mistaken artery. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 42. Peripheral Nerve Blocks and Ultrasound Guidance for Regional Anesthesia, PDF p. 1581, printed p. 1315, passage ID 287931]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly supplies dynamic scanning, Doppler differentiation, volume, phrenic, and spread details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The unresolved nerve-versus-artery identification makes color Doppler and rescanning the priority.

## S02-Q203

After an epidural test dose containing approximately 15 mcg epinephrine, which hemodynamic response is expected if the catheter is intravascular?

A. Lower-extremity numbness and motor block without a high spinal.

B. A slight tachycardic or hypertensive response.

C. No change while dosing slowly and aspirating intermittently.

D. Dizziness, buzzing in the ears, or lip numbness from local anesthetic.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** An epidural test dose containing epinephrine should produce a slight tachycardic or hypertensive response if the catheter is intravascular. Miller states that response directly, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Lower-extremity numbness and motor block without high spinal suggest intrathecal test-dose placement, but A is not the intravascular response asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that approximately 15 mcg epinephrine in the test dose produces slight tachycardia and/or hypertension when placement is intravascular. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**C — false:** A test dose is intended to reveal abnormal responses; no change does not identify intravascular placement, so C does not provide the expected response. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Dizziness, tinnitus, or lip numbness reflects intravascular local-anesthetic effect, but the question asks specifically for the epinephrine hemodynamic response. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly distinguishes intravascular epinephrine, intrathecal local anesthetic, and safety surveillance responses.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The approximately 15-mcg epinephrine component makes the expected hemodynamic signal the discriminator.

## S02-Q213

During controlled ventilation, the PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient widens while airway dead space is otherwise stable. Which interpretation is supported?

A. Use the inverse PaCO2–alveolar-ventilation relationship to interpret the widening gradient.

B. Substitute PETCO2 for PaCO2 in the Bohr equation because alveolar dead space is very small.

C. The PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient is a useful trend for detecting increased alveolar dead space.

D. Apply the derived VD/VT to minute ventilation or a single breath.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** With stable airway dead space, a widening PaCO2–PETCO2 gradient is a useful trend for increased alveolar dead space. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1766, passage ID 314687]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The ventilation equation explains PaCO2 but does not interpret the widening gradient. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1766, passage ID 314687]

**B — wrong condition:** PETCO2 substitution applies to healthy spontaneous breathing, not this case. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1766, passage ID 314687]

**C — correct:** Correct: Stoelting identifies the gradient as a trend for increased alveolar dead space. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1766, passage ID 314687]

**D — true but irrelevant:** VD/VT application does not interpret this changing gradient. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1766, passage ID 314687]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting passage supports gradient interpretation.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Stable airway dead space makes alveolar dead space relevant.

## S02-Q214

During anesthesia, which integrated profile correctly relates ventilation, wasted ventilation, hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, and arterial oxygenation?

A. Ventilation to adequately ventilated areas with inadequate blood flow is wasted or dead-space ventilation.

B. Chronic hypoxemia increases red-cell mass to improve oxygen-carrying capacity.

C. VD/VT identifies adequately ventilated areas with inadequate or absent pulmonary blood flow.

D. PaCO2 reflects alveolar ventilation; VD/VT reflects wasted ventilation; and hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction diverts blood from hypoxic alveoli to help preserve V/Q matching and oxygenation.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The integrated profile links PaCO2 with alveolar ventilation, VD/VT with wasted ventilation, and HPV with diversion from hypoxic alveoli to preserve oxygenation. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 54, printed p. 43, passage ID 232476]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Wasted ventilation is only one component of the requested profile. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 54, printed p. 43, passage ID 232476]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Red-cell mass is an adaptation, not the requested ventilation/HPV integration. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 54, printed p. 43, passage ID 232476]

**C — incomplete:** VD/VT is correct but C omits PaCO2 and HPV. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 54, printed p. 43, passage ID 232476]

**D — correct:** Correct: Stoelting supports all linked relationships and oxygenation consequence. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 54, printed p. 43, passage ID 232476]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting passage supports each component.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for integrated physiology.

## S02-Q215

A patient becomes hypoxemic during anesthesia after a reduction in inspired oxygen. Which framework best integrates the major transport mechanisms that should be considered?

A. Consider low inspired PO2 or hypoventilation lowering PAO2, V/Q mismatch, right-to-left shunt, and diffusion limitation; hypoventilation, V/Q mismatch, and shunt account for most perioperative cases.

B. Classify ventilation reaching adequately ventilated but poorly perfused regions as wasted dead-space ventilation.

C. Attribute hypoxemia from a thickened alveolar-capillary barrier, such as interstitial lung disease, to diffusion limitation alone.

D. Use VD/VT as the measure of carbon-dioxide transfer efficiency.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Perioperative hypoxemia may result from low inspired PO2 or hypoventilation, V/Q mismatch, shunt, or diffusion limitation; the first three explain most cases. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1398, printed p. 1168, passage ID 287038] [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1398, printed p. 1168, passage ID 287040] [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 54, printed p. 43, passage ID 232476]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller lists four mechanisms and identifies the first three as most perioperative. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1398, printed p. 1168, passage ID 287038] [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1398, printed p. 1168, passage ID 287040]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Wasted dead-space ventilation is valid but not the broad hypoxemia framework. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 54, printed p. 43, passage ID 232476]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Diffusion limitation is one mechanism, not the complete majority framework. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1398, printed p. 1168, passage ID 287040]

**D — true but irrelevant:** VD/VT concerns CO2-transfer efficiency, not the complete oxygenation differential. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 54, printed p. 43, passage ID 232476]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller and Stoelting passages support the framework.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The answer integrates four mechanisms.

## S02-Q216

A patient with low hemoglobin and reduced cardiac output has impaired systemic oxygen delivery. Which Fick-principle relationships should guide interpretation of delivery and extraction?

A. Recognize that hemorrhage lowers delivery through both reduced cardiac output and reduced arterial oxygen content.

B. DO2 = CO × CaO2; VO2 = CO × (CaO2 − CvO2); and oxygen extraction is (CaO2 − CvO2)/CaO2.

C. Calculate CaO2 as 1.36 × hemoglobin × SaO2 plus PaO2 × 0.003.

D. Calculate CvO2 as 1.36 × hemoglobin × SvO2 plus PvO2 × 0.003.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The Fick framework is DO2=CO×CaO2, VO2=CO×(CaO2−CvO2), with extraction (CaO2−CvO2)/CaO2. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1297, passage ID 245451]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Hemorrhage lowers output and arterial content, but A is a consequence, not the equations. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 47. Physiology and Pharmacology of Resuscitation, PDF p. 2778, passage ID 316232]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash provides the delivery, consumption, and extraction relationships. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1297, passage ID 245451]

**C — true but irrelevant:** CaO2 is one component, not the complete Fick framework. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1297, passage ID 245451]

**D — true but irrelevant:** CvO2 is one component, not the complete Fick framework. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 17. Hemostasis and Transfusion Medicine, PDF p. 1297, passage ID 245451]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash and Stoelting passages support equations and consequence.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for the integrated equation set.

## S02-Q217

A patient with restrictive lung disease has low compliance and persistent hypoxemia during mechanical ventilation. Which ventilator strategy is best supported?

A. Delay extubation for tachypnea alone even when gas exchange and other assessments are satisfactory.

B. Expect rapid, shallow breathing during weaning as the primary ventilator adjustment.

C. Use low tidal volumes, limit plateau pressure below 30 cm H2O, and carefully titrate PEEP with an inspiratory pause to optimize compliance.

D. Use mechanical ventilation and PEEP whenever persistent hypoxemia occurs, without further compliance or pressure assessment.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Restrictive low compliance with hypoxemia calls for low tidal volumes, plateau pressure below 30 cm H2O, and carefully titrated PEEP with inspiratory pause. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 51, passage ID 232461]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Tachypnea alone should not delay extubation, but A is a weaning criterion. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 51, passage ID 232461]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Rapid shallow breathing is a weaning pattern, not the adjustment. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 51, passage ID 232461]

**C — correct:** Correct: Stoelting recommends low tidal volume, plateau limitation, and PEEP titration. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 51, passage ID 232461]

**D — incomplete:** Ventilation and PEEP may be needed, but D omits compliance and pressure titration. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 51, passage ID 232461]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting passage supports the strategy.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Low compliance requires limits plus PEEP titration.

## S02-Q218

During volume-control ventilation, peak airway pressure rises abruptly while plateau pressure is unchanged. Which pressure relationship best localizes the problem?

A. Pressure-control ventilation has no discernible peak-to-plateau difference, making static and dynamic compliance difficult to distinguish.

B. Static compliance driving pressure is plateau pressure minus PEEP.

C. Static compliance reflects the actual distending pressure in the patient’s alveoli.

D. The peak-minus-plateau pressure difference represents gas-flow resistance, while plateau pressure reflects alveolar distending pressure and static compliance.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** With volume control, unchanged plateau and rising peak indicate increased resistive pressure; peak-minus-plateau is flow resistance while plateau reflects alveolar distending pressure/static compliance. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1747, passage ID 314656] [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1746, passage ID 314655]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Pressure control can obscure the difference, but A is another mode. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1746, passage ID 314655]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Plateau minus PEEP is static driving pressure, but B omits resistance. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1746, passage ID 314655]

**C — incomplete:** Static compliance reflects alveolar pressure, but C omits the resistive change. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1746, passage ID 314655]

**D — correct:** Correct: Stoelting identifies peak-minus-plateau as resistance and plateau as alveolar/static-compliance information. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1747, passage ID 314656] [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1746, passage ID 314655]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting passages support each relationship.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Unchanged plateau with rising peak localizes resistance.

## S02-Q219

A mechanically ventilated patient’s expiratory flow remains above zero when the next breath begins. Which adjustment best permits complete lung emptying?

A. Recognize auto-PEEP and increase expiratory time while reducing tidal volume or respiratory rate.

B. Measure auto-PEEP with end-expiratory flow interruption on an intensive-care ventilator.

C. Use the proportionality of auto-PEEP to tidal volume and its inverse relation to expiratory time as the adjustment itself.

D. Recognize that auto-PEEP increases end-expiratory volume and risks hyperinflation and reduced venous return.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Persistent end-expiratory flow identifies auto-PEEP; increasing expiratory time and reducing tidal volume or respiratory rate permits complete emptying. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287088] [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287087]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller recommends more expiratory time and lower tidal volume or rate after detecting auto-PEEP. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287088] [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287087]

**B — true but irrelevant:** End-expiratory interruption measures auto-PEEP, but B is measurement, not adjustment. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1799, passage ID 314747]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Proportionality explains auto-PEEP, but C does not state the practical adjustment. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 24. Gas Exchange, PDF p. 1799, passage ID 314747]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Hyperinflation and reduced venous return are consequences, not the emptying intervention. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 37. Respiratory Monitoring, PDF p. 1409, printed p. 1179, passage ID 287087]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller and Stoelting passages support detection, adjustment, measurement, and consequences.

- The question asks how to permit emptying.

## S02-Q220

A patient with severe COPD is scheduled for major surgery. Which integrated profile best captures the pulmonary physiology and perioperative risk that should shape anesthetic planning?

A. Use PFT-predicted values and GOLD criteria to categorize COPD severity as the complete perioperative risk assessment.

B. Expect air-space enlargement with alveolar-wall destruction, air trapping and hyperinflation, impaired mechanics and gas exchange, and increased risk of postoperative pneumonia, prolonged ventilation, and respiratory failure.

C. Focus on possible right-ventricular dysfunction or cor pulmonale as the defining COPD physiology and risk.

D. Consider patients who can climb three flights of stairs to have reduced perioperative risk regardless of COPD physiology.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Severe COPD combines air-space destruction, air trapping/hyperinflation, impaired mechanics/gas exchange, and increased postoperative pulmonary risk. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 38. Anesthesia for Thoracic Surgery, PDF p. 3139, passage ID 248618]

**A — true but irrelevant:** PFT/GOLD categories describe severity, but A is not the complete physiology/risk profile. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 38. Anesthesia for Thoracic Surgery, PDF p. 3139, passage ID 248618]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash links destruction/hyperinflation with pneumonia, prolonged ventilation, and respiratory failure. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 38. Anesthesia for Thoracic Surgery, PDF p. 3139, passage ID 248618]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Cor pulmonale can occur, but C is one consequence rather than the integrated profile. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 38. Anesthesia for Thoracic Surgery, PDF p. 3139, passage ID 248618]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Exercise capacity may lower risk, but D cannot override severe COPD physiology. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 38. Anesthesia for Thoracic Surgery, PDF p. 3139, passage ID 248618]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passage supports the integrated profile.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The answer includes physiology and perioperative consequences.

## S02-Q006

An airway team compares direct and video laryngoscopy for a patient whose laryngeal view has previously been difficult. Which view-related benefit is supported for video laryngoscopy?

A. A video laryngoscope may show the glottis well while endotracheal-tube passage remains difficult.

B. Adequate mouth opening is required for video-laryngoscope use.

C. Video laryngoscopy can improve Cormack and Lehane grade 1–2 views.

D. Video laryngoscopy can serve as a rescue technique in a difficult airway.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks specifically for a view-related benefit of video laryngoscopy in a patient with a previously difficult laryngeal view. The cited passage directly identifies improvement in Cormack and Lehane grade 1–2 views, making C the best answer. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 774, printed p. 707, passage ID 273668]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A good glottic view can still be accompanied by difficult tube passage, but that is a limitation rather than the view-related benefit requested. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 774, printed p. 707, passage ID 273668]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Adequate mouth opening is a use requirement, but it does not state the supported improvement in laryngeal view asked for by the stem. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 774, printed p. 707, passage ID 273668]

**C — correct:** Correct: the textbook passage directly identifies improvement in Cormack and Lehane grade 1–2 views as the view-related benefit. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 774, printed p. 707, passage ID 273668]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Video laryngoscopy can serve as a rescue technique, but rescue utility is broader than the specific view improvement asked for in this stem. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 774, printed p. 707, passage ID 273668]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen textbook passage directly supports the view improvement and separately supports the true but less direct alternatives.

- Original rationale did not distinguish a view benefit from tube-delivery difficulty, mouth-opening requirements, or rescue utility.

## S02-Q007

During adult tracheal-tube exchange, where should the distal tip of an airway exchange catheter ideally be positioned?

A. In a mainstem bronchus.

B. At the carina.

C. In the proximal trachea just below the vocal cords.

D. In the midtrachea.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks for the ideal distal-tip location during adult tracheal-tube exchange. The cited passage states that an airway exchange catheter is ideally positioned in the midtrachea, so D directly matches the requested location.
