**B — false:** The carina is not the midtracheal location identified as ideal for the airway exchange catheter distal tip. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 784, printed p. 717, passage ID 273713]

**C — false:** The proximal trachea just below the cords is not the midtracheal position specified by the textbook. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 784, printed p. 717, passage ID 273713]

**D — correct:** Correct: the distal tip of the airway exchange catheter is ideally positioned in the midtrachea. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 29 The Difficult Airway, PDF p. 784, printed p. 717, passage ID 273713]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The ideal midtracheal location is directly stated in the frozen textbook passage.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale repeated the midtracheal statement without explicitly excluding each alternate location.

## S02-Q008

Under the Difficult Airway Society (DAS) 2025 guidelines, a second-generation supraglottic airway provides satisfactory ventilation after failed intubation. The operation is essential, but tracheal intubation itself is not. What may be considered?

A. Continue the operation using the supraglottic airway device.

B. Perform blind tracheal intubation through the supraglottic airway.

C. Discontinue anaesthesia and wake the patient because proceeding is not essential.

D. Make one flexible-bronchoscope intubation attempt through the supraglottic airway.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** evidence_limited

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The frozen packet contains the keyed and exclusion statements in a DAS 2025 guideline record, while its attached textbook records address general airway devices but do not establish the named guideline’s Plan-A recommendation. Under the textbook-only rule, the keyed continuation option cannot be supported.

**A — evidence limited:** The keyed option is supported only by the excluded guideline record; no attached textbook quote establishes this named Plan-A recommendation.

**B — evidence limited:** The attached textbook records do not establish the guideline-specific prohibition on blind intubation through the supraglottic airway.

**C — evidence limited:** The attached textbook records do not establish the guideline-specific wake-versus-proceed decision for the stated failed-intubation scenario.

**D — evidence limited:** The attached textbook records do not establish the guideline-specific single flexible-bronchoscope attempt described in this option.

**Evidence sufficiency:** Insufficient under textbook-only authority. The frozen correct and distractor claims rely on DAS 2025 guideline records; the attached textbook quotes are general airway statements and do not entail the guideline-specific options.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence mixes textbook and guideline records.
- Do not use the guideline records as textbook citations; route this item through an authorized guideline workflow or retrieve direct textbook support.

## S02-Q009

A patient with a previously difficult airway is fully responsive and maintaining spontaneous ventilation before planned tube removal. Which physiologic rationale supports an awake extubation approach?

A. Deep extubation avoids coughing and adverse hemodynamic effects but risks obstruction and hypoventilation.

B. An awake patient more easily maintains a patent airway because pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes recover.

C. An extubation plan should include a reintubation plan if the airway cannot be maintained.

D. Extubation during a light stage-II plane increases laryngospasm and other airway complications.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient is fully responsive and spontaneously ventilating before planned tube removal, and the question asks for the physiologic rationale for awake extubation. The cited passage directly attributes easier maintenance of a patent airway to recovery of awake pharyngeal muscle tone and airway reflexes, so B is the best answer. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 40. Airway Management in the Adult, PDF p. 1512, printed p. 1263, passage ID 287594]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Deep extubation can reduce coughing and adverse hemodynamic effects but risks obstruction and hypoventilation; it does not provide the awake-airway rationale requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 40. Airway Management in the Adult, PDF p. 1512, printed p. 1263, passage ID 287594]

**B — correct:** Correct: recovery of awake pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes makes maintenance of a patent airway easier in the stated awake-extubation plan. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 40. Airway Management in the Adult, PDF p. 1512, printed p. 1263, passage ID 287594]

**C — true but irrelevant:** A preemptive reintubation plan is required when airway maintenance may fail, but it is a planning requirement rather than the physiologic reason awake extubation protects airway patency. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 40. Airway Management in the Adult, PDF p. 1512, printed p. 1263, passage ID 287594]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Light stage-II extubation increases laryngospasm and other complications, but that warning does not explain the awake patient’s recovered tone and reflexes. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 40. Airway Management in the Adult, PDF p. 1512, printed p. 1263, passage ID 287594]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The key physiologic mechanism and the true but differently purposed alternatives are all directly supported.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not connect awake pharyngeal tone/reflex recovery to the stem’s awake-extubation rationale.

## S02-Q010

Immediately after extubation, jaw thrust with CPAP has been tried but the upper airway remains obstructed. What next noninvasive airway adjunct is supported before tracheal reintubation?

A. Delay airway opening until the cause of obstruction has been identified and treated.

B. Proceed immediately to tracheal reintubation without another noninvasive airway-opening measure.

C. Insert an oral, nasal, or laryngeal mask airway.

D. Continue jaw thrust with CPAP as the only possible noninvasive intervention.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Jaw thrust with CPAP has already failed in the stem. Miller’s postanesthesia-care sequence states that noninvasive measures precede reintubation and that, if CPAP is not effective, an oral, nasal, or laryngeal mask airway can be inserted; C is therefore the supported next adjunct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 76. The Postanesthesia Care Unit, PDF p. 2968, printed p. 2489, passage ID 294726]

**A — wrong timing or magnitude:** Miller places identifying and treating the cause after the airway has been opened and ventilation ensured; delaying airway opening is not the next step in this obstructed patient. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 76. The Postanesthesia Care Unit, PDF p. 2968, printed p. 2489, passage ID 294726]

**B — wrong timing or magnitude:** The cited sequence attempts noninvasive airway-opening measures before tracheal reintubation, but immediate reintubation skips the supported next adjunct after failed CPAP. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 76. The Postanesthesia Care Unit, PDF p. 2968, printed p. 2489, passage ID 294726]

**C — correct:** Correct: after CPAP is ineffective, Miller specifically supports inserting an oral, nasal, or laryngeal mask airway before reintubation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 76. The Postanesthesia Care Unit, PDF p. 2968, printed p. 2489, passage ID 294726]

**D — wrong timing or magnitude:** Jaw thrust with CPAP is often enough for obstruction from decreased pharyngeal tone, but the stem states that this maneuver has already been tried and remains ineffective. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 76. The Postanesthesia Care Unit, PDF p. 2968, printed p. 2489, passage ID 294726]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen passage gives the exact sequence and the condition—CPAP ineffective—that selects C.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Original rationale did not apply the failed-CPAP condition to the next-step sequence and included irrelevant evidence records.

## S02-Q021

A patient with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy develops hypotension after induction. Which hemodynamic strategy best addresses the obstructive physiology?

A. For mitral stenosis, maintain heart rate at the low end of normal and avoid and treat tachycardia.

B. For aortic stenosis, maintain sinus rhythm, a normal heart rate, and adequate preload, afterload, and contractility.

C. For coronary artery disease, control heart rate, avoid hypo- and hypervolemia, and assure adequate perfusion pressure.

D. Increase afterload and decrease contractility, treating hypotension aggressively with an alpha-adrenergic agonist.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem identifies hypertrophic obstructive physiology and asks how to correct the post-induction hypotension. Increasing afterload and reducing contractility oppose dynamic obstruction, and the cited passage specifically directs aggressive alpha-adrenergic treatment, so D is the stem-matched strategy. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3303, passage ID 248912]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A low-normal heart rate and avoidance of tachycardia are goals for mitral stenosis, but A addresses a different lesion rather than the obstructive hypertrophic physiology in this stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3296, passage ID 248901]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Maintaining rhythm, heart rate, preload, afterload, and contractility is appropriate for aortic stenosis, but B does not provide the hypertrophic-obstruction response asked for here. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 6 Valvular heart disease, PDF p. 131, passage ID 232812]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Heart-rate control, avoidance of abnormal volume, and perfusion pressure are coronary-disease goals, but C describes coronary disease rather than treating dynamic outflow obstruction. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3282, passage ID 248879]

**D — correct:** Correct: the cited hypertrophic-cardiomyopathy passage calls for increased afterload, decreased contractility, and aggressive alpha-adrenergic treatment of hypotension, directly matching the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3303, passage ID 248912]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen textbook evidence directly states the hypertrophic-cardiomyopathy hemodynamic targets and distinguishes the other options as goals for different cardiac conditions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The original rationale required an explicit lesion-specific discriminator among several true cardiac-hemodynamic statements.

## S02-Q022

A patient with high-risk aortic stenosis and LV dysfunction is being considered for neuraxial sympathectomy. Which management plan best limits hemodynamic deterioration?

A. Avoid myocardial depressants and vasodilators, meticulously maintain preload and afterload, and avoid an abrupt SVR decrease with sympathectomy.

B. For mitral stenosis, avoid tachyarrhythmias, hypervolemia, and further increases in right-ventricular afterload.

C. If new-onset atrial fibrillation occurs before induction, postpone surgery if possible until rate control or sinus rhythm is achieved.

D. For severe pulmonary hypertension, maintain SVR and preload or venous return, treat hypoxemia and hypercarbia, and avoid myocardial depression.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient has high-risk aortic stenosis with LV dysfunction and is being considered for neuraxial sympathectomy, so the discriminator is prevention of myocardial depression and an abrupt fall in systemic vascular resistance. A states all of those protections and is the best match. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 41 Cardiovascular Disease, PDF p. 1063, printed p. 997, passage ID 274981]

**A — correct:** Correct: for high-risk aortic stenosis with LV dysfunction, the cited text specifies avoiding myocardial depressants and vasodilators, preserving preload and afterload, and avoiding an abrupt sympathectomy-related SVR decrease. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 41 Cardiovascular Disease, PDF p. 1063, printed p. 997, passage ID 274981]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Avoiding tachyarrhythmia, hypervolemia, and further right-ventricular afterload is a mitral-stenosis strategy, but B does not address the high-risk aortic-stenosis sympathectomy in the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3296, passage ID 248901]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Postponing surgery until new atrial fibrillation is controlled may be appropriate, but C changes the clinical problem and does not provide the requested aortic-stenosis plan for sympathectomy. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 8 Abnormalities of cardiac conduction and cardiac rhythm, PDF p. 181, passage ID 233034]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Maintaining SVR and venous return while treating hypoxemia, hypercarbia, acidosis, and myocardial depression applies to severe pulmonary hypertension, not the aortic-stenosis sympathectomy scenario asked here. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 41 Cardiovascular Disease, PDF p. 1063, printed p. 997, passage ID 274981]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly supplies the aortic-stenosis/LV-dysfunction plan; the other cited textbook passages identify alternatives for different conditions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The options mix several valid cardiovascular-management statements, so the rationale must preserve their truth while explaining the condition mismatch.

## S02-Q023

A patient with atrial fibrillation and rheumatic mitral stenosis needs long-term stroke-prevention anticoagulation. Which management option best matches this scenario?

A. Use aspirin plus warfarin as the mechanical-heart-valve strategy.

B. Use warfarin and avoid a direct oral anticoagulant.

C. Select a direct oral anticoagulant for valvular atrial fibrillation.

D. Use risk-stratified bridging only when an existing warfarin regimen must be interrupted.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for long-term stroke-prevention anticoagulation in atrial fibrillation with rheumatic mitral stenosis. Miller identifies warfarin as the anticoagulant of choice for valvular atrial fibrillation and contrasts it with DOAC use in nonvalvular atrial fibrillation, making B the direct answer. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 46. Patient Blood Management: Coagulation, PDF p. 1730, printed p. 1446, passage ID 288621]

**A — wrong condition:** Aspirin with warfarin is described for selected mechanical-valve patients, but the stem asks about rheumatic mitral stenosis and does not describe a mechanical valve; it therefore does not answer the stated valvular-AF choice. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 995, printed p. 833, passage ID 285147]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that warfarin remains the anticoagulant of choice for valvular atrial fibrillation, while DOACs are discussed for nonvalvular atrial fibrillation; that distinction matches rheumatic mitral stenosis. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 46. Patient Blood Management: Coagulation, PDF p. 1730, printed p. 1446, passage ID 288621]

**C — wrong condition:** A DOAC is presented by Miller in contrast with warfarin for nonvalvular atrial fibrillation, whereas the stem specifies valvular disease from rheumatic mitral stenosis; C reverses the textbook distinction. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 46. Patient Blood Management: Coagulation, PDF p. 1730, printed p. 1446, passage ID 288621]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Risk-stratified bridging concerns temporary interruption of an existing anticoagulant, but the lead-in asks which agent should be used long term; D does not identify that agent. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 46. Patient Blood Management: Coagulation, PDF p. 1730, printed p. 1446, passage ID 288621]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The originally frozen evidence was living-web guidance, so it is not used; Miller 10e directly establishes the valvular-versus-nonvalvular anticoagulant distinction and the mechanical-valve contrast.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence was non-textbook web guidance; all supporting citations in this rewrite are newly verified textbook passages.

## S02-Q024

During surgery on a patient with an implanted cardioverter-defibrillator, a magnet is applied to address electrocautery interference. What response should be expected?

A. Magnet application induces an asynchronous mode in a majority of pacemakers.

B. Convert the ICD to an asynchronous pacemaker without affecting its antitachycardia function.

C. The magnet inhibits ICD tachyarrhythmia detection and therefore prevents shock delivery, without changing the underlying pacemaker mode.

D. Asynchronous pacing from an ICD always requires reprogramming.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem concerns an implanted cardioverter-defibrillator and magnet use during electrocautery. The cited Miller passage states that a magnet inhibits ICD tachyarrhythmia detection and shock therapy without changing the underlying pacemaker mode, so C is the device-specific answer. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 34. Implantable Cardiac Pulse Generators: Pacemakers and Cardioverter-Defibrillators, PDF p. 1312, printed p. 1092, passage ID 286638]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Magnet-induced asynchronous pacing is described for a majority of pacemakers, but A concerns pacemakers generally and does not state the ICD response asked for in this stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 34. Implantable Cardiac Pulse Generators: Pacemakers and Cardioverter-Defibrillators, PDF p. 1312, printed p. 1092, passage ID 286638]

**B — wrong condition:** The ICD magnet response disables antitachycardia therapy but does not convert the underlying pacemaker to asynchronous mode; B combines the pacemaker response with the wrong device behavior. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 34. Implantable Cardiac Pulse Generators: Pacemakers and Cardioverter-Defibrillators, PDF p. 1312, printed p. 1092, passage ID 286638]

**C — correct:** Correct: the passage directly states that magnet application inhibits ICD tachyarrhythmia detection and shock delivery while leaving the underlying pacemaker mode unchanged. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 34. Implantable Cardiac Pulse Generators: Pacemakers and Cardioverter-Defibrillators, PDF p. 1312, printed p. 1092, passage ID 286638]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Asynchronous pacing from an ICD always requiring reprogramming is a true statement, but it addresses a different device-management question than the magnet response asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 34. Implantable Cardiac Pulse Generators: Pacemakers and Cardioverter-Defibrillators, PDF p. 1305, printed p. 34, passage ID 286612]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Miller directly states the ICD magnet response and contrasts it with pacemaker asynchronous-mode behavior.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The original rationale needed an explicit pacemaker-versus-ICD distinction.

## S02-Q025

Immediately before induction for elective surgery, a patient develops new-onset atrial fibrillation. Which perioperative plan is most appropriate if the operation can be deferred?

A. Use esmolol for ventricular-rate control and to increase conversion to sinus rhythm from 8% to 34%.

B. Control the ventricular response rate as the immediate goal in new-onset atrial fibrillation.

C. Manage stable postoperative atrial fibrillation medically with rate or rhythm control and electrically cardiovert unstable patients.

D. Postpone surgery if possible until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The arrhythmia is new-onset and occurs immediately before induction, while the operation is deferrable. The cited perioperative passage specifically recommends postponing surgery until ventricular rate control or sinus rhythm is achieved, making D the timing-specific answer. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 8 Abnormalities of cardiac conduction and cardiac rhythm, PDF p. 181, passage ID 233034]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Esmolol and conversion-rate data are described for postoperative atrial fibrillation or flutter, but A does not give the pre-induction postponement plan required by this stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 38. Anesthesia for Thoracic Surgery, PDF p. 3269, passage ID 248850]

**B — incomplete:** Rate control is the immediate treatment goal in new-onset atrial fibrillation, but B omits the stem-specific decision to defer elective surgery until rate control or sinus rhythm is achieved. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 76. The Postanesthesia Care Unit, PDF p. 2977, printed p. 2498, passage ID 294766]

**C — wrong condition:** Medical management of stable postoperative atrial fibrillation and electrical cardioversion when unstable concerns a postoperative complication, not new-onset atrial fibrillation before induction for a deferrable operation. [Slinger Thoracics 2e (2019).pdf, 53: Anesthetic Management of Post-thoracotomy Complications, PDF p. 863, passage ID 231536]

**D — correct:** Correct: Stoelting explicitly recommends postponing surgery, if possible, when new atrial fibrillation occurs before induction until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is restored. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 8 Abnormalities of cardiac conduction and cardiac rhythm, PDF p. 181, passage ID 233034]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen pre-induction textbook passage directly answers the timing question; the other passages support why the distractors apply to different contexts or omit the required timing decision.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Several options are true in postoperative or treatment-only contexts; the decisive feature is new onset immediately before induction with a deferrable operation.

## S02-Q026

According to Stoelting P&P, 6th edition, an adult cardiac-surgery team discusses whether an ACT threshold of 360 seconds can ever be accepted during cardiopulmonary bypass. Which interpretation matches the supplied evidence?

A. An ACT longer than 350 seconds is sometimes considered adequate, although it is not the target used by most centers.

B. In children, an ACT below 300 seconds despite standard heparin raises suspicion for antithrombin III deficiency.

C. In pediatric cardiac surgery, the target ACT before bypass is usually 480 seconds.

D. Most cardiac surgical centers target an ACT longer than 400 seconds during adult bypass.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The adult bypass question asks whether an ACT of 360 seconds can ever be accepted, not what most centers target. Stoelting states that ACT longer than 350 seconds is sometimes considered adequate while most centers target longer than 400 seconds, so A is the precise interpretation. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 30. Anticoagulants, PDF p. 2012, passage ID 315079]

**A — correct:** Correct: the cited adult CPB passage says an ACT above 350 seconds is sometimes considered adequate, while acknowledging that most centers use a higher target. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 30. Anticoagulants, PDF p. 2012, passage ID 315079]

**B — true but irrelevant:** An ACT below 300 seconds despite standard heparin raises concern for antithrombin III deficiency in children, but the stem asks about an adult cardiac-surgery threshold of 360 seconds. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 17 Cardiopulmonary bypass and management, PDF p. 841, passage ID 186411]

**C — true but irrelevant:** An ACT target of 480 seconds is described for pediatric cardiac surgery, but C is not the adult bypass interpretation requested by the stem. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 17 Cardiopulmonary bypass and management, PDF p. 845, passage ID 186435]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Most adult centers targeting an ACT above 400 seconds is true, but D overlooks the question’s narrower point that a value above 350 seconds is sometimes considered adequate. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 30. Anticoagulants, PDF p. 2012, passage ID 315079]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly distinguishes occasional adequacy above 350 seconds from the usual target above 400 seconds; the pediatric passages explain why B and C do not fit the adult stem.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key distinction is sometimes acceptable versus usual adult target, with age and population also separating the distractors.

## S02-Q027

After cardiopulmonary bypass, new ventricular dysfunction follows an episode in which air, thrombus, or calcium could have entered the coronary circulation. Which recognized ischemic etiology should be considered?

A. Kinked or clotted grafts are recognized technical ischemic causes of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

B. Coronary embolism from air, thrombus, or calcium can cause the post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

C. Inadequate myocardial protection is a recognized ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

D. Intraoperative infarction is a recognized ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem supplies a possible entry of air, thrombus, or calcium into the coronary circulation after bypass. The cited Barash list identifies coronary embolism from exactly those materials as an ischemic cause of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction, making B the direct match. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3358, passage ID 249004]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Kinked or clotted grafts are recognized technical causes of post-bypass ventricular dysfunction, but the stem specifically points to embolic material entering the coronary circulation. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3358, passage ID 249004]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash explicitly lists coronary embolism from air, thrombus, or calcium among ischemic etiologies of right- or left-ventricular dysfunction after CPB, exactly matching the stem clues. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3358, passage ID 249004]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Inadequate myocardial protection is also an ischemic etiology, but it does not identify the embolic mechanism specified by the air/thrombus/calcium clue. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3358, passage ID 249004]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Intraoperative infarction is listed as an ischemic etiology, but D is a broader outcome and does not name the coronary embolic cause given in the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3358, passage ID 249004]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. One frozen Barash passage lists the embolic, technical, protection, and infarction etiologies and uniquely matches the material-specific clue.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The distractors are recognized ischemic causes; the rationale must distinguish the stem’s embolic mechanism rather than call them false.

## S02-Q028

During the checklist before separation from cardiopulmonary bypass, which item specifically addresses volatile-anesthetic delivery?

A. Confirm that the heart is de-aired by checking lead II and TEE.

B. Assess vascular resistance because CPB flow is proportional to mean arterial pressure divided by resistance.

C. Turn off the vaporizers before separation from cardiopulmonary bypass.

D. Achieve normothermia at approximately 37°C nasopharyngeal, 35.5°C bladder, and 35°C rectal temperature.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks specifically which pre-separation checklist item addresses volatile-anesthetic delivery. The Barash checklist says vaporizers are off; the other choices concern de-airing, vascular resistance, or temperature, so C is the direct answer. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3356, passage ID 249001]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Checking lead II and TEE to confirm de-airing is a checklist item, but it addresses intracardiac air rather than volatile-anesthetic delivery. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3356, passage ID 249001]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Assessing vascular resistance and its relation to CPB flow is listed in the checklist, but it is a hemodynamic item and not the vaporizer action requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3356, passage ID 249001]

**C — correct:** Correct: the checklist explicitly specifies vaporizers off before separation from bypass, directly addressing volatile-anesthetic delivery. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3356, passage ID 249001]

**D — true but irrelevant:** The listed temperature values define normothermia before separation, but D concerns thermal status rather than volatile-anesthetic delivery. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 39. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgery, PDF p. 3356, passage ID 249001]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen checklist passage contains each listed item and explicitly identifies the vaporizer action.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Several options are valid checklist elements; the decisive discriminator is the requested volatile-anesthetic domain.

## S02-Q029

During thoracoabdominal aortic surgery, the team wants a spinal-cord-protection technique that lowers spinal-cord metabolic demand. Which approach matches the supplied evidence?

A. Use cerebrospinal-fluid drainage for spinal-cord protection during thoracoabdominal aortic surgery.

B. Maintain proximal hypertension during aortic cross-clamping for spinal-cord protection.

C. Reimplant critical intercostal arteries when possible for spinal-cord protection.

D. Use moderate hypothermia to 30°–32°C, including spinal cooling, to reduce spinal-cord metabolism.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the thoracoabdominal-aortic technique that lowers spinal-cord metabolic demand. Stoelting directly links moderate hypothermia to 30–32°C, including spinal cooling, with reduced spinal-cord metabolism, so D uniquely answers the mechanism asked. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 264, passage ID 233370]

**A — true but irrelevant:** CSF drainage is a recognized spinal-cord-protection technique, but it does not supply the metabolic-demand reduction specified in the lead-in. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 264, passage ID 233370]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Maintaining proximal hypertension during cross-clamping is listed as a protection technique, but it preserves perfusion rather than directly lowering spinal-cord metabolism as asked. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 264, passage ID 233370]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Reimplanting critical intercostal arteries can protect the cord, but it is a perfusion-preservation maneuver and not the hypothermic metabolic strategy requested. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 264, passage ID 233370]

**D — correct:** Correct: Stoelting explicitly describes moderate hypothermia to 30–32°C, including spinal cooling, as reducing spinal-cord metabolism during thoracoabdominal aortic surgery. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 264, passage ID 233370]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen passage lists all four techniques and directly connects only D’s technique to reduced spinal-cord metabolism.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The distractors are valid cord-protection measures but answer different physiologic goals.

## S02-Q030

Which monitoring modality used during carotid endarterectomy is described as a poor indicator of cerebral-perfusion adequacy?

A. Carotid stump pressure.

B. Transcranial Doppler ultrasonography.

C. Somatosensory evoked-potential monitoring.

D. Electroencephalography.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks which carotid-endarterectomy monitor is specifically a poor indicator of cerebral-perfusion adequacy. Stoelting explicitly characterizes internal-carotid stump pressure that way, making A the direct answer. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 272, passage ID 233408]

**A — correct:** Correct: the cited passage directly states that stump pressure, or internal-carotid back pressure, is a poor indicator of cerebral-perfusion adequacy. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 272, passage ID 233408]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Transcranial Doppler has listed uses for shunt decisions, malfunction recognition, and postoperative hyperperfusion, so B does not match the monitor identified as poor for perfusion adequacy. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 272, passage ID 233408]

**C — true but irrelevant:** SEP monitoring can detect changes with reduced regional cerebral blood flow, although interpretation can be confounded; C is not the passage’s explicitly poor adequacy indicator. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 272, passage ID 233408]

**D — true but irrelevant:** EEG has limitations because it may miss subcortical or small cortical infarcts, but the stem asks for the modality directly called a poor indicator of cerebral-perfusion adequacy: stump pressure. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 12 Vascular disease, PDF p. 272, passage ID 233408]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly labels stump pressure as poor and describes the distinct roles or limitations of the other modalities.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks for a specific limitation, not the monitor with the fewest capabilities overall.

## S02-Q031

During carotid clamping in a patient with severe contralateral carotid disease and no neurophysiologic monitor, which blood-pressure response is advised?

A. If a patient becomes agitated, confused, or unresponsive following carotid occlusion during awake carotid surgery, the anesthesiologist should increase systemic blood pressure up to 20% greater than preoperative values.

B. Induce hypertension to approximately 10%–20% above baseline during clamping.

C. Cerebral autoregulation is impaired and cerebral perfusion varies with changes in blood pressure in the days to months following an acute stroke.

D. Gradual reduction of arterial blood pressure over several weeks before carotid endarterectomy restores intravascular volume, resets cerebral autoregulation to a more normal range, and improves perioperative management.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem specifies severe contralateral carotid disease, carotid clamping, and no neurophysiologic monitor. Miller recommends induced hypertension approximately 10%–20% above baseline during clamping in that exact situation, so B is the stem-specific answer. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 52. Anesthesia for Vascular Surgery, PDF p. 2057, printed p. 1731, passage ID 290152]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Increasing systemic pressure up to 20% above preoperative values is described when awake carotid surgery produces neurologic symptoms, but A does not match the stated no-monitor clamping plan as directly as B. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 37. Anesthesia for Neurosurgery, PDF p. 3110, passage ID 248559]

**B — correct:** Correct: for contralateral occlusion or severe stenosis without neurophysiologic monitoring, Miller advocates induced hypertension approximately 10%–20% above baseline during carotid clamping. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 52. Anesthesia for Vascular Surgery, PDF p. 2057, printed p. 1731, passage ID 290152]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Impaired autoregulation after acute stroke is a relevant cerebral-perfusion fact, but C describes a different clinical context and does not give the intraoperative blood-pressure response requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1061, printed p. 886, passage ID 285496]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Gradual preoperative blood-pressure reduction is described for restoring volume and autoregulation over weeks, but D is not the immediate clamping response in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 52. Anesthesia for Vascular Surgery, PDF p. 2055, printed p. 1729, passage ID 290142]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller vascular-surgery passage directly matches the severe contralateral-disease/no-monitor scenario; other textbook citations describe different triggers or time scales.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The options include true blood-pressure concepts from other contexts; monitoring availability and timing are decisive.

## S02-Q041

An adolescent asks to discuss a sensitive health issue without a parent present. Which clinician behavior best supports trust while handling confidentiality?

A. When confidentiality entails minimal harm, encourage adolescents to be forthright with parents but respect their decision not to be.

B. When confidentiality may result in serious harm, clinicians may be ethically justified in notifying the parents.

C. Interview the adolescent in private and keep promises about confidentiality.

D. Provide emancipated and mature minors with a right to complete confidentiality.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The adolescent requests a private discussion about a sensitive issue. The cited Cote passage directly identifies private interviewing and keeping confidentiality promises as trust-enhancing behavior, so C best answers the lead-in. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 126, passage ID 183040]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Encouraging forthrightness with parents while respecting an adolescent’s decision is a confidentiality principle, but A does not describe the trust-building private interview asked for here. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 126, passage ID 183040]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Notifying parents may be ethically justified when confidentiality risks serious harm, but B addresses an exception rather than the routine trust behavior in the stem. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 126, passage ID 183040]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote states that clinicians enhance trust by interviewing the adolescent in private and keeping promises about confidentiality, exactly matching the requested behavior. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 126, passage ID 183040]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Complete confidentiality for emancipated or mature minors is a true Cote statement, but it is not the trust-building behavior of privately interviewing and keeping confidentiality promises. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 126, passage ID 183040]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the private-interview and confidentiality behavior and places the other statements in different confidentiality contexts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The options contain true confidentiality principles as well as an overbroad claim; the lead-in asks specifically about trust-building behavior.

## S02-Q042

A diagnostic test produces a discordant response pattern. Which sensitivity–specificity interpretation matches the supplied evidence?

A. Given a concordant response, a practitioner can be 88% confident that a positive response is a true positive.

B. Given a discordant response, a practitioner can be 81% confident that a positive response is a true positive.

C. The prevalence of cervical zygapophysial joint pain is high, around 60%.

D. A discordant response has lower specificity but high sensitivity.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the sensitivity–specificity interpretation of a discordant response. Bonica explicitly states that a discordant response has lower specificity but high sensitivity, so D directly matches the supplied evidence. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 98: Diagnostic and Therapeutic Nerve Blocks, PDF p. 4899, printed p. 4899, passage ID 262289]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The 88% true-positive confidence is associated with a concordant response, but the stem asks specifically about a discordant response. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 98: Diagnostic and Therapeutic Nerve Blocks, PDF p. 4899, printed p. 4899, passage ID 262289]

**B — true but irrelevant:** The 81% true-positive confidence is a stated discordant-response value, but it does not provide the requested sensitivity–specificity interpretation as directly as D. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 98: Diagnostic and Therapeutic Nerve Blocks, PDF p. 4899, printed p. 4899, passage ID 262289]

**C — true but irrelevant:** A prevalence around 60% is a population characteristic, but it does not answer how a discordant response changes sensitivity and specificity. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 98: Diagnostic and Therapeutic Nerve Blocks, PDF p. 4899, printed p. 4899, passage ID 262289]

**D — correct:** Correct: Bonica directly characterizes a discordant response as having lower specificity while retaining high sensitivity. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 98: Diagnostic and Therapeutic Nerve Blocks, PDF p. 4899, printed p. 4899, passage ID 262289]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Bonica passage directly states the requested interpretation and distinguishes the associated prevalence and confidence values.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The rationale must distinguish the requested test-property relationship from related numerical findings in the same passage.

## S02-Q043

In a randomized trial, groups receive different levels of care beyond the interventions being evaluated. Which bias does this describe?

A. Performance bias: systematic differences in care other than the preplanned interventions.

B. Selection bias in a randomized trial is systematic difference between patients receiving each intervention.

C. Attrition bias is systematic difference in withdrawal of patients from the two intervention groups.

D. Detection bias is systematic difference in ascertainment and recording of outcomes.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem describes groups receiving different care beyond the preplanned interventions. Barash defines performance bias as systematic differences in care other than those interventions, so A is the exact bias classification. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 534, passage ID 244127]

**A — correct:** Correct: performance bias is the systematic difference in care given to study patients apart from the preplanned interventions, exactly as described in the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 534, passage ID 244127]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Selection bias concerns systematic differences between patients receiving each intervention, but the stem describes differences in care delivered after group assignment. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 534, passage ID 244127]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Attrition bias concerns systematic differences in withdrawal from the two groups, but no withdrawal pattern is described in the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 534, passage ID 244127]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Detection bias concerns systematic differences in outcome ascertainment and recording, but the stem concerns treatment received rather than outcome measurement. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 534, passage ID 244127]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash definitions distinguish performance, selection, attrition, and detection bias.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The options are definitions of neighboring trial biases; the affected domain is care delivery beyond the planned intervention.

## S02-Q044

For a two-by-two outcome table with A and B in the intervention group and C and D in the comparison group, which formula calculates the risk difference?

A. Calculate the risk ratio as (A/(A + B)) divided by (C/(C + D)).

B. A/(A + B) minus C/(C + D).

C. Calculate NNT as the reciprocal of risk difference: NNT = 1/RD.

D. Calculate the odds ratio as (A/B) divided by (C/D).

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** For the stated two-by-two table, risk difference is intervention-group risk minus comparison-group risk. Barash gives that expression as A/(A+B) minus C/(C+D), making B correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 523, passage ID 244109]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A/(A+B) divided by C/(C+D) is the risk-ratio expression, but the stem asks for subtraction to calculate risk difference. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 523, passage ID 244109]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash defines risk difference as the intervention risk A/(A+B) minus the comparison risk C/(C+D). [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 523, passage ID 244109]

**C — true but irrelevant:** NNT equals 1/RD, but C gives a downstream measure rather than the risk-difference formula requested by the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 523, passage ID 244109]

**D — true but irrelevant:** The odds-ratio formula uses odds A/B and C/D, but the stem asks for risk difference based on group risks, not odds. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 7. Experimental Design and Statistics, PDF p. 523, passage ID 244109]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash formula list directly distinguishes risk difference from risk ratio, NNT, and odds ratio.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The arithmetic operators and denominators distinguish four related measures; the keyed expression must be preserved exactly.

## S02-Q045

Which readability-level recommendation is intended to improve patient understanding of written and oral risk communication?

A. Track and respond to family emotional cues to reduce cognitive load.

B. Present risk data using absolute risks and frequencies.

C. Use language at approximately an eighth-grade level.

D. Recognize that the last risk or benefit presented may carry more weight.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for the readability-level recommendation intended to improve understanding of written and oral risk communication. Cote specifically recommends language at the eighth-grade level, so C is the direct answer. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 124, passage ID 183032]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Responding to family emotional cues is a communication recommendation, but A does not specify the readability level asked for in the stem. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 124, passage ID 183032]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Absolute risks and frequencies improve risk communication, but B concerns data format rather than the language reading level requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 124, passage ID 183032]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote recommends using language at the eighth-grade level to improve understanding of written and oral communications. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 124, passage ID 183032]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Recency or order effects can alter risk perception, but D does not answer the readability-level recommendation asked for by the lead-in. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 3 Ethical issues in pediatric anesthesiology, PDF p. 124, passage ID 183032]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote communication list directly states the grade-level recommendation and distinguishes the other communication techniques.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks specifically for reading level, not another valid risk-communication technique.

## S02-Q046

After an adverse perioperative outcome, an initial factual disclosure and apology have occurred, but new information continues to emerge. Which communication practice should persist over time?

A. Speculate about which other team member should be blamed.

B. Repeat the initial known-facts disclosure and simple empathetic apology as though no new information emerged.

C. Limit further communication to coordination among healthcare team members.

D. Continue open dialogue with the patient and family as information is clarified.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Because new information continues to emerge after the initial disclosure, the relevant longitudinal practice is to maintain open dialogue as facts are clarified. Chestnut states this directly, making D correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 32 Shared Decision-Making and Communication, PDF p. 858, printed p. 791, passage ID 274057]

**A — false:** Speculation that blames another team member can raise concern that the team is hiding the truth, so A does not follow the cited communication approach. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 32 Shared Decision-Making and Communication, PDF p. 858, printed p. 791, passage ID 274057]

**B — incomplete:** Known-facts disclosure and a simple empathetic apology are appropriate initial elements, but B fails the stem because it treats an evolving situation as though no new information emerged. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 32 Shared Decision-Making and Communication, PDF p. 858, printed p. 791, passage ID 274057]

**C — incomplete:** Care-team coordination is required, but limiting communication to the team omits Chestnut’s explicit requirement to maintain open dialogue with the patient and family. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 32 Shared Decision-Making and Communication, PDF p. 858, printed p. 791, passage ID 274057]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut says to maintain open dialogue with the patient and family as information is clarified, exactly matching the continuing-communication scenario. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 32 Shared Decision-Making and Communication, PDF p. 858, printed p. 791, passage ID 274057]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly addresses evolving facts, apology, coordination, blame, and continued patient-family dialogue.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem makes the initial disclosure complete; the discriminator is what persists while information evolves.

## S02-Q047

Which bundle consists of personal protective equipment appropriate for a high-risk occupational exposure?

A. Gloves, gowns, boots, shoe covers, eyewear, masks, and shields.

B. Wash hands frequently and thoroughly before and after patient care.

C. Provide the HBV vaccine series and post-vaccination antibody testing.

D. Dispose of used sharps immediately in puncture-proof receptacles and do not recap needles.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for a bundle of personal protective equipment for high-risk exposure. Cote’s PPE list includes gloves, gowns, boots, shoe covers, eyewear, masks, and shields, so A is the complete bundle. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote’s occupational-exposure passage lists gloves, gowns, boots, shoe covers, eyewear, masks, and shields as appropriate PPE. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Hand washing is a standard precaution, but B is a hygiene action rather than the requested PPE bundle. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**C — true but irrelevant:** HBV vaccination and antibody testing are occupational protections, but C is preventive immunization rather than PPE equipment. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Immediate sharps disposal and no recapping reduce needlestick risk, but D is a sharps-safety practice and not the PPE bundle requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly lists the PPE bundle and distinguishes hygiene, immunization, and sharps-safety measures.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The distractors are valid occupational precautions but do not consist of PPE.

## S02-Q048

According to Cote, 7th edition, an anesthesia provider sustains an occupational HIV exposure. When should postexposure prophylaxis be initiated?

A. Wait 24 hours before initiating prophylaxis.

B. Initiate occupational HIV postexposure prophylaxis within 2 hours; efficacy is thought to diminish after 24–36 hours.

C. Wait 36 hours before initiating prophylaxis.

D. Delay prophylaxis until source assessment is complete.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks when occupational HIV postexposure prophylaxis should begin. Cote states that PEP should be initiated within 2 hours and that efficacy is thought to diminish after 24–36 hours, making B the precise timing answer. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 1998, passage ID 191804]

**A — wrong timing or magnitude:** Waiting 24 hours is later than Cote’s recommended initiation within 2 hours and therefore does not match the preferred timing. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 1998, passage ID 191804]

**B — correct:** Correct: Cote specifies initiation within 2 hours and notes diminishing efficacy after 24–36 hours following exposure. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 1998, passage ID 191804]

**C — wrong timing or magnitude:** Waiting 36 hours is later than the within-2-hour initiation stated by Cote and therefore fails the exposure-timing question. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 1998, passage ID 191804]

**D — wrong timing or magnitude:** Delaying PEP for source assessment conflicts with Cote’s statement that rapid initiation should not be delayed for source assessment, so D gives the wrong timing. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 1997, passage ID 191802]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passages directly state the two-hour initiation window, diminishing efficacy, and no delay for source assessment.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The timing options are deliberately close to the efficacy-diminution window; the recommended start is within 2 hours.

## S02-Q049

Before occupational blood exposure occurs, which preventive measure should be arranged for healthcare providers?

A. Wash hands frequently and thoroughly before and after patient care.

B. Dispose of used sharps immediately in puncture-proof receptacles and do not recap needles.

C. Provide the HBV vaccine series and post-vaccination antibody testing.

D. Use gloves, gowns, boots, shoe covers, eyewear, masks, and shields as appropriate PPE.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The preventive measure must be arranged before an occupational blood exposure. Cote specifically recommends HBV vaccination for healthcare providers with post-vaccination antibody testing, so C is the pre-exposure measure requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Hand washing is recommended before and after patient care, but it is a point-of-care precaution rather than the arranged pre-exposure vaccination measure. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Immediate sharps disposal and avoiding recapping are important practices, but B does not provide the pre-exposure immunization and antibody-testing plan. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote states that healthcare providers should receive the HBV vaccine series and antibody testing after completion to document response. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**D — true but irrelevant:** PPE is appropriate for the patient-care situation, but D is exposure-barrier equipment and does not represent the arranged vaccination-and-testing prevention asked for. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 47 Infectious disease considerations for the operating room, PDF p. 2010, passage ID 191850]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly supports the HBV series and antibody testing and lists the other valid but different precautions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The discriminator is pre-exposure occupational prevention arranged for the provider, not immediate barrier or sharps practices.

## S02-Q059

A patient has new confusion, a respiratory rate of 26 per minute, and a systolic blood pressure of 95 mm Hg. How should this qSOFA screen be interpreted?

A. Use serum creatinine above 0.8 mg/dL as a qSOFA criterion.

B. Require dysfunction in all six full-SOFA organ domains before identifying risk.

C. Use a mean arterial pressure below 70 mm Hg as the qSOFA blood-pressure criterion.

D. The patient is at risk because at least two qSOFA criteria are present.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient has confusion, respiratory rate 26, and systolic pressure 95: all three qSOFA criteria listed by Chestnut are present. At least two criteria indicate risk, so D is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1357, printed p. 1291, passage ID 276292]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Creatinine above 0.8 mg/dL is a pregnancy-related observation contrasted with the SOFA kidney threshold, but it is not a qSOFA criterion in this screen. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1357, printed p. 1291, passage ID 276292]

**B — wrong condition:** Full SOFA evaluates multiple organ domains, but qSOFA risk does not require dysfunction in all six domains; two of its three bedside criteria suffice. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1357, printed p. 1291, passage ID 276292]

**C — wrong condition:** A MAP below 70 mm Hg is a SOFA-related threshold, but qSOFA uses systolic pressure below 100 mm Hg; C substitutes the wrong pressure criterion. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1357, printed p. 1291, passage ID 276292]

**D — correct:** Correct: confusion, respiratory rate above 22, and systolic blood pressure below 100 are all present, exceeding the two-criterion qSOFA risk threshold stated by Chestnut. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 54 Trauma and Critical Care, PDF p. 1357, printed p. 1291, passage ID 276292]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly defines the three qSOFA criteria and the threshold of two or more.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem supplies all three qSOFA abnormalities; MAP and full-SOFA thresholds are deliberate distractors.

## S02-Q060

During adult CPR, which minimum compression-depth target is required for high-quality compressions?

A. At least 2 inches (5 cm).

B. Use a compression rate of 100 to 120 per minute.

C. Allow complete chest recoil between compressions.

D. Use a 30:2 compression-to-ventilation ratio without an advanced airway.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the minimum adult compression depth for high-quality CPR. The newly retrieved Lange textbook passage states that adult compressions depress the sternum approximately 2 inches (5 cm), supporting A; the frozen AHA record is not used as authority. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 55 Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation, PDF p. 2392, passage ID 280052]

**A — correct:** Correct: Lange states that in adults the sternum is depressed approximately 2 inches (5 cm) during chest compressions, matching the minimum depth target. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 55 Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation, PDF p. 2392, passage ID 280052]

**B — true but irrelevant:** A compression rate of 100 per minute is described by Lange, but B asks about rate rather than the minimum compression depth requested by the stem. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 55 Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation, PDF p. 2392, passage ID 280052]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Allowing the sternum to return to its normal position is part of effective compressions, but C concerns recoil and not the minimum depth asked for. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 55 Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation, PDF p. 2392, passage ID 280052]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A 30:2 ratio without an advanced airway is a ventilation/compression relationship, but D does not specify the adult compression-depth target. [Lange 7e (2022).pdf, 55 Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation, PDF p. 2392, passage ID 280052]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The frozen evidence was AHA guidance and therefore excluded; Lange 7e directly supports the adult 2-inch/5-cm depth and contrasts rate, recoil, and ratio.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The frozen item’s source was non-textbook AHA guidance; all citations here use newly verified Lange 7e textbook evidence.

## S02-Q061

According to the AHA 2025 Resuscitation Algorithms, waveform capnography during adult cardiac arrest shows a low or decreasing ETCO2. What should the team do?

A. After ROSC, transition to post-cardiac-arrest care.

B. Promptly reassess CPR quality.

C. If ROSC has not occurred, continue CPR in two-minute cycles before rhythm checks.

D. Maintain high-quality CPR by minimizing interruptions and avoiding excessive ventilation.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** A low or decreasing ETCO2 during CPR signals inadequate resuscitation performance and should trigger reassessment of CPR quality. Barash explains that ETCO2 reflects cardiac output during CPR and recommends using it to judge compression effectiveness, supporting B with textbook evidence. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 58. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation, PDF p. 4911, passage ID 251909]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Post-ROSC care is a later branch of the arrest algorithm, but the stem presents low/decreasing ETCO2 during ongoing CPR and asks for the immediate response. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 82. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Advanced Cardiac Life Support, PDF p. 3132, printed p. 2, passage ID 295528]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash states that continuous-waveform ETCO2 should be used to judge compression effectiveness and that technique should be altered to maximize a low measurement, directly supporting CPR-quality reassessment. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 58. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation, PDF p. 4911, passage ID 251909]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Two-minute CPR cycles before rhythm checks are part of the broader arrest sequence, but C does not address the specific information supplied by a low or decreasing ETCO2. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 82. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Advanced Cardiac Life Support, PDF p. 3132, printed p. 2, passage ID 295527]

**D — incomplete:** Minimizing interruptions and avoiding excessive ventilation are quality principles, but D does not state the ETCO2-triggered reassessment requested by the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 82. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Advanced Cardiac Life Support, PDF p. 3132, printed p. 2, passage ID 295527]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The frozen AHA evidence was not used; Barash directly links ETCO2 to CPR effectiveness and recommends using it to assess and improve compressions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence was non-textbook AHA guidance; new Barash evidence supports the keyed CPR-quality response, while algorithm-branch options remain stem-relative distractors.

## S02-Q062

Which option correctly states both the CRASH-2 tranexamic-acid dosing regimen and its overall-mortality finding?

A. Tranexamic acid reduced deaths due to bleeding in the reported trial.

B. The trial included 20,211 trauma patients.

C. A 1-g loading dose followed by 1 g over 8 hours reduced overall mortality.

D. Epsilon-aminocaproic acid is less well studied than tranexamic acid.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for both the CRASH-2 dosing regimen and its overall-mortality finding. Stoelting reports a 1-g loading dose followed by 1 g over 8 hours with reduced overall mortality, so C contains both required elements. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 31. Physiology and Management of Massive Transfusion, PDF p. 2063, passage ID 315164]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The trial reported fewer deaths due to bleeding, but A gives a component outcome and does not state the requested dose plus overall-mortality combination. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 31. Physiology and Management of Massive Transfusion, PDF p. 2063, passage ID 315164]

**B — true but irrelevant:** The sample included 20,211 trauma patients, but B supplies sample size rather than both the regimen and overall-mortality finding asked for. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 31. Physiology and Management of Massive Transfusion, PDF p. 2063, passage ID 315164]

**C — correct:** Correct: Stoelting reports tranexamic acid as 1 g loading followed by 1 g over 8 hours and states that overall mortality was reduced. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 31. Physiology and Management of Massive Transfusion, PDF p. 2063, passage ID 315164]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Epsilon-aminocaproic acid is described as less studied, but D neither gives the CRASH-2 dose nor reports the overall-mortality result. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 31. Physiology and Management of Massive Transfusion, PDF p. 2063, passage ID 315164]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly supplies the dose, sample size, bleeding outcome, comparator, and overall-mortality finding.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question requires the conjunction of regimen and overall mortality, not either isolated trial detail.

## S02-Q063

A patient with suspected unstable pelvic-ring injury remains hypotensive during initial trauma care. Which temporary maneuver can stabilize and tamponade some pelvic fractures?

A. Use preperitoneal packing to rapidly control pelvic-fracture hemorrhage and reduce transfusion and mortality.

B. Provide greater fluid and blood resuscitation volumes for mechanically unstable pelvic-ring fractures.

C. Use REBOA as a temporary hemorrhage-control technique for hemodynamically significant pelvic fractures.

D. Apply a pelvic binder or tightly knotted sheet.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The hypotensive patient has a suspected unstable pelvic-ring injury and needs a temporary mechanical stabilization/tamponade maneuver. Miller directly describes a pelvic binder or tightly knotted sheet for that purpose, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2421, printed p. 2032, passage ID 291984]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Preperitoneal packing is a rapid hemorrhage-control method, but A is an operative bleeding intervention rather than the temporary external stabilization/tamponade maneuver requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2421, printed p. 2032, passage ID 291984]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Mechanical instability is associated with greater fluid and blood resuscitation volumes, but B describes a resuscitation requirement and not a temporary pelvic stabilization maneuver. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2421, printed p. 2032, passage ID 291984]

**C — true but irrelevant:** REBOA can provide temporary hemorrhage control, but C is an endovascular technique and does not identify the binder or tightly knotted sheet asked for here. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2421, printed p. 2032, passage ID 291984]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller states that a pelvic binder or tightly knotted bed sheet can temporarily stabilize and tamponade some unstable pelvic fractures. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2421, printed p. 2032, passage ID 291984]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller trauma passage directly identifies the temporary binder/sheet maneuver and distinguishes operative, resuscitative, and endovascular alternatives.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key discriminator is temporary mechanical pelvic stabilization/tamponade, not any general hemorrhage-control method.

## S02-Q064

A neurocritical-care patient has an ICP of 28 mm Hg with impending herniation while definitive ICP-lowering measures are being arranged. Which temporary ventilation target is supported?

A. Use short-term hyperventilation to PaCO2 30–35 mm Hg.

B. Use hyperventilation only as a temporizing measure until other ICP-lowering measures are available.

C. Elevate the head 30 degrees and avoid head rotation.

D. Maintain the checklist's normal PaCO2 of 35–38 mm Hg.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient’s ICP is 28 mm Hg with impending herniation, exceeding the threshold for a temporary ventilation intervention. Miller recommends short-term hyperventilation to PaCO2 30–35 mm Hg for ICP above 20–25 mm Hg, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 80. Neurocritical Care, PDF p. 3080, printed p. 2581, passage ID 295273]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller specifies short-term hyperventilation to PaCO2 30–35 mm Hg when ICP exceeds 20–25 mm Hg, matching the impending-herniation scenario. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 80. Neurocritical Care, PDF p. 3080, printed p. 2581, passage ID 295273]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Hyperventilation is indeed only a temporizing measure, but B omits the ventilation target that the lead-in specifically asks for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 80. Neurocritical Care, PDF p. 3080, printed p. 2581, passage ID 295273]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Head elevation and avoiding rotation are listed ICP measures, but C is a positioning intervention rather than the temporary ventilation target requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 80. Neurocritical Care, PDF p. 3080, printed p. 2581, passage ID 295273]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Normocapnia at PaCO2 35–38 mm Hg is a checklist goal, but D does not provide the short-term hyperventilation target for markedly elevated ICP. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 80. Neurocritical Care, PDF p. 3080, printed p. 2581, passage ID 295273]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller neurocritical-care checklist states the ICP threshold, temporary target, positioning, and normocapnia alternatives.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The word ‘temporary’ distinguishes the short-term hyperventilation target from routine normocapnia.

## S02-Q065

Which agents are listed as hyperosmolar options for intracranial hypertension in the supplied neurocritical-care checklist?

B. Mannitol or hypertonic saline.

**Answer: B**

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks which agents are hyperosmolar options in the neurocritical-care checklist. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2075, printed p. 1744, passage ID 290248]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller’s checklist lists mannitol and hypertonic saline as hyperosmolar therapy options. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2075, printed p. 1744, passage ID 290248]

**C — true but irrelevant:** CSF drainage through ventriculostomy or brain needle is a pressure-management option, but C is not an administered hyperosmolar agent. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2075, printed p. 1744, passage ID 290248]

**D — true but irrelevant:** The listed normo-targets are checklist goals, but D does not identify the hyperosmolar agents requested by the lead-in. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 53. Anesthesia for Neurologic Surgery and Neurointerventions, PDF p. 2075, printed p. 1744, passage ID 290248]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller checklist directly categorizes hyperosmolar therapy and distinguishes suppression, drainage, and physiologic goals.

## S02-Q066

A patient on VA-ECMO develops rising pulmonary-artery pressure followed by pulmonary edema and hemorrhage. Which ECMO complication best explains this sequence?

A. Left-upper-extremity VA-ECMO blood gases may not reflect coronary or cerebral perfusion because of the mixing-cloud location.

B. Use pulmonary-artery catheter information during VA-ECMO weaning.

C. VA-ECMO afterload stress causing LV distention.

D. Use a central venous catheter for vasoactive and inotropic drug access during VA-ECMO.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Rising pulmonary-artery pressure followed by pulmonary edema and hemorrhage is the sequence Miller describes for VA-ECMO afterload-related LV distention. Thus C identifies the complication and mechanism that fit the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 81. Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation and Cardiac Devices, PDF p. 3118, printed p. 2611, passage ID 295460]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Left-upper-extremity blood gases may not represent coronary or cerebral perfusion because of mixing, but A does not explain rising pulmonary pressure followed by edema or hemorrhage. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 81. Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation and Cardiac Devices, PDF p. 3118, printed p. 2611, passage ID 295460]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Pulmonary-artery catheter data can be useful during VA-ECMO weaning, but B is a monitoring use and not the complication causing the described sequence. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 81. Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation and Cardiac Devices, PDF p. 3118, printed p. 2611, passage ID 295460]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller states that VA-ECMO afterload stress can distend the LV, with rising pulmonary-artery pressure preceding pulmonary edema or pulmonary hemorrhage. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 81. Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation and Cardiac Devices, PDF p. 3118, printed p. 2611, passage ID 295460]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A central venous catheter provides vasoactive and inotropic access, but D is an access detail and does not account for the pulmonary-pressure/edema sequence. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 81. Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation and Cardiac Devices, PDF p. 3118, printed p. 2611, passage ID 295460]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller ECMO passage directly describes the complication sequence and contrasts monitoring and access details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The temporal sequence of rising pulmonary pressure before edema/hemorrhage is the specific clue to LV distention from afterload stress.

## S02-Q067

Before apnea testing for possible brain death, which set of prerequisites must be confirmed?

A. Begin the apnea test with PaCO2 40±5 mm Hg and PEEP 5 cm H2O oxygenation.

B. Rule out all reversible causes of coma before reflex assessment.

C. Allow spinal reflexes to remain intact during brain-death testing.

D. Irreversible coma, no sedatives or paralytics, near-normal temperature, systolic BP above 100 mm Hg, and no spontaneous ventilation.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Before apnea testing, the stem asks for the prerequisite set rather than an apnea-test maneuver. Stoelting lists irreversible coma, no sedatives or paralytics, near-normal temperature, systolic pressure above 100 mm Hg, and no spontaneous ventilation, so D is correct. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 296, printed p. 285, passage ID 233509]

**A — true but irrelevant:** PaCO2 40±5 mm Hg and PEEP 5 cm H2O oxygenation describe starting the apnea test, but A is not the prerequisite set requested before testing. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 296, printed p. 285, passage ID 233509]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Reversible causes should be ruled out before reflex assessment, but B is only one prerequisite and omits the complete physiologic and medication conditions in the stem’s requested set. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 296, printed p. 285, passage ID 233509]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Spinal reflexes may remain intact during testing, but C is a recognition about interpretation and not the required pretest prerequisites. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 296, printed p. 285, passage ID 233509]

**D — correct:** Correct: Stoelting’s prerequisite list includes irreversible coma, absent sedative/paralytic effects, core temperature at least 36°C or near normal, systolic pressure above 100, and no spontaneous ventilation. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 13 Diseases affecting the brain, PDF p. 296, printed p. 285, passage ID 233509]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly lists the prerequisite set and distinguishes it from apnea-test setup and interpretation details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The options mix prerequisites with test setup and interpretation; the lead-in explicitly asks what must be confirmed before testing.

## S02-Q077

Which statement best reflects the supplied evidence about processed EEG suppression and its perioperative implications?

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks which statement reflects the supplied evidence about severe processed-EEG suppression and perioperative implications. Geriatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2380, printed p. 1996, passage ID 291784]
