**B — true but irrelevant:** Decreased cardiovascular reserve in older patients is a relevant geriatric consideration, but B does not address processed-EEG suppression or delirium. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 61. Geriatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2380, printed p. 1996, passage ID 291784]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Single-channel or processed EEG can be used for depth monitoring, but C describes monitoring modality and not the suppression–delirium association asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 61. Geriatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2380, printed p. 1996, passage ID 291784]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Lower induction and maintenance doses may help avoid burst suppression, but D describes a prevention strategy rather than the evidence relationship asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 61. Geriatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2380, printed p. 1996, passage ID 291784]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the suppression–delirium association and includes the related monitoring, dosing, and geriatric-reserve statements.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for an evidence association, not a monitoring technique or preventive dosing action.

## S02-Q078

After succinylcholine, four TOF responses remain equal without fade. Which statement about this depolarizing-block assessment is correct?

A. During partial nondepolarizing block, TOF ratio decreases and fades with block depth.

B. During partial depolarizing block, TOF fade does not occur and TOF ratio remains 1.0.

C. Fade after succinylcholine signifies phase-II block.

D. During depolarizing-block assessment, TOF stimulation consists of four supramaximal stimuli every 0.5 seconds.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Four equal TOF responses without fade are consistent with partial depolarizing block. Miller states that partial depolarizing block has no TOF fade and a TOF ratio of 1.0, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1458, printed p. 1213, passage ID 287352]

**A — true but irrelevant:** TOF fade with decreasing ratio describes partial nondepolarizing block, but A does not match the succinylcholine depolarizing pattern in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1458, printed p. 1213, passage ID 287352]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that partial depolarizing block produces no TOF fade and leaves the TOF ratio at 1.0. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1458, printed p. 1213, passage ID 287352]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Fade after succinylcholine signifies phase-II block, but C describes a later phase-II phenomenon rather than the equal responses in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1458, printed p. 1213, passage ID 287352]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Four supramaximal stimuli at 0.5-second intervals define TOF stimulation, but D describes the test stimulus and not the depolarizing-block interpretation requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 39. Neuromuscular Monitoring, PDF p. 1458, printed p. 1213, passage ID 287352]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage distinguishes depolarizing, nondepolarizing, phase-II, and stimulus-pattern concepts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- No fade with a TOF ratio of 1.0 is the key depolarizing-block discriminator.

## S02-Q079

An anesthesia machine develops rebreathing and erratic circuit pressure. Which broad statement best describes the vulnerability created by the circle system's complex design?

A. Recognize that malfunction of unidirectional valves can be life-threatening.

B. Recognize that circle systems may compromise tidal-volume delivery because they are large and compliant.

C. Circle-system misconnections, disconnections, obstructions, and leaks are possible because of its complex design.

D. Recognize that CO2 absorbers can permit anesthetic degradation.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem describes rebreathing and erratic pressure in a complex circle system and asks for the broad vulnerability created by that design. Miller states that ten or more connections make circle systems susceptible to misconnections, disconnections, obstructions, and leaks, so C is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 20. Inhaled Anesthetic Delivery Systems, PDF p. 584, printed p. 482, passage ID 283190]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Unidirectional-valve malfunction can be life-threatening, but A identifies one component failure rather than the broad complex-design vulnerability asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 20. Inhaled Anesthetic Delivery Systems, PDF p. 584, printed p. 482, passage ID 283190]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Large compliant systems may compromise tidal-volume delivery, but B describes a compliance consequence and not the broad set of design-related failure modes. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 20. Inhaled Anesthetic Delivery Systems, PDF p. 584, printed p. 482, passage ID 283190]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller explicitly states that circle-system complexity and numerous connections permit misconnections, disconnections, obstructions, and leaks. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 20. Inhaled Anesthetic Delivery Systems, PDF p. 584, printed p. 482, passage ID 283190]

**D — true but irrelevant:** CO2 absorbers can permit anesthetic degradation, but D is one absorber-related issue and does not state the broad complex-circle-system vulnerability. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 20. Inhaled Anesthetic Delivery Systems, PDF p. 584, printed p. 482, passage ID 283190]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the broad circle-system failure modes and provides the narrower distractor issues.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem asks for a design-level vulnerability, not a single valve, compliance, or absorber complication.

## S02-Q080

Which statement correctly identifies the Bain breathing circuit?

A. An unrecognized disconnection or kinking of the inner fresh-gas hose can cause hypercapnia.

B. Bain fresh-gas inflow must be 2.5 times minute ventilation to prevent rebreathing.

C. The Bain circuit can be used during spontaneous and controlled ventilation.

D. The Bain circuit is a coaxial modification of Mapleson D.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for the defining circuit classification. Barash directly identifies the Bain circuit as a coaxial modification of the Mapleson D system, so D is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 2012, passage ID 246695]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Inner-hose disconnection or kinking can cause hypercapnia, but A describes a Bain malfunction and not the circuit’s defining classification. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 2012, passage ID 246695]

**B — true but irrelevant:** A fresh-gas inflow of 2.5 times minute ventilation is a rebreathing-prevention requirement, but B does not identify what circuit Bain is. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 2012, passage ID 246695]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Bain may be used for spontaneous and controlled ventilation, but C is a use characteristic and not the defining circuit relationship asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 2012, passage ID 246695]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash explicitly defines the Bain circuit as a coaxial circuit and a modification of Mapleson D. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 2012, passage ID 246695]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage states the circuit definition and distinguishes it from flow, ventilation-use, and malfunction facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The defining classification is requested; the other options are true operational details but not the classification.

## S02-Q081

When selecting a ventilator mode, which flow pattern comparison supports favoring pressure-controlled over volume-controlled ventilation?

A. Pressure-controlled ventilation uses decelerating flow, whereas volume-controlled ventilation uses continuous flow.

B. Pressure-controlled ventilation has variable tidal volume because of respiratory-circuit compliance and resistance.

C. Tidal volume above 10 mL/kg should be avoided.

D. Tidal volume between 6 and 10 mL/kg may be justifiable in pediatric ventilation.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The lead-in asks for the flow-pattern comparison supporting pressure-controlled ventilation. Miller directly states that PCV uses decelerating flow whereas VCV uses continuous inspiratory flow, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2729, printed p. 2286, passage ID 293498]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller identifies decelerating flow with PCV and continuous inspiratory flow with VCV, exactly matching the requested comparison. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2729, printed p. 2286, passage ID 293498]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Variable tidal volume from circuit compliance and resistance is a PCV disadvantage, but B does not provide the flow-pattern comparison asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2729, printed p. 2286, passage ID 293498]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Avoiding tidal volumes above 10 mL/kg is a ventilation-volume point, but C does not distinguish PCV from VCV by flow pattern. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2729, printed p. 2286, passage ID 293498]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A 6–10 mL/kg range may be justifiable in pediatric ventilation, but D concerns tidal-volume magnitude and not the PCV/VCV flow comparison. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 72. Pediatric Anesthesia, PDF p. 2729, printed p. 2286, passage ID 293498]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the flow-pattern distinction and supplies the volume-related distractor facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks specifically for flow pattern, not tidal-volume variability or volume targets.

## S02-Q082

Which vaporizer-safety feature specifically prevents filling a vaporizer with the wrong volatile anesthetic?

A. Locating the filler port at the maximum safe liquid level minimizes overfilling.

B. Keyed filling devices prevent filling a vaporizer with the wrong agent.

C. Vaporizer interlocks prevent simultaneous administration of more than one volatile anesthetic.

D. Antispill designs prevent hazards from vaporizer tipping.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks which vaporizer feature prevents filling with the wrong volatile anesthetic. Barash directly identifies agent-specific keyed filling devices for that purpose, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 1979, passage ID 246641]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Locating the filler port at the maximum safe liquid level minimizes overfilling, but A does not prevent selection of the wrong agent. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 1979, passage ID 246641]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash states that agent-specific keyed filling devices help prevent filling a vaporizer with the wrong volatile agent. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 1979, passage ID 246641]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Interlocks prevent simultaneous delivery of more than one volatile anesthetic, but C addresses simultaneous use rather than incorrect filling. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 1979, passage ID 246641]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Antispill designs address hazards if a vaporizer tips, but D does not prevent filling with the wrong anesthetic. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 25. The Anesthesia Workstation and Delivery Systems for Inhaled Anesthetics, PDF p. 1979, passage ID 246641]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly maps keyed filling, filler-port, interlock, and antispill safety features to their distinct hazards.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The safety function requested is wrong-agent prevention, not overfill, simultaneous-delivery, or tipping prevention.

## S02-Q083

Which triplet contains one example from each operating-room fire-triangle category?

A. Electrosurgery, lasers, and bipolar electrosurgery.

B. Drapes, gauze, and hair.

C. Oxygen, a surgical drape, and electrosurgery.

D. Air, oxygen, and nitrous oxide.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The fire triangle requires one oxidizer, one fuel, and one ignition source. Barash lists oxygen as an oxidizer, a surgical drape as fuel, and electrosurgery as an ignition source, so C contains one of each. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 5. Electrical Safety and Fires in the Operating Room, PDF p. 381, passage ID 243832]

**A — wrong condition:** Electrosurgery, lasers, and bipolar electrosurgery are all ignition sources, so A does not contain one item from each fire-triangle category. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 5. Electrical Safety and Fires in the Operating Room, PDF p. 381, passage ID 243832]

**B — wrong condition:** Drapes, gauze, and hair are all listed as fuels, so B does not contain an oxidizer or ignition source. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 5. Electrical Safety and Fires in the Operating Room, PDF p. 381, passage ID 243832]

**C — correct:** Correct: oxygen is an oxidizer, a surgical drape is a fuel, and electrosurgery is an ignition source in Barash’s operating-room fire-triangle examples. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 5. Electrical Safety and Fires in the Operating Room, PDF p. 381, passage ID 243832]

**D — wrong condition:** Air, oxygen, and nitrous oxide are all oxidizers in the cited table, so D does not provide fuel or ignition source categories. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 5. Electrical Safety and Fires in the Operating Room, PDF p. 381, passage ID 243832]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash table explicitly categorizes the listed oxidizers, fuels, and ignition sources.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key requires category diversity; multiple examples from one category do not form a fire triangle.

Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1984, printed p. 1674, passage ID 289797]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Right-ventricular collapse is also a tamponade sign, but B is incomplete compared with the requested combination of respiratory variation, chamber collapse, and flow variation. **C — true but irrelevant:** IVC plethora is listed among tamponade signs, but C is a single finding and does not provide the combined physiology specified by the lead-in. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1984, printed p. 1674, passage ID 289797]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller’s Doppler list includes exaggerated ventricular respiratory variation, right-sided collapse, and abnormal respiratory variation in transvalvular flow velocities. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 50. Anesthesia for Cardiac Surgical Procedures, PDF p. 1984, printed p. 1674, passage ID 289797]

**Editorial QA observations:**

## S02-Q085

A dyspneic patient has diffuse bilateral B-lines moving with preserved lung sliding and no focal liver-like consolidation. Which diagnosis is best supported?

A. Pulmonary edema.

B. Normally aerated lung with an A-line pattern.

C. Pneumothorax at the examined sites.

D. Focal pulmonary consolidation.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Diffuse moving B-lines indicate interstitial fluid and are classically associated with pulmonary edema. Miller also distinguishes A-lines, pneumothorax assessment, and consolidation, so A best fits the described diffuse pattern. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 33. Perioperative Echocardiography and Point-of-Care Ultrasound (POCUS), PDF p. 1287, printed p. 1076, passage ID 286524]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller states that B-lines indicate fluid in interlobar septa and are classically seen in pulmonary edema; their diffuse moving pattern fits the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 33. Perioperative Echocardiography and Point-of-Care Ultrasound (POCUS), PDF p. 1287, printed p. 1076, passage ID 286524]

**B — wrong condition:** Normally aerated lung with an A-line pattern represents air, whereas the stem describes B-lines indicating interstitial fluid; B is the wrong ultrasound pattern. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 33. Perioperative Echocardiography and Point-of-Care Ultrasound (POCUS), PDF p. 1287, printed p. 1076, passage ID 286524]

**C — wrong condition:** B-lines or lung sliding can help exclude pneumothorax at the examined area, but C contradicts the fluid-rich B-line pattern and gives the wrong diagnosis. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 33. Perioperative Echocardiography and Point-of-Care Ultrasound (POCUS), PDF p. 1287, printed p. 1076, passage ID 286524]

**D — wrong condition:** A liver-like consolidated lung with air bronchograms is a consolidation pattern, but the stem specifies diffuse B-lines without focal liver-like consolidation. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 33. Perioperative Echocardiography and Point-of-Care Ultrasound (POCUS), PDF p. 1287, printed p. 1076, passage ID 286524]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller ultrasound passage directly contrasts B-lines, A-lines, pneumothorax, and consolidation.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The absence of focal consolidation and preserved sliding narrows the pattern to diffuse interstitial-fluid findings.

## S02-Q091

After sitting-position neurosurgery, imaging shows an expanding intracranial air collection producing mass effect. Which complication is present?

A. Position-related cerebral hypoperfusion.

B. Tension pneumocephalus.

C. Positional cerebral desaturation.

D. Position-related spinal cord ischemia.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** An expanding intracranial air collection producing mass effect after sitting-position neurosurgery is tension pneumocephalus. Miller defines it as air in subdural or ventricular space causing pressure on intracranial structures, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1126, printed p. 944, passage ID 285776]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Sitting surgery can reduce cerebral perfusion, but A does not identify the expanding intracranial-air mass with mass effect described here. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1126, printed p. 944, passage ID 285776]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller defines tension pneumocephalus as subdural or ventricular air causing pressure on intracranial structures after neurosurgery in the sitting position. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1126, printed p. 944, passage ID 285776]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Sitting position can affect cerebral oxygen saturation, but C does not account for the expanding intracranial air collection. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1126, printed p. 944, passage ID 285776]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Neck positioning can impair spinal perfusion, but D describes a different complication and does not explain intracranial mass effect from air. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 30. Patient Positioning and Associated Risks, PDF p. 1126, printed p. 944, passage ID 285776]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly defines tension pneumocephalus and contrasts sitting-position cerebral, oxygenation, and spinal risks.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The expanding air collection and mass effect are the decisive findings.

## S02-Q092

A patient sustains a traumatic spinal-cord injury. Which framework distinguishes the immediate mechanical damage from later injury caused by the body's response?

A. Secondary injury accounts for much death and disability after trauma.

B. Prompt diagnosis and early goal-directed therapy mitigate secondary CNS injury.

C. CNS trauma has a primary mechanically disruptive injury and a secondary response in which the body's reaction worsens injury.

D. The ABCDE approach is paramount to CNS-trauma resuscitation.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The requested framework separates immediate mechanical CNS injury from later injury caused by the body’s response. Barash describes primary stretching, compression, or transection and secondary biochemical injury, so C is the complete answer. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 37. Anesthesia for Neurosurgery, PDF p. 3118, passage ID 248575]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Secondary injury contributes to death and disability, but A gives an outcome rather than the primary-versus-secondary framework requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 37. Anesthesia for Neurosurgery, PDF p. 3118, passage ID 248575]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Early diagnosis and hemodynamic support can mitigate secondary injury, but B gives management rather than the two-part injury framework. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 37. Anesthesia for Neurosurgery, PDF p. 3118, passage ID 248575]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash distinguishes primary mechanical disruption from secondary biochemical injury in which the body’s response worsens the lesion. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 37. Anesthesia for Neurosurgery, PDF p. 3118, passage ID 248575]

**D — true but irrelevant:** ABCDE is a broad trauma-resuscitation approach, but D does not distinguish immediate mechanical injury from later response-mediated injury. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2414, printed p. 2025, passage ID 291955]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly describes primary and secondary CNS injury and related management.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question tests the injury framework, not an outcome or resuscitation approach.

## S02-Q093

Following spinal-cord injury, a patient has flaccid areflexia but no hypotension or bradycardia. Which terminology distinguishes this neurologic state from neurogenic shock?

A. Cervical spinal injury can occur without visible skeletal injury.

B. High spinal injuries cause neurogenic shock through vasodilation, loss of cardiac inotropy, and bradycardia from cardiac-fiber denervation.

C. Biochemical, vascular, and electrolyte changes can worsen spinal-cord lesions for up to three days.

D. Spinal shock refers to loss of reflexes and differs from neurogenic shock.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Flaccid areflexia without hypotension or bradycardia indicates spinal shock, which is loss of reflexes and differs from neurogenic shock with autonomic cardiovascular findings. Miller states this distinction directly, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2418, printed p. 2029, passage ID 291972]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Cervical injury can occur without visible skeletal injury, but A does not name the reflex-loss state in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2418, printed p. 2029, passage ID 291972]

**B — true but irrelevant:** High spinal injury can cause neurogenic shock with vasodilation, reduced inotropy, hypotension, and bradycardia, but B describes findings absent from this patient. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2418, printed p. 2029, passage ID 291972]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Biochemical, vascular, and electrolyte changes can worsen lesions for three days, but C describes secondary-injury timing rather than the requested terminology. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2418, printed p. 2029, passage ID 291972]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller distinguishes neurogenic shock from spinal shock, the latter referring to loss of reflexes; absent autonomic signs fit spinal shock. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2418, printed p. 2029, passage ID 291972]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly distinguishes spinal and neurogenic shock and supplies the related trauma facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Areflexia without autonomic cardiovascular dysfunction is the discriminator.

## S02-Q094

During prone neurosurgery without Mayfield fixation, which headrest plan directly protects the eyes from external pressure?

A. Use a padded or foam headrest, place the eyes in its opening, and check them frequently for pressure.

B. Permit impaired venous drainage and elevated intraocular pressure.

C. Use Mayfield tongs despite the stated plan not to use fixation.

D. Keep the head in a dependent position.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** In prone neurosurgery without Mayfield fixation, external eye pressure must be prevented. Barash recommends a padded or foam headrest, eyes in its opening, and frequent pressure checks, so A is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 49. Anesthesia for Ophthalmologic Surgery, PDF p. 4203, passage ID 250579]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash specifies a padded or foam headrest with the eyes in its opening and frequent checks for pressure during prone procedures. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 49. Anesthesia for Ophthalmologic Surgery, PDF p. 4203, passage ID 250579]

**B — false:** Impaired venous drainage or elevated IOP increases ocular venous pressure and retinal-ischemia risk, so B describes a hazard rather than eye protection. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 49. Anesthesia for Ophthalmologic Surgery, PDF p. 4203, passage ID 250579]

**C — wrong condition:** Mayfield tongs are an alternative, but C conflicts with the stated plan not to use fixation and does not give the headrest protection plan. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 49. Anesthesia for Ophthalmologic Surgery, PDF p. 4203, passage ID 250579]

**D — false:** A dependent head can elevate venous pressure, so D increases the ocular risk rather than protecting the eyes from external pressure. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 49. Anesthesia for Ophthalmologic Surgery, PDF p. 4203, passage ID 250579]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly states the padded-headrest plan and positioning hazards.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The preventable hazard is external ocular pressure in the prone position.

## S02-Q095

During cerebral rSO2 monitoring, PaCO2 is changing as ventilation is adjusted. What condition is needed before interpreting the rSO2 trend?

A. Interpret the value solely from the 0.5- to 3-cm sampling depth.

B. Establish steady-state ventilation and interpret rSO2 in relation to PaCO2.

C. Move the forehead probe to make it represent every brain region.

D. Wait only until rSO2 reaches 30% to 35%, regardless of ventilation state.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Changing PaCO2 makes cerebral rSO2 difficult to interpret until ventilation is steady. Cote states that PaCO2 greatly influences rSO2 and values should be interpreted only during steady-state ventilation, so B is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 49 Pediatric equipment and monitoring, PDF p. 2073, passage ID 192141]

**A — true but irrelevant:** NIRS samples tissue 0.5–3 cm below the probe, but A gives sampling depth rather than the PaCO2-dependent interpretation condition. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 49 Pediatric equipment and monitoring, PDF p. 2073, passage ID 192141]

**B — correct:** Correct: Cote recommends interpreting cerebral rSO2 only during steady-state ventilation because PaCO2 can greatly influence the value. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 49 Pediatric equipment and monitoring, PDF p. 2073, passage ID 192141]

**C — false:** A forehead probe reflects frontal-cortex tissue and cannot represent every brain region, so C does not solve the PaCO2-related interpretation issue. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 49 Pediatric equipment and monitoring, PDF p. 2073, passage ID 192141]

**D — wrong condition:** An rSO2 of 30–35% with prolonged ischemia may be associated with infarction, but D gives a threshold consequence and ignores the required steady ventilation state. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 49 Pediatric equipment and monitoring, PDF p. 2073, passage ID 192141]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the PaCO2 influence and steady-state requirement.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Ventilation stability, not sampling depth or a low-value threshold, is the requested prerequisite.

## S02-Q105

According to Chestnut’s Obstetric Anesthesia, 6th edition, a patient has postpartum hemorrhage from uterine atony despite an ongoing oxytocin infusion. Which initial treatment is appropriate?

A. If oxytocin response is inadequate, employ additional uterotonic agents.

B. Use oxytocin as first-line prophylaxis for uterine atony after third-trimester delivery.

C. Use bimanual compression and uterine massage while continuing the oxytocin infusion to restore uterine tone.

D. Recognize an atonic, poorly contractile uterus with vaginal bleeding as a common finding in uterine atony.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The patient has uterine atony despite an ongoing oxytocin infusion, so initial treatment should restore tone mechanically while continuing oxytocin. Chestnut recommends bimanual compression, massage, and continued oxytocin, making C correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 37 Antepartum and Postpartum Hemorrhage, PDF p. 976, printed p. 910, passage ID 274588]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Additional uterotonics are employed when oxytocin response is inadequate, but A does not identify the initial compression-and-massage maneuver asked for. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 37 Antepartum and Postpartum Hemorrhage, PDF p. 976, printed p. 910, passage ID 274588]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Oxytocin is first-line prophylaxis after third-trimester delivery, but B concerns prevention and does not answer treatment of ongoing atony despite infusion. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 37 Antepartum and Postpartum Hemorrhage, PDF p. 974, printed p. 908, passage ID 274582]

**C — correct:** Correct: Chestnut states that bimanual compression and uterine massage with continued oxytocin may restore uterine tone. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 37 Antepartum and Postpartum Hemorrhage, PDF p. 976, printed p. 910, passage ID 274588]

**D — true but irrelevant:** An atonic uterus with vaginal bleeding is a common finding, but D identifies diagnosis rather than the initial treatment requested. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 37 Antepartum and Postpartum Hemorrhage, PDF p. 974, printed p. 908, passage ID 274582]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Chestnut passages directly support the treatment and distinguish prophylaxis, additional agents, and diagnostic findings.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The existing oxytocin infusion and request for initial treatment make mechanical tone restoration the discriminator.

## S02-Q106

During major obstetric hemorrhage, blood-bank supplies are becoming insufficient. Which statement specifically describes the indication and potential benefit of intraoperative cell salvage in this scenario?

A. A leucocyte-depletion filter is associated with very low amniotic-fluid contamination risk.

B. Rapid volume replacement matters more than the particular fluid type.

C. Cell salvage may leave fetal red cells and a risk of maternal isoimmunization.

D. It can prevent morbidity and mortality when hemorrhage overwhelms blood-bank supplies.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem describes intractable obstetric hemorrhage overwhelming blood-bank supplies. Chestnut states that intraoperative cell salvage may prevent morbidity and mortality in this situation, so D is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 619, printed p. 610, passage ID 272941]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A leukocyte-depletion filter is associated with very low amniotic-fluid contamination risk, but A addresses filter safety rather than the indication and benefit asked for. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 619, printed p. 610, passage ID 272941]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Rapid volume replacement is more important than fluid type, but B concerns resuscitation fluid choice and not cell-salvage benefit. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 619, printed p. 610, passage ID 272941]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Cell salvage may leave fetal cells and permit isoimmunization, but C is a risk consideration rather than the stated morbidity/mortality benefit. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 619, printed p. 610, passage ID 272941]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut states that cell salvage may prevent morbidity and mortality when hemorrhage overwhelms blood-bank supplies. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 26 Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery, PDF p. 619, printed p. 610, passage ID 272941]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Chestnut passage directly states the indication, benefit, filter risk, isoimmunization risk, and fluid principle.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks for indication plus potential benefit, not a safety limitation or resuscitation principle.

## S02-Q107

According to Stoelting’s Pharmacology & Physiology in Anesthetic Practice, 6th edition, a patient with preeclampsia is receiving magnesium sulfate. Which monitoring plan best detects toxicity or renal impairment?

A. Use loading/maintenance dosing with urine-output, respiratory-rate, deep-tendon-reflex, and serum-level monitoring.

B. Prolonged magnesium administration can cause neonatal lethargy, hypotension, and respiratory depression.

C. Magnesium sulfate causes systemic, vertebral, and uterine vasodilation in preeclampsia.

D. Magnesium reduces progression to eclampsia compared with placebo or no anticonvulsant.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Magnesium toxicity and renal impairment require both a dosing regimen and clinical/serum monitoring. Stoelting specifies urine output, respiratory rate, deep-tendon reflexes, and serum magnesium levels, so A is correct. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 17. Intravenous Fluids and Electrolytes, PDF p. 1394, passage ID 314098]

**A — correct:** Correct: Stoelting combines magnesium loading/maintenance dosing with urine-output, respiratory-rate, deep-tendon-reflex, and serum-level monitoring. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 17. Intravenous Fluids and Electrolytes, PDF p. 1394, passage ID 314098]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Neonatal lethargy, hypotension, and respiratory depression are possible after prolonged exposure, but B does not provide maternal toxicity monitoring. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 17. Intravenous Fluids and Electrolytes, PDF p. 1394, passage ID 314098]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Magnesium-related vasodilation describes a pharmacologic effect, but C does not identify the monitoring plan for toxicity or renal impairment. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 17. Intravenous Fluids and Electrolytes, PDF p. 1394, passage ID 314098]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Reduced progression to eclampsia is a treatment benefit, but D does not detect magnesium toxicity or renal impairment. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 17. Intravenous Fluids and Electrolytes, PDF p. 1394, passage ID 314099]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Stoelting evidence directly supplies the dosing and monitoring elements and the related benefit/adverse-effect distractors.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The requested bundle is a monitoring plan, not a mechanism, neonatal effect, or efficacy outcome.

## S02-Q108

According to Stoelting’s Anesthesia and Co-Existing Disease, 8th edition, a pregnant patient has severe-range hypertension with end-organ risk. Which medication strategy is supported?

A. Target systolic pressure below 160 mm Hg and diastolic pressure below 110 mm Hg.

B. Use hydralazine, labetalol, or nifedipine as effective recommended antihypertensives.

C. Start labetalol because of familiarity and typical preeclamptic hemodynamics.

D. Add hydralazine or nifedipine as a second drug if blood pressure remains uncontrolled.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** For severe-range pregnancy hypertension, the question asks which medication strategy is supported. Stoelting states that hydralazine, labetalol, and nifedipine are all effective and recommended, so B is correct. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 32 Pregnancy-associated diseases, PDF p. 717, passage ID 235310]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Keeping pressure below 160/110 is the treatment goal, but A gives a target rather than the supported medication strategy asked for. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 32 Pregnancy-associated diseases, PDF p. 717, passage ID 235310]

**B — correct:** Correct: Stoelting identifies hydralazine, labetalol, and nifedipine as effective and recommended antihypertensives. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 32 Pregnancy-associated diseases, PDF p. 717, passage ID 235310]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Starting labetalol is a common practice preference, but C does not reflect the text’s broader statement that all three agents are recommended. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 32 Pregnancy-associated diseases, PDF p. 717, passage ID 235310]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Adding hydralazine or nifedipine after inadequate control is a second-drug step, but D does not state the recommended medication set as directly as B. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 32 Pregnancy-associated diseases, PDF p. 717, passage ID 235310]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly states the recommended agents and the target, preference, and second-drug details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key tests the complete recommended medication set rather than a target or sequencing preference.

## S02-Q109

During nonobstetric surgery in pregnancy, which positioning intervention is imperative to prevent aortocaval compression?

A. Maternal blood pressure and flow are directly linked to fetal perfusion through the placenta.

B. A decrease in maternal blood pressure decreases placental and fetal blood flow because uteroplacental flow is not autoregulated.

C. Use uterine displacement to prevent aortocaval compression.

D. Supine positioning leads to a decline in cardiac output.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** During pregnancy, the positioning intervention must prevent aortocaval compression. Cote explicitly states that uterine displacement is imperative for this purpose, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 36 Fetal intervention and the exit procedure, PDF p. 1576, passage ID 189869]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Maternal pressure and flow link to fetal perfusion, but A states a physiologic relationship rather than the positioning intervention requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 36 Fetal intervention and the exit procedure, PDF p. 1576, passage ID 189869]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Reduced maternal pressure can reduce placental flow because uteroplacental flow is not autoregulated, but B does not specify the positioning maneuver. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 36 Fetal intervention and the exit procedure, PDF p. 1576, passage ID 189869]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote states that it is imperative to prevent aortocaval compression by uterine displacement. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 36 Fetal intervention and the exit procedure, PDF p. 1576, passage ID 189869]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Supine position can reduce cardiac output, but D is a consequence and not the intervention that prevents aortocaval compression. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 36 Fetal intervention and the exit procedure, PDF p. 1576, passage ID 189869]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states uterine displacement and the related maternal-fetal flow concepts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks for an intervention, not the hemodynamic consequence or fetal-perfusion relationship.

## S02-Q110

In animal studies of pregnancy with pneumoperitoneum, which finding was reported when maternal ventilation was guided by end-tidal rather than arterial CO2?

A. A clinical study found the same acidosis and tachycardia pattern.

B. Uteroplacental perfusion increased by 61% at 20-mm Hg pneumoperitoneum.

C. Only mechanical fetal or uterine trauma was reported.

D. Maternal and fetal acidosis and tachycardia occurred.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The animal-study finding associated with end-tidal rather than arterial CO2-guided ventilation was maternal and fetal acidosis with tachycardia. Chestnut states that finding directly, so D is correct. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 408, printed p. 383, passage ID 271927]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A clinical study found no differences in maternal pH, PaCO2, or gradients, but A is not the animal-study finding asked for. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 408, printed p. 383, passage ID 271929]

**B — false:** Uteroplacental perfusion decreased by 61% in the cited animal study, rather than increased, so B reverses the reported direction. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 408, printed p. 383, passage ID 271928]

**C — incomplete:** Trauma is one concern of laparoscopy, but C omits the acidosis and tachycardia finding linked to end-tidal CO2-guided ventilation. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 408, printed p. 383, passage ID 271927]

**D — correct:** Correct: Chestnut reports maternal and fetal acidosis and tachycardia during insufflation in some animal studies when ventilation was guided by end-tidal rather than arterial CO2. [Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf, 17 Nonobstetric Surgery during Pregnancy, PDF p. 408, printed p. 383, passage ID 271927]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Chestnut passages directly distinguish animal findings, clinical findings, perfusion direction, and laparoscopy concerns.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key links the acid-base/heart-rate finding specifically to end-tidal rather than arterial CO2-guided ventilation.

## S02-Q111

According to the AHA 2025 Cardiac Arrest in Pregnancy Algorithm, if spontaneous circulation has not returned during maternal arrest, what delivery target applies?

A. Aim for resuscitative delivery by 5 minutes if there is no return of spontaneous circulation, to improve maternal and, when feasible, newborn outcome.

B. Prioritize early airway management during pregnancy cardiac arrest.

C. Provide continuous left lateral uterine displacement when the fundal height is at or above the umbilicus.

D. Use an AED or defibrillator when indicated during pregnancy cardiac arrest.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** If maternal arrest has no return of spontaneous circulation, resuscitative delivery should be targeted by 5 minutes. Newly retrieved Barash textbook evidence states that perimortem cesarean delivery should ideally occur within 5 minutes and improves maternal and infant survival, so A is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3506, passage ID 249279]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash recommends perimortem cesarean delivery ideally within 5 minutes when ROSC is not achieved, to improve maternal and infant outcomes. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3506, passage ID 249279]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Early airway management is an important pregnancy-arrest adaptation, but B does not state the delivery-time target asked for in the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3529, passage ID 249321]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Continuous uterine displacement is recommended at or above the umbilicus, but C concerns positioning and not the resuscitative-delivery deadline. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3506, passage ID 249279]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Defibrillation remains indicated when appropriate, but D does not answer when delivery should occur if ROSC is absent. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3506, passage ID 249279]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The frozen AHA algorithm was not used; Barash directly supports the five-minute delivery target and pregnancy-arrest context.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence was non-textbook AHA guidance; the new Barash citation is the source of truth for the timing target.

## S02-Q112

According to the AHA 2025 Cardiac Arrest in Pregnancy Algorithm, which bundle best adapts resuscitation to pregnancy?

A. If there is no ROSC, set a delivery goal of 5 minutes.

B. Use indicated defibrillation, address the arrest etiology, prioritize early airway management, place IV access above the diaphragm, and stop magnesium and give calcium when applicable.

C. Detach fetal monitors during pregnancy cardiac arrest management.

D. Provide continuous left lateral uterine displacement when the fundal height is at or above the umbilicus.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** evidence_limited

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for the pregnancy-specific resuscitation bundle. Barash describes indicated defibrillation, early airway management, IV access above the diaphragm, left uterine displacement, and stopping magnesium with calcium when applicable; B captures the requested core bundle. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3529, passage ID 249321]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A five-minute delivery target applies when ROSC is absent, but A is one later resuscitative-delivery goal and not the complete optimization bundle requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3529, passage ID 249321]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash supports indicated defibrillation, early airway management, IV access above the diaphragm, and stopping magnesium with calcium when applicable as pregnancy-specific resuscitation adaptations. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3529, passage ID 249321]

**C — evidence limited:** The frozen algorithm states that fetal monitors are detached, but the textbook corpus does not independently establish that algorithm-specific detail; it is irrelevant to the requested resuscitation bundle.

**D — true but irrelevant:** Uterine displacement at or above the umbilicus is appropriate, but D is one positioning measure and does not give the complete resuscitation bundle. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3529, passage ID 249321]

**Evidence sufficiency:** The keyed textbook retrieval supports the bundle, but the fetal-monitor detachment statement is available only in the excluded frozen AHA algorithm evidence, so that distractor is flagged evidence-limited.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence was non-textbook AHA guidance; the rewrite uses newly verified Barash passages only.

## S02-Q113

According to Barash’s Clinical Anesthesia, 9th edition, a term newborn is breathing or crying with good tone. What cord-management plan is supported?

A. Begin the newborn appraisal by asking whether gestation was term, whether the newborn is crying or breathing vigorously, and whether tone is good.

B. Designate one delivery-room team member specifically to care for the newborn at every delivery.

C. Delay cord clamping at least 30 seconds; if the newborn is not term, breathing vigorously, and well toned, provide resuscitation.

D. Check emergency-resuscitation equipment carefully before delivery.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The newborn is term, breathing or crying, and has good tone, so Barash recommends delaying cord clamping at least 30 seconds; if any appraisal answer is no, resuscitation is provided. C states that conditional plan. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3543, passage ID 249347]

**A — true but irrelevant:** The three newborn appraisal questions are the screening step, but A does not state the cord-clamping and conditional-resuscitation plan requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3543, passage ID 249347]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Designating a newborn-care team member is appropriate preparation, but B does not answer the management plan for a vigorous term newborn’s cord. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3543, passage ID 249347]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash states that if all three appraisal answers are yes, cord clamping should be delayed at least 30 seconds; if any is no, further resuscitation should be provided. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3543, passage ID 249347]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Checking emergency equipment before delivery is appropriate preparation, but D does not provide the cord-management decision asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 41. Obstetric Anesthesia, PDF p. 3543, passage ID 249347]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly supports appraisal, delayed clamping, conditional resuscitation, staffing, and equipment checks.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The answer depends on all three newborn appraisal criteria being positive.

## S02-Q119

A patient rescued after prolonged limb compression develops dark urine and rising creatinine. Which crush-syndrome consequence best explains the expected complication pattern?

A. Mannitol osmotic diuresis and sodium-bicarbonate urine alkalization remain controversial.

B. Severe rhabdomyolysis may produce a total body fluid deficit of 15 L.

C. The most critical treatment for crush syndrome is crystalloid fluid resuscitation.

D. Ischemic muscle injury in crush syndrome causes myoglobinuria, acute renal failure, and profound electrolyte disturbances.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Dark urine and rising creatinine after limb compression indicate myoglobinuria with evolving acute renal failure and electrolyte disturbance. Miller directly links ischemic muscle injury to this sequence, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2422, printed p. 2033, passage ID 291991]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Mannitol diuresis and urine alkalization remain controversial, but A concerns debated treatment rather than the complication pattern in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2422, printed p. 2033, passage ID 291991]

**B — true but irrelevant:** A 15-L fluid deficit may occur in severe rhabdomyolysis, but B is a magnitude detail and does not explain dark urine with renal injury. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2422, printed p. 2033, passage ID 291991]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Crystalloid resuscitation is the most critical treatment, but C gives management rather than the ischemic-muscle complication sequence asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2422, printed p. 2033, passage ID 291991]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller states that ischemic muscle injury causes myoglobinuria, which can lead to acute renal failure and profound electrolyte disturbances. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 62. Anesthesia for Trauma, PDF p. 2422, printed p. 2033, passage ID 291991]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the complication chain and contrasts treatment, fluid-loss, and controversial adjunct details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Dark urine and rising creatinine are stem clues to myoglobinuria and acute renal failure.

## S02-Q120

A patient has severe facial burns with suspected upper-airway inhalation injury and progressive edema. Which early airway plan is supported?

A. Prophylactic intubation is indicated when severe facial or pulmonary burns and upper-airway inhalation injury are suspected.

B. Burn-airway deterioration occurs rapidly because upper-airway epithelium swells precipitously in the first hours.

C. Upper-airway obstruction from edema of the lips, nose, tongue, pharynx, glottis, and subglottis is common after burn injury.

D. A delay in early airway instrumentation may make intubation impossible a few hours later.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Severe facial burns with suspected upper-airway inhalation injury and progressive edema warrant early prophylactic intubation. Cote explicitly recommends prophylactic intubation when these injuries are suspected, so A is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 34 Burn injuries, PDF p. 1490, passage ID 189483]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote states that prophylactic intubation should be performed when severe facial or pulmonary burns and upper-airway inhalation injury are suspected. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 34 Burn injuries, PDF p. 1490, passage ID 189483]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Rapid upper-airway epithelial swelling explains why the airway can deteriorate, but B gives the mechanism and not the early airway action requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 34 Burn injuries, PDF p. 1490, passage ID 189483]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Edema-related upper-airway obstruction is common, but C describes the complication and not the prophylactic airway plan. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 34 Burn injuries, PDF p. 1490, passage ID 189483]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Delayed instrumentation may make intubation impossible later, but D states a reason for urgency rather than the requested supported early plan. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 34 Burn injuries, PDF p. 1490, passage ID 189483]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote burn passage directly states the prophylactic-intubation recommendation and the reasons for early action.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The progressive edema and suspected inhalation injury make early prophylactic airway control the discriminator.

## S02-Q121

Which hemodynamic profile describes burn shock?

A. A hyperdynamic, euvolemic state with increased cardiac output.

B. A hypodynamic, hypovolemic state with rapid intravascular fluid loss and decreased cardiac output.

C. A distributive state with increased cardiac output and no volume loss.

D. Primary cardiogenic congestion with preserved intravascular volume.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Burn shock is defined by rapid intravascular fluid loss with reduced cardiac output, not a hyperdynamic euvolemic profile. Miller directly characterizes it as hypodynamic and hypovolemic, so B is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 83. Burn Management, PDF p. 3164, printed p. 83, passage ID 295657]

**A — false:** Burn shock is not hyperdynamic and euvolemic; Miller states that it is hypodynamic and hypovolemic with decreased cardiac output. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 83. Burn Management, PDF p. 3164, printed p. 83, passage ID 295657]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller states that burn shock is hypodynamic and hypovolemic, with rapid intravascular fluid loss and decreased cardiac output. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 83. Burn Management, PDF p. 3164, printed p. 83, passage ID 295657]

**C — wrong condition:** A distributive high-output state does not describe the rapid volume-loss, low-output burn-shock profile in the stem. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 83. Burn Management, PDF p. 3164, printed p. 83, passage ID 295657]

**D — wrong condition:** Primary cardiogenic congestion with preserved volume does not match Miller’s description of hypovolemia from rapid intravascular fluid loss. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 83. Burn Management, PDF p. 3164, printed p. 83, passage ID 295657]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the burn-shock hemodynamic profile.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key discriminator is low-output hypovolemia from rapid intravascular fluid loss.

## S02-Q122

According to Thrombosis Canada: Orthopedic Surgery Thromboprophylaxis Guidance, which procedure or injury setting is particularly high risk for venous thromboembolism?

A. Evidence-based thromboprophylaxis reduces DVT risk by at least 50% in these patients.

B. Recommend extended prophylaxis for 14–35 days after hip or knee arthroplasty or hip-fracture surgery.

C. Hip or knee arthroplasty, hip-fracture surgery, and major lower-extremity injuries.

D. For most elective orthopedic patients receiving prophylaxis, start anticoagulation approximately 12 hours after surgery.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks which orthopedic procedure or injury settings are particularly high risk for VTE. Barash’s Caprini table identifies elective lower-extremity arthroplasty and hip/pelvis/leg fracture surgery as high-risk categories, supporting C; the frozen web guidance is not used. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4873, passage ID 251827]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A reduction in DVT risk from prophylaxis is an outcome of prevention, but A does not identify the high-risk procedure or injury settings asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4873, passage ID 251827]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Extended prophylaxis after arthroplasty or hip fracture is a duration recommendation, but B does not supply the requested high-risk setting list. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4873, passage ID 251827]

**C — correct:** Correct: the textbook risk table identifies elective lower-extremity arthroplasty and hip/pelvis/leg fracture surgery as high-risk orthopedic settings, matching the procedure/injury grouping in C. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4873, passage ID 251827]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Starting prophylaxis around 12 hours after surgery is a timing detail, but D does not identify which procedures or injuries are particularly high risk. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 57. Critical Care Medicine, PDF p. 4873, passage ID 251827]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The frozen evidence was living-web guidance; Barash’s textbook risk table supports the high-risk orthopedic categories, while timing and prophylaxis outcomes are treated as distractors.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence was non-textbook guidance; the rewrite uses newly retrieved Barash textbook evidence only.

## S02-Q123

After spine surgery, which postoperative analgesic plan best reduces opioid exposure and opioid-related adverse effects within an enhanced-recovery pathway?

A. Select systemic opioids while anticipating respiratory depression, sedation, and gastrointestinal ileus.

B. Use an indwelling local-anesthetic catheter to prolong analgesia with minimal opioid-related side effects.

C. Use traditional systemic opioids as the postoperative analgesic approach after spine surgery.

D. Use multimodal enhanced-recovery analgesia to reduce pain scores, opioid consumption, opioid-related side effects, and hospital stay.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The stem asks for an enhanced-recovery analgesic plan that reduces opioid exposure and adverse effects after spine surgery. Barash states that multimodal analgesia in ERAS pathways reduces pain, opioid consumption, opioid side effects, and hospital stay, so D is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4365, passage ID 250884]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Systemic opioids are traditional and can cause respiratory depression, sedation, and ileus, but A does not provide the opioid-sparing ERAS strategy requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4365, passage ID 250884]

**B — true but irrelevant:** An indwelling local-anesthetic catheter can prolong analgesia with minimal opioid effects, but B is one technique and not the complete multimodal pathway outcome. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4389, passage ID 250927]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Traditional opioids may be used after spine surgery, but C does not address the opioid-reducing enhanced-recovery plan in the lead-in. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4365, passage ID 250884]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash reports that multimodal ERAS analgesia can reduce pain scores, opioid consumption, opioid-related side effects, and hospital stay. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 51. Anesthesia for Orthopedic Surgery, PDF p. 4365, passage ID 250884]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passages directly support multimodal ERAS and the related opioid and catheter facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks for a pathway-level opioid-sparing strategy, not a single analgesic modality.

## S02-Q133

A cyanotic child with reduced pulmonary blood flow is being induced; anesthetic changes in SVR and PVR may alter shunt flow. Which congenital-heart classification applies?

A. A simple right-to-left shunt with decreased pulmonary blood flow and cyanosis.

B. A complex shunt with mixing of pulmonary and systemic blood flow with cyanosis.

C. An obstructive lesion such as aortic, mitral, or pulmonary stenosis, coarctation, or interrupted arch.

D. A simple left-to-right shunt with increased pulmonary blood flow.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The child is cyanotic with reduced pulmonary blood flow, which matches Cote’s simple right-to-left shunt category. Cote labels that category as decreased pulmonary blood flow with cyanosis, so A is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 15 Anesthesia for children undergoing heart surgery, PDF p. 737, passage ID 185903]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote defines a simple right-to-left shunt as decreased pulmonary blood flow with cyanosis, exactly matching the stem. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 15 Anesthesia for children undergoing heart surgery, PDF p. 737, passage ID 185903]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Complex mixing shunts can produce cyanosis, but B does not match the stem’s simple reduced-pulmonary-flow classification. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 15 Anesthesia for children undergoing heart surgery, PDF p. 737, passage ID 185903]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Aortic, mitral, pulmonary stenosis, coarctation, and interrupted arch are obstructive lesions, but C is not the shunt category described. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 15 Anesthesia for children undergoing heart surgery, PDF p. 737, passage ID 185903]

**D — wrong condition:** A simple left-to-right shunt has increased pulmonary blood flow, so D does not match this cyanotic child with reduced pulmonary blood flow. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 15 Anesthesia for children undergoing heart surgery, PDF p. 737, passage ID 185903]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote classification table directly distinguishes simple right-to-left, complex, obstructive, and left-to-right categories.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Cyanosis plus reduced pulmonary blood flow is the exact simple right-to-left descriptor.

## S02-Q134

A child at high risk for postoperative complications from obstructive sleep apnea is scheduled for adenotonsillectomy. Which perioperative plan is supported?

A. Use the five-item STBUR tool because it is easy to administer in a busy perioperative clinic.

B. Operate in a facility capable of treating complex pediatric patients and hospitalize the child overnight for close monitoring.

C. Use the American Academy of Pediatrics guideline for management of children with OSA.

D. Use the proposed pediatric OSA risk-assessment score despite its lack of validation.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** A high-risk child undergoing adenotonsillectomy needs a setting able to treat complex pediatric complications and overnight observation. Cote’s cited recommendation states both, so B is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 93, passage ID 182870]

**A — true but irrelevant:** STBUR is simple and easy to administer, but A is a screening-tool feature and not the high-risk operative setting and admission plan. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 93, passage ID 182870]

**B — correct:** Correct: Cote states that high-risk patients should undergo surgery in a facility capable of treating complex pediatric patients and be hospitalized overnight for close monitoring. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 93, passage ID 182870]

**C — true but irrelevant:** An AAP guideline exists, but C identifies a source rather than the specific facility and overnight-monitoring plan requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 93, passage ID 182870]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A proposed risk score may be unvalidated, but D does not provide the supported perioperative disposition for a high-risk child. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 93, passage ID 182870]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the high-risk facility and overnight-monitoring recommendation and the related assessment-tool facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks for disposition and monitoring, not a screening tool or guideline name.

## S02-Q135

During induction of a child with an obstructing mediastinal mass, airway or cardiovascular obstruction worsens. Which immediate positional rescue should the team be prepared to use?

A. Select biopsy under local anesthesia when airway or cardiovascular obstruction is present.

B. Select biopsy under general anesthesia when there is no airway or cardiovascular obstruction.

C. Turn the child lateral or prone.

D. Have rigid bronchoscopy and tracheostomy available when biopsy under general anesthesia is not feasible.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** During worsening airway or cardiovascular obstruction from a mediastinal mass, positional change may relieve compression. Cote’s emergency preparation specifically says to turn the child lateral or prone, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 661, passage ID 185562]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Biopsy under local anesthesia is selected when obstruction is present, but A is an anesthetic-choice plan and not the immediate positional rescue asked for. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 661, passage ID 185562]

**B — true but irrelevant:** General-anesthesia biopsy may be considered without obstruction, but B describes a different condition and does not provide the rescue maneuver. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 661, passage ID 185562]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote’s warning for an obstructing mediastinal mass is to be prepared to turn the child lateral or prone. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 661, passage ID 185562]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Rigid bronchoscopy and tracheostomy should be available when general anesthesia is not feasible, but D is equipment preparation rather than positional rescue. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 13 Anesthesia for thoracic surgery, PDF p. 661, passage ID 185562]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states positional rescue and the alternative biopsy and airway preparations.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key is an immediate positional rescue during obstruction, not planning or equipment availability.

## S02-Q136

An infant with pyloric stenosis has dehydration, hypochloremic hypokalemic alkalosis, and is not ready for induction. Which correction plan is most appropriate?

A. Proceed directly to induction because correction is immediate.

B. Replace free water alone because vomiting loses only water.

C. Promote renal hydrogen-ion excretion to accelerate correction.

D. Give normal saline with 20 mEq/L potassium at 10–20 mL/kg/h until volume, potassium, chloride, and bicarbonate targets resolve.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The dehydrated infant’s hypochloremic hypokalemic alkalosis requires correction before induction with volume, potassium, chloride, and bicarbonate targets. Cote recommends normal saline with 20 mEq/L potassium at 10–20 mL/kg/h until those targets resolve, so D is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 396, passage ID 184369]

**A — false:** Cote states that correction may take 24–48 hours depending on severity, so proceeding directly to induction before correcting the imbalance is not supported. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 396, passage ID 184369]

**B — false:** Vomiting loses hydrogen, chloride, and water, not only free water, so B does not correct the documented electrolyte and volume deficits. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 396, passage ID 184368]

**C — false:** Renal hydrogen-ion excretion in exchange for sodium exacerbates or prevents resolution of alkalosis, so C does not support correction of the relevant renal effect. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 396, passage ID 184368]

**D — correct:** Correct: Cote recommends NS with 20 mEq/L potassium at 10–20 mL/kg/h until potassium exceeds 3.0, chloride exceeds 100, bicarbonate is below 30, and hypovolemia resolves. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 396, passage ID 184369]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Cote passages directly support the correction regimen, losses, renal physiology, and time course.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The patient is not ready for induction; correction of all volume and electrolyte abnormalities is the discriminator.

## S02-Q137

During prolonged neonatal anesthesia, approximately what endogenous glucose-production rate must gluconeogenesis provide?

A. Approximately 4 mg/kg/min.

B. A rate equal to 60%–70% of the maternal serum glucose concentration.

C. A rate defined by the onset of jitteriness, lethargy, temperature instability, or seizures.

D. A D10W bolus of 2–4 mL/kg followed by no glucose infusion.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks the approximate endogenous glucose-production rate required from neonatal gluconeogenesis. Cote states approximately 4 mg/kg/min, so A is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 384, passage ID 184304]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote states that neonatal gluconeogenesis must yield glucose at approximately 4 mg/kg/min. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 384, passage ID 184304]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Fetal glucose at birth is 60%–70% of maternal concentration, but B is a concentration comparison and not the gluconeogenesis rate requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 384, passage ID 184304]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Jitteriness, lethargy, temperature instability, and seizures are hypoglycemia symptoms, but C does not give the endogenous production rate. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 384, passage ID 184304]

**D — true but irrelevant:** D10W bolus and infusion are treatment for hypoglycemia, but D is an administered regimen and not the normal gluconeogenesis rate. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 7 Fluid management, PDF p. 384, passage ID 184304]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the rate and related glucose concentration, symptoms, and treatment details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks endogenous production, not a glucose concentration, symptom, or dextrose-treatment dose.

## S02-Q138

Why are premature infants and neonates particularly dependent on nonshivering thermogenesis during anesthesia?

A. Regulation of skin blood flow is less efficient in the preterm neonate.

B. Preterm infants and neonates have minimal muscle mass for shivering, so nonshivering thermogenesis via brown fat is their prime source of generating body heat.

C. Anesthesia further disrupts normal thermoregulation by shifting blood flow peripherally.

D. Hypothermia can contribute to developing a metabolic acidosis, impaired peripheral perfusion, and increased risk of infection.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Neonates and preterm infants have little muscle mass for shivering, so brown-fat nonshivering thermogenesis is their prime heat source. Cote states that mechanism directly, making B correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 44, passage ID 182640]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Less efficient skin-blood-flow regulation contributes to vulnerability, but A does not explain dependence on brown-fat thermogenesis. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 44, passage ID 182640]

**B — correct:** Correct: Cote states that minimal neonatal muscle mass limits shivering and makes brown-fat nonshivering thermogenesis the prime heat source. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 44, passage ID 182640]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Anesthesia shifts blood flow peripherally and disrupts thermoregulation, but C describes anesthesia’s added effect rather than the neonatal dependence mechanism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 44, passage ID 182640]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Hypothermia can cause acidosis, impaired perfusion, and infection risk, but D describes consequences and not why brown fat is the prime heat source. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 1 Growth and development, PDF p. 44, passage ID 182640]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the brown-fat mechanism and related thermoregulatory vulnerabilities.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks why nonshivering thermogenesis is relied upon, not another risk of neonatal hypothermia.

## S02-Q139

Multiple pediatric intercostal injection sites require enough local-anesthetic volume while the total bupivacaine dose remains within the safety limit. Which adjustment is appropriate?

A. Ignore concentration as long as the maximum dose is 2 mg/kg.

B. Keep the concentration unchanged and rely only on epinephrine.

C. Decrease the bupivacaine concentration to provide adequate volume while limiting systemic toxicity.

D. Increase the total amount by 30% in infants younger than 6 months.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Multiple intercostal injections require adequate volume without exceeding the bupivacaine dose limit. Cote recommends decreasing concentration to provide the needed volume while avoiding systemic toxicity, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1716, passage ID 190492]

**A — false:** Concentration cannot be ignored: Cote gives a maximum 2 mg/kg dose, while the required volume still depends on lowering concentration; A does not protect against toxicity. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1716, passage ID 190492]

**B — incomplete:** Keeping concentration unchanged and relying only on epinephrine omits Cote’s recommended concentration reduction for adequate volume and does not directly solve dose-related toxicity. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1716, passage ID 190492]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote states that bupivacaine concentration should be decreased to provide adequate volume for the desired blocks while avoiding systemic toxicity. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1716, passage ID 190492]

**D — false:** Cote says the amount should be reduced by about 30% in infants younger than six months, not increased; D reverses the age adjustment. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1716, passage ID 190492]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Cote passages directly support concentration, maximum dose, epinephrine, and infant dose-adjustment distinctions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The requested adjustment balances volume and systemic dose; epinephrine and age reduction are additional considerations.

## S02-Q140

During an ultrasound-guided pediatric axillary block, which local-anesthetic distribution is called the doughnut sign?

A. Separate injections limited to the median, radial, and ulnar nerves.

B. Spread only at the anterior, posterior, and lateral aspects of the artery.

C. Selective spread around only the musculocutaneous nerve.

D. Circumferential spread around the axillary artery.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The ultrasound doughnut sign refers to circumferential local-anesthetic spread around the axillary artery. Cote states that objective directly, so D is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1732, passage ID 190548]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Separate injections may be required for median, radial, and ulnar nerves, but A does not describe the circumferential arterial spread called the doughnut sign. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1732, passage ID 190548]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Anterior, posterior, and lateral arterial injection positions are technical landmarks, but B does not identify the resulting circumferential doughnut pattern. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1732, passage ID 190548]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Selective musculocutaneous-nerve injection is a separate step, but C is not the circumferential spread around the artery requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1732, passage ID 190548]

**D — correct:** Correct: Cote defines the doughnut sign as circumferential spread of local anesthetic around the axillary artery. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1732, passage ID 190548]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly defines the doughnut sign and distinguishes nerve-specific injections and arterial landmarks.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The sign names the distribution pattern, not the number of injections or a single nerve target.

## S02-Q141

According to the 2025 AHA/AAP Pediatric Bradycardia With a Pulse Algorithm, a 4-year-old has a heart rate of 52/min with hypotension and altered mental status. Which sequence is indicated for this bradycardia with cardiopulmonary compromise?
