A. For pediatric bradycardia with cardiopulmonary compromise, maintain the airway, provide oxygen and positive-pressure ventilation, then start CPR if the heart rate remains below 60; give epinephrine if bradycardia persists.

B. The algorithm lists hypothermia, hypoxia, toxins or medications, increased intracranial pressure, vagal tone, and heart block as possible causes.

C. If there is no cardiopulmonary compromise, support ABCs, consider oxygen, observe, obtain a 12-lead ECG, and treat underlying causes.

D. Atropine is used for increased vagal tone or primary atrioventricular block.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The child has bradycardia with hypotension and altered mental status, indicating cardiopulmonary compromise. Miller’s pediatric algorithm directs airway support, oxygen/positive-pressure ventilation, CPR if HR remains below 60, and epinephrine, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 82. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Advanced Cardiac Life Support, PDF p. 3153, printed p. 2644, passage ID 295610]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller’s pediatric bradycardia algorithm gives airway/oxygenation support first, then CPR for persistent HR below 60 and epinephrine. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 82. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Advanced Cardiac Life Support, PDF p. 3153, printed p. 2644, passage ID 295610]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Hypothermia, hypoxia, toxins, intracranial pressure, vagal tone, and heart block are possible causes, but B does not give the compromise-treatment sequence. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 82. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Advanced Cardiac Life Support, PDF p. 3153, printed p. 2644, passage ID 295610]

**C — wrong condition:** The no-compromise pathway supports observation and evaluation, but this child has hypotension and altered mental status, so C does not fit. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 82. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Advanced Cardiac Life Support, PDF p. 3153, printed p. 2644, passage ID 295610]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Atropine is used for vagal tone or primary AV block, but D is an etiology-specific option and not the general compromise sequence requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 82. Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Advanced Cardiac Life Support, PDF p. 3153, printed p. 2644, passage ID 295610]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The frozen AHA algorithm was not used; Miller 10e directly reproduces the pediatric bradycardia sequence.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Frozen evidence was non-textbook AHA guidance; the new Miller passage is the authority for the sequence.

## S02-Q158

Which option is a multicomponent postoperative delirium-prevention program rather than screening, evidence limitation, or treatment after onset?

A. Preoperative delirium screening with a validated tool.

B. Sleep support, reorientation and sensory aids, early mobility, pain care, medication reconciliation, and nutrition optimization.

C. Medication intended to shorten established non-ICU delirium.

D. Recognition that no single multicomponent program is the gold standard.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for a multicomponent postoperative delirium-prevention program. Stoelting lists sleep enhancement, reorientation, sensory aids, early mobility, pain management, medication reconciliation, and nutrition optimization, so B is correct. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 30 Diseases of aging, PDF p. 670, passage ID 235103]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Validated preoperative screening is recommended, but A is detection before surgery and not the postoperative prevention bundle. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 30 Diseases of aging, PDF p. 670, passage ID 235103]

**B — correct:** Correct: Stoelting’s majority program elements include sleep, reorientation, cognitive aids, early mobilization, pain care, medication reconciliation, and nutrition optimization. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 30 Diseases of aging, PDF p. 670, passage ID 235103]

**C — true but irrelevant:** No medication is definitively proven to shorten established non-ICU delirium, but C concerns treatment after onset rather than prevention. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 30 Diseases of aging, PDF p. 670, passage ID 235103]

**D — true but irrelevant:** No single multicomponent program is the gold standard, but D states an evidence limitation and not the component bundle requested. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 30 Diseases of aging, PDF p. 670, passage ID 235103]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly lists program elements and contrasts screening, treatment, and lack of a gold standard.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The requested answer is a bundle of preventive components, not a screening or treatment statement.

## S02-Q159

Which definition describes the Fried frailty phenotype?

A. The nine-level Clinical Frailty Scale defines frailty at a Clinical Frailty Scale score of 4 or greater.

B. Patient frailty is associated with adverse surgical outcomes.

C. Frailty is at least 3 of 5: weight loss, exhaustion, low activity, slow gait, and reduced grip strength.

D. The deficit-accumulation model is based on 92 signs, symptoms, functional impairments, and laboratory abnormalities.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The Fried phenotype defines frailty as at least three of five domains: unintentional weight loss, exhaustion, low physical activity, slow gait, and reduced grip strength. Miller states that definition directly, so C is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 979, printed p. 817, passage ID 285073]

**A — true but irrelevant:** A CFS score of at least 4 defines frailty in the Clinical Frailty Scale, but A is a different model from the Fried phenotype. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 979, printed p. 817, passage ID 285073]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Frailty is associated with adverse surgical outcomes, but B gives an outcome association and not the phenotype’s five domains. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 979, printed p. 817, passage ID 285073]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller identifies at least three of five Fried domains—weight loss, exhaustion, low activity, slow gait, and reduced grip strength—as frailty. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 979, printed p. 817, passage ID 285073]

**D — true but irrelevant:** The 92-variable deficit-accumulation model is a different frailty model, so D does not define the Fried phenotype. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 979, printed p. 817, passage ID 285073]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage distinguishes Fried, deficit-accumulation, CFS, and outcome concepts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The model name is decisive because the alternatives describe other frailty frameworks or associations.

## S02-Q160

According to Barash's Clinical Anesthesia, 9th edition, which postoperative oxygen-and-oximetry plan applies to a hospitalized patient at increased perioperative risk from OSA?

A. Patients with confirmed or suspected OSA should be considered at high risk for a difficult airway and postoperative pulmonary complications.

B. Preoperative initiation of CPAP should be considered, especially in severe OSA.

C. The STOP-Bang questionnaire can accurately identify moderate-to-severe OSA in the preoperative assessment.

D. Continue oxygen until baseline room-air saturation is maintained and use continuous oximetry after recovery-room discharge.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The hospitalized patient remains at increased perioperative respiratory risk from OSA after recovery-room discharge. Barash recommends continuous oxygen until baseline room-air saturation and continuous pulse oximetry after discharge, so D is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 45. Anesthesia and Obesity, PDF p. 3887, passage ID 249991]

**A — true but irrelevant:** OSA increases difficult-airway and pulmonary-complication risk, but A identifies risk and not the postoperative oxygen-and-oximetry plan requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 45. Anesthesia and Obesity, PDF p. 3851, passage ID 249929]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Preoperative CPAP should be considered, especially in severe OSA, but B is a preoperative intervention and not the postoperative monitoring plan. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 45. Anesthesia and Obesity, PDF p. 3851, passage ID 249929]

**C — true but irrelevant:** STOP-Bang can identify moderate-to-severe OSA, but C is a preoperative screening tool and does not state the post-discharge plan. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 23. Preoperative Patient Assessment and Management, PDF p. 1777, passage ID 246298]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash recommends continuous supplemental oxygen until baseline room-air saturation and continuous pulse oximetry after recovery-room discharge for hospitalized high-risk patients. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 45. Anesthesia and Obesity, PDF p. 3887, passage ID 249991]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support postoperative oxygen/oximetry and distinguish screening, risk, and preoperative CPAP.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem’s hospitalized post-recovery-room setting makes continuous oxygen and oximetry the decisive plan.

## S02-Q161

Which statement specifically describes airway resistance and work of breathing during spontaneous ventilation in obesity?

A. Both airway resistance and work of breathing are increased.

B. Supine positioning further reduces FRC and may cause dangerous hypoxemia.

C. Sitting preoxygenation extends apnea tolerance compared with supine preoxygenation.

D. Most induction agents and opioids use lean-body-weight dosing, whereas other agents use ideal or total body weight.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for airway resistance and work of breathing during spontaneous ventilation in obesity. Slinger directly states that both are increased, so A is correct. [Slinger Thoracics 2e (2019).pdf, 33: Thoracic Anesthesia for Morbidly Obese Patients and Obese Patients with Obstructive Sleep Apnea, PDF p. 537, passage ID 230195]

**A — correct:** Correct: Slinger states that airway resistance and work of breathing are increased in the spontaneously breathing morbidly obese patient. [Slinger Thoracics 2e (2019).pdf, 33: Thoracic Anesthesia for Morbidly Obese Patients and Obese Patients with Obstructive Sleep Apnea, PDF p. 537, passage ID 230195]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Supine positioning further reduces already reduced FRC and can cause hypoxemia, but B concerns positioning rather than spontaneous airway resistance and work. [Slinger Thoracics 2e (2019).pdf, 33: Thoracic Anesthesia for Morbidly Obese Patients and Obese Patients with Obstructive Sleep Apnea, PDF p. 540, passage ID 230205]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Sitting preoxygenation extends apnea tolerance, but C is a preoxygenation finding and not the requested resistance/work profile. [Slinger Thoracics 2e (2019).pdf, 33: Thoracic Anesthesia for Morbidly Obese Patients and Obese Patients with Obstructive Sleep Apnea, PDF p. 540, passage ID 230205]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Weight-based dosing differs among agents, but D is pharmacokinetic dosing guidance and does not address airway resistance or work of breathing. [Slinger Thoracics 2e (2019).pdf, 33: Thoracic Anesthesia for Morbidly Obese Patients and Obese Patients with Obstructive Sleep Apnea, PDF p. 540, passage ID 230205]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Slinger passages directly support the requested respiratory profile and related obesity-management facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in specifies spontaneous ventilation, distinguishing the answer from positioning, preoxygenation, and dosing statements.

## S02-Q162

A patient with myasthenia gravis requires neuromuscular blockade for tracheal intubation. Which dosing and monitoring plan is most appropriate if a nondepolarizing blocker is necessary?

A. Tracheal intubation can often be accomplished without neuromuscular blockers because of intrinsic muscle weakness and volatile-anesthetic relaxation.

B. If a nondepolarizing neuromuscular blocker is necessary in myasthenia gravis, the initial dose should be decreased by one-half to two-thirds and the response monitored with a peripheral nerve stimulator.

C. Volatile anesthetics can decrease the required muscle-relaxant dose or eliminate the need for a relaxant.

D. Sugammadex may produce more reliable rocuronium reversal than neostigmine with glycopyrrolate.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** If a nondepolarizing blocker is necessary in myasthenia gravis, the dose must be substantially reduced and neuromuscular response monitored. Stoelting specifies one-half to two-thirds reduction with peripheral stimulation, so B is correct. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 24 Skin and collagen disorders, PDF p. 523, passage ID 234465]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Intubation may often be possible without a blocker, but A is an alternative and does not give the reduced-dose monitoring plan when blockade is necessary. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 24 Skin and collagen disorders, PDF p. 523, passage ID 234465]

**B — correct:** Correct: Stoelting recommends decreasing the initial nondepolarizing-blocker dose by one-half to two-thirds and monitoring response with a peripheral nerve stimulator. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 24 Skin and collagen disorders, PDF p. 523, passage ID 234465]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Volatile anesthetics may reduce or eliminate relaxant need, but C does not state the myasthenia-specific dose reduction and monitoring requirement. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 24 Skin and collagen disorders, PDF p. 523, passage ID 234465]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Sugammadex may provide more reliable reversal, but D concerns reversal choice and not the initial dose and response monitoring asked for. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 24 Skin and collagen disorders, PDF p. 523, passage ID 234465]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly supports reduced dosing and peripheral stimulation and distinguishes alternative blockade and reversal statements.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The conditional phrase ‘if necessary’ makes reduced dose and monitoring, rather than avoiding blockade, decisive.

## S02-Q163

A child with a personal or family history suggesting susceptibility to malignant hyperthermia is scheduled for anesthesia. Which anesthetic trigger specifically must be avoided?

A. Nondepolarizing agents without cardiovascular adverse effects may avoid tachycardia as an early sign of malignant hyperthermia.

B. Total intravenous anesthesia is the safest general-anesthetic technique for children at risk for malignant hyperthermia.

C. Succinylcholine, especially in combination with halogenated agents, should be avoided in children at risk for malignant hyperthermia.

D. Rhabdomyolysis has been reported after succinylcholine in children with Duchenne and Becker muscular dystrophies.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** A child with susceptibility to malignant hyperthermia must avoid the specified trigger. Cote directly recommends avoiding succinylcholine, especially with halogenated agents, so C is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 252, passage ID 183642]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Nondepolarizing agents without cardiovascular effects may avoid tachycardia, but A is an advantage and not the trigger that must be avoided. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 252, passage ID 183642]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Total intravenous anesthesia is described as safest for at-risk children, but B is a technique choice and not the specific anesthetic trigger asked for. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 252, passage ID 183642]

**C — correct:** Correct: Cote states that succinylcholine, especially combined with halogenated agents, should be avoided in children at risk for MH. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 252, passage ID 183642]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Rhabdomyolysis after succinylcholine is reported in Duchenne and Becker dystrophy, but D is a related complication and not the MH trigger policy requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 252, passage ID 183642]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the trigger avoidance recommendation and related TIVA, nondepolarizing, and dystrophy facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks for a trigger-specific policy, not the safest replacement technique or associated dystrophy complication.

## S02-Q164

A child with suspected Duchenne muscular dystrophy needs airway management. Which succinylcholine policy best reflects the hyperkalemic cardiac-arrest risk?

A. Atropine 0.01–0.02 mg/kg IV should precede succinylcholine to decrease arrhythmias, particularly after a second dose.

B. In infants younger than 1 year, 3 mg/kg IV succinylcholine is appropriate because of the larger volume of distribution.

C. Almost all reported hyperkalemic cardiac-arrest cases occurred in male children 8 years old or younger.

D. The FDA issued a black-box warning against routine succinylcholine use in children and adolescents except for emergency airway management because of hyperkalemic cardiac arrests, primarily in undiagnosed Duchenne muscular dystrophy.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Suspected Duchenne muscular dystrophy creates a hyperkalemic cardiac-arrest risk with succinylcholine. Cote reports the FDA black-box warning against routine pediatric use except emergency airway management, so D is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 247, passage ID 183611]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Atropine before a second succinylcholine dose may reduce arrhythmias, but A does not address the routine-use warning in suspected dystrophy. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 247, passage ID 183611]

**B — true but irrelevant:** A 3 mg/kg infant dose is described for volume of distribution, but B is dosing detail and not the hyperkalemic-arrest safety policy. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 247, passage ID 183611]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Most reported cases occurred in young boys, but C is epidemiologic context and does not state the FDA routine-use warning. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 247, passage ID 183611]

**D — correct:** Correct: Cote states that the FDA warned against routine succinylcholine in children/adolescents except emergency airway management because of hyperkalemic arrests, primarily in undiagnosed Duchenne dystrophy. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 247, passage ID 183611]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly supports the warning and contrasts dose, atropine, and epidemiologic details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The suspected disease context makes the routine succinylcholine warning the decisive safety point.

## S02-Q165

During perioperative anaphylaxis, which mediator-effect profile is correctly matched to histamine?

A. Histamine increases capillary permeability, causes peripheral vasodilation and bronchoconstriction, and produces urticaria.

B. Prostaglandins cause bronchoconstriction and attract eosinophils.

C. Platelet-activating factor causes platelet aggregation and release of vasoactive amines.

D. Leukotrienes increase capillary permeability and bronchoconstriction, cause negative inotropy, and constrict coronary arteries.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks which mediator-effect profile is correctly matched to histamine. Stoelting lists increased capillary permeability, peripheral vasodilation, bronchoconstriction, and urticaria for histamine, so A is correct. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 26 Diseases related to immune system dysfunction, PDF p. 586, printed p. 575, passage ID 234734]

**A — correct:** Correct: Stoelting’s mediator table assigns increased capillary permeability, peripheral vasodilation, bronchoconstriction, and urticaria to histamine. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 26 Diseases related to immune system dysfunction, PDF p. 586, printed p. 575, passage ID 234734]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Bronchoconstriction and eosinophil attraction are listed for prostaglandins, but B is not the histamine profile requested. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 26 Diseases related to immune system dysfunction, PDF p. 586, printed p. 575, passage ID 234734]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Platelet aggregation and vasoactive-amine release are platelet-activating-factor effects, but C identifies a different mediator. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 26 Diseases related to immune system dysfunction, PDF p. 586, printed p. 575, passage ID 234734]

**D — true but irrelevant:** The capillary, bronchial, inotropic, and coronary effects in D are assigned to leukotrienes, not histamine, so D is the wrong mediator match. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 26 Diseases related to immune system dysfunction, PDF p. 586, printed p. 575, passage ID 234734]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting table directly maps each mediator to its effects.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Several profiles are true for other mediators; the requested mediator is histamine.

## S02-Q166

A child has repeated mucosal exposure to latex during prior procedures. Which statement best explains the resulting latex-sensitization mechanism?

A. Latex allergy occurs more frequently in atopic individuals and those with selected fruit and vegetable allergies.

B. Latex allergy is an acquired immunologic IgE-mediated sensitivity resulting from repeated exposure to latex, usually on mucous membranes.

**Answer: B**

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Repeated mucosal latex exposure causes acquired IgE-mediated sensitization. Cote states that mechanism directly, so B is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 66, passage ID 182738]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Atopy and selected fruit allergies increase frequency, but A describes risk associations and not the acquired exposure mechanism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 66, passage ID 182738]

**B — correct:** Correct: Cote defines latex allergy as acquired IgE-mediated sensitivity after repeated exposure, usually on mucous membranes. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 66, passage ID 182738]

**C — true but irrelevant:** The 0.3%–4% incidence is an epidemiologic estimate, but C does not explain the sensitization mechanism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 66, passage ID 182738]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Neural-tube and urologic conditions with repeated catheterization are high-risk populations, but D is a clinical association rather than the immunologic mechanism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 66, passage ID 182738]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly supports mechanism, incidence, risk groups, and allergy associations.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks mechanism; epidemiology and risk populations are deliberately secondary.

## S02-Q167

A patient taking a serotonergic antidepressant requires methylene blue for an urgent procedure, and the medication cannot be deferred. Which mechanism specifically explains the perioperative serotonin-toxicity risk from this exposure?

A. The interaction of meperidine with monoamine-oxidase inhibitors is the most classic drug–drug interaction associated with serotonin syndrome.

B. High CNS concentrations of serotonin can produce confusion, hyperactivity, memory problems, muscle twitching, sweating, shivering, and fever.

C. Methylene blue is a potent reversible monoamine-oxidase inhibitor, and phenylpiperidine opioids such as fentanyl, methadone, meperidine, and tramadol act as weak serotonin-reuptake inhibitors; both have been associated with serotonin toxicity in patients receiving serotonergic medications.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Methylene blue is a potent reversible MAOI and several phenylpiperidine opioids weakly inhibit serotonin reuptake; together these exposures can cause serotonin toxicity with serotonergic drugs. Barash states that mechanism, so C is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 785, passage ID 244577]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Meperidine with an MAOI is a classic interaction, but A does not explain the specific methylene-blue/phenylpiperidine exposure in the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 785, passage ID 244577]

**B — true but irrelevant:** High CNS serotonin can produce the listed symptoms, but B describes manifestations rather than the drug mechanism causing risk here. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 785, passage ID 244576]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash identifies methylene blue as a potent reversible MAOI and fentanyl, methadone, meperidine, and tramadol as weak serotonin-reuptake inhibitors associated with serotonin toxicity. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 785, passage ID 244577]

**D — true but irrelevant:** A 24-hour post-methylene-blue washout is a management precaution, but D does not explain the pharmacologic interaction mechanism asked for. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 786, passage ID 244578]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support the mechanism, symptoms, classic interaction, and washout precaution.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem asks mechanism, not symptoms or the post-exposure washout plan.

## S02-Q168

**B — true but irrelevant:** Excess anticholinergic can cause CNS excitation, but B is toxicity information rather than a perioperative bradycardia indication. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 73, passage ID 182773]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Infants require a greater per-kilogram dose to speed heart rate, but C is a dosing comparison and not the indication set requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 284, passage ID 183794]

**D — correct:** Correct: Cote lists bradycardia associated with succinylcholine, anesthetic myocardial depression, neostigmine, and the oculocardiac reflex as appropriate anticholinergic indications. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 284, passage ID 183794]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passages directly state atropine indications and distinguish pain, CNS toxicity, and dose facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question asks indications, not adverse effects or dose comparisons.

## S02-Q169

A child develops upper-airway obstruction during recovery from anesthesia. Which mechanism best explains this complication?

A. Anesthetic drugs may further decrease pharyngeal muscle tone, precipitating airway obstruction during recovery from anesthesia.

B. Postoperative apneic episodes usually involve failure to breathe and/or failure to maintain a patent airway.

C. Anesthetic drugs depress ventilatory responses to both hypoxia and hypercapnia.

D. Central apnea results from decreased respiratory-center output.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The child’s upper-airway obstruction during recovery is best explained by anesthesia further reducing pharyngeal muscle tone. Cote states that mechanism directly, so A is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 35 The extremely premature infant (micropremie) and common neonatal emergencies, PDF p. 1529, passage ID 189650]

**A — correct:** Correct: Cote states that anesthetic drugs may further decrease pharyngeal muscle tone, precipitating recovery airway obstruction. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 35 The extremely premature infant (micropremie) and common neonatal emergencies, PDF p. 1529, passage ID 189650]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Postoperative apnea can involve failure to breathe or maintain patency, but B describes a broad definition and not the pharyngeal-tone mechanism. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 35 The extremely premature infant (micropremie) and common neonatal emergencies, PDF p. 1529, passage ID 189650]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Anesthetic depression of hypoxic and hypercapnic responses can contribute to apnea, but C does not specifically explain upper-airway obstruction. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 35 The extremely premature infant (micropremie) and common neonatal emergencies, PDF p. 1529, passage ID 189650]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Central apnea from reduced respiratory-center output is a different mechanism, so D does not explain the upper-airway obstruction in this stem. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 35 The extremely premature infant (micropremie) and common neonatal emergencies, PDF p. 1529, passage ID 189650]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the pharyngeal-tone mechanism and distinguishes central, ventilatory, and broad apnea descriptions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The complication is upper-airway obstruction, making pharyngeal tone more specific than general respiratory depression.

## S02-Q170

[Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 97, passage ID 182888]

**B — correct:** Correct: Cote states that common postoperative-apnea causes relate to metabolic derangements, pharmacologic effects, or CNS immaturity. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 97, passage ID 182888]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Avoiding hypothermia, hypoglycemia, hypocalcemia, acidosis, and hypoxemia addresses metabolic causes, but C is one category and not the complete grouping. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 97, passage ID 182888]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Residual anesthetic action may contribute, but D is one pharmacologic cause and not the broad category set requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 2 Preoperative evaluation, premedication, and induction of anesthesia, PDF p. 97, passage ID 182888]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly states the three broad categories and examples.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for categories, not one cause or preventive measure.

## S02-Q171

A patient has chronic hyponatremia of more than 48 hours' duration and requires correction before surgery. Which principle should guide the rate of correction?

A. Brain cells may compensate over time by lowering intracellular osmolality through movement of potassium and organic solutes out of brain cells.

B. Hyponatremia treatment should be tailored to intravascular volume status, chronicity, and the presence of symptoms.

C. Chronic hyponatremia should be corrected cautiously because cerebral compensation for hypoosmolarity makes sudden increases in osmolality risk cerebral water loss and osmotic demyelination.

D. In acute hyponatremia, symptoms typically occur at sodium concentrations of 120–125 mEq/L and may progress to seizures and coma below 110 mEq/L.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Chronic hyponatremia permits cerebral osmotic adaptation, so rapid correction can cause water loss and osmotic demyelination. Miller states that cautious correction is required, making C correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 43. Perioperative Fluid and Electrolyte Therapy, PDF p. 1623, printed p. 1354, passage ID 288108]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Loss of intracellular potassium and organic solutes is the cerebral compensation mechanism, but A does not state the safety principle for correction rate. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 20 Fluid, electrolyte, and acid-base disorders, PDF p. 410, printed p. 399, passage ID 233984]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Treatment is tailored to volume status, chronicity, and symptoms, but B is a general treatment-planning principle and not the osmotic risk explanation asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 43. Perioperative Fluid and Electrolyte Therapy, PDF p. 1623, printed p. 1354, passage ID 288108]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller states that chronic hyponatremia should be corrected cautiously because sudden osmotic increase risks cerebral water loss and osmotic demyelination. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 43. Perioperative Fluid and Electrolyte Therapy, PDF p. 1623, printed p. 1354, passage ID 288108]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Acute hyponatremia symptom thresholds are relevant to acute disease, but D does not address cautious correction of this chronic case. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 43. Perioperative Fluid and Electrolyte Therapy, PDF p. 1623, printed p. 1354, passage ID 288108]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller and Stoelting passages directly support adaptation, chronic correction risk, treatment tailoring, and acute thresholds.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The duration greater than 48 hours is the discriminator for cautious correction.

## S02-Q172

Two patients with PaCO2 of 60 mm Hg have respiratory failure: one developed it abruptly with pH 7.20, and the other has chronic CO2 retention with pH 7.38. Which statement best explains this difference?

A. Respiratory failure is often accompanied by decreased functional residual capacity and lung compliance, with pulmonary vascular resistance and pulmonary hypertension likely if it persists.

B. In acute respiratory failure, PaCO2 can be increased, unchanged, or decreased depending on alveolar ventilation relative to metabolic carbon-dioxide production.

C. A PaCO2 above 50 mm Hg without respiratory compensation for metabolic alkalosis is consistent with acute respiratory failure.

D. Acute respiratory failure typically causes an abrupt PaCO2 increase with a corresponding pH decrease, whereas chronic respiratory failure has near-normal pH despite increased PaCO2 because of renal bicarbonate reabsorption.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** At identical PaCO2, the pH difference distinguishes abrupt from compensated chronic respiratory failure. Stoelting states that acute failure causes PaCO2 rise with pH fall, while chronic retention has near-normal pH from renal bicarbonate reabsorption, so D is correct. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 52, printed p. 41, passage ID 232466]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Persistent respiratory failure may reduce FRC/compliance and increase pulmonary vascular resistance, but A does not explain the acute-versus-chronic pH difference. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 52, printed p. 41, passage ID 232466]

**B — true but irrelevant:** PaCO2 can vary with ventilation and metabolic production, but B does not account for the contrasting pH and renal compensation in the stem. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 52, printed p. 41, passage ID 232466]

**C — true but irrelevant:** A PaCO2 above 50 mm Hg without respiratory compensation for metabolic alkalosis can be consistent with acute respiratory failure, but C is a true partial criterion and does not explain the paired acute-versus-chronic pH difference. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 52, printed p. 41, passage ID 232466]

**D — correct:** Correct: Stoelting describes abrupt PaCO2 increase with pH decrease in acute failure and near-normal pH in chronic retention from renal bicarbonate reabsorption. [Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf, 3 Restrictive respiratory diseases and lung transplantation, PDF p. 52, printed p. 41, passage ID 232466]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Stoelting passage directly states the acute/chronic pH and bicarbonate distinction.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Equal PaCO2 with different pH is explained by time-dependent renal compensation.

## S02-Q173

Compared with systemic opioids, which perioperative regional-analgesia relationship is supported?

A. Neuraxial and peripheral regional analgesia can provide superior analgesia and improve outcomes including major morbidity.

B. Use multimodal agents acting at different mechanisms and nervous-system sites.

C. Extend pain interventions into the postoperative phase for maximum benefit.

D. Use aggressive nonopioid multimodal interventions throughout the perioperative period.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Compared with systemic opioids, perioperative neuraxial and peripheral regional analgesia can provide superior analgesia and improve outcomes including major morbidity. Bonica states that relationship directly, so A is correct. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 52: Regional Anesthesia Techniques for Acute Pain Management, PDF p. 2714, printed p. 2714, passage ID 257854]

**A — correct:** Correct: Bonica states that perioperative neuraxial and peripheral analgesia provide superior analgesia to systemic opioids and can improve outcomes including major morbidity. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 52: Regional Anesthesia Techniques for Acute Pain Management, PDF p. 2714, printed p. 2714, passage ID 257854]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Multimodal agents acting at different mechanisms can provide synergistic benefit, but B does not give the regional-versus-systemic relationship requested. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 52: Regional Anesthesia Techniques for Acute Pain Management, PDF p. 2714, printed p. 2714, passage ID 257854]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Extending interventions postoperatively can maximize benefit, but C is a timing principle and not the comparative analgesia outcome asked for. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 52: Regional Anesthesia Techniques for Acute Pain Management, PDF p. 2714, printed p. 2714, passage ID 257854]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Aggressive nonopioid multimodal care is widely used, but D does not specifically state the superior regional-analgesia relationship versus systemic opioids. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 52: Regional Anesthesia Techniques for Acute Pain Management, PDF p. 2714, printed p. 2714, passage ID 257854]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Bonica passage directly supports the comparison and related multimodal/postoperative principles.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The stem asks comparative regional analgesia benefit, not multimodal composition or timing.

## S02-Q185

A patient receiving long-term high-dose opioids reports worsening pain despite dose escalation. Which integrated explanation and analgesic principle best applies?

A. Opioid-induced hyperalgesia is a paradoxical opioid effect in which pain sensitivity increases.

B. During long-term or high-dose opioid treatment, opioid-induced hyperalgesia can cause a paradoxical increase in pain, and NMDA-receptor antagonism can reduce opioid tolerance and opioid-induced hyperalgesia.

C. Postoperative patients who received intraoperative remifentanil may have a higher incidence of OIH and require greater morphine doses.

D. Methadone is a μ-opioid-receptor agonist and relatively potent NMDA-receptor antagonist.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Worsening pain with long-term/high-dose opioid escalation suggests opioid-induced hyperalgesia; NMDA antagonism can reduce tolerance and OIH. Barash supports both elements, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1538, passage ID 245887] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1530, passage ID 245875]

**A — true but irrelevant:** OIH is a paradoxical increase in pain sensitivity, but A omits the NMDA-antagonism analgesic principle required by the integrated lead-in. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1538, passage ID 245887]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash links long-term/high-dose escalation to paradoxical pain and states that NMDA antagonism reduces opioid tolerance and OIH. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1538, passage ID 245887] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1530, passage ID 245875]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Remifentanil exposure can increase postoperative OIH and morphine requirements, but C is a specific perioperative observation and not the general chronic-escalation principle. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1538, passage ID 245887]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Methadone is an MOR agonist and NMDA antagonist, but D names one drug and does not explain the integrated OIH response and treatment principle. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1530, passage ID 245875]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support OIH, NMDA antagonism, remifentanil, and methadone details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question requires both diagnosis of paradoxical pain and the NMDA-based analgesic principle.

## S02-Q186

In severe renal and hepatic dysfunction, which nondepolarizing-blocker profile provides organ-independent elimination?

A. Vecuronium is partly renally eliminated and lasts longer in renal failure.

B. Approximately 40%–50% of pancuronium is excreted in urine.

C. Atracurium and cisatracurium undergo organ-independent Hofmann elimination.

D. Cisatracurium is four times as potent as atracurium and does not release histamine.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Severe renal and hepatic dysfunction favors organ-independent elimination. Miller describes atracurium Hofmann/ester degradation and cisatracurium predominantly organ-independent Hofmann clearance, so C is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2158, printed p. 1814, passage ID 290660] [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2158, printed p. 1814, passage ID 290661]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Vecuronium is partly renally eliminated and lasts longer in renal failure, but A is precisely why it is not the organ-independent choice. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2158, printed p. 1814, passage ID 290661]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Pancuronium’s urinary excretion is a renal-dependent feature, but B does not provide the organ-independent profile requested. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2158, printed p. 1814, passage ID 290660]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller states that atracurium undergoes organ-independent Hofmann/ester degradation and cisatracurium has predominantly organ-independent Hofmann clearance. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2158, printed p. 1814, passage ID 290660] [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 55. Anesthesia and the Renal and Genitourinary Systems, PDF p. 2158, printed p. 1814, passage ID 290661]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Cisatracurium’s potency and histamine profile are separate pharmacologic properties, but D does not address organ-independent elimination. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 24. Pharmacology of Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs and Antagonists (Reversal Agents), PDF p. 822, printed p. 682, passage ID 284318]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller passages directly distinguish renal-dependent vecuronium/pancuronium from organ-independent atracurium/cisatracurium.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The relevant selection criterion is elimination pathway, not potency or histamine release.

## S02-Q187

After nondepolarizing neuromuscular blockade, which monitoring and reversal plan best establishes readiness for tracheal extubation?

A. At a train-of-four count of 2 or more, give sugammadex 2 mg/kg and extubate after 2 to 4 minutes without confirming the ratio.

B. Use head lift and handgrip as sufficient evidence that clinically important residual paralysis is absent.

C. If no tactile or visual train-of-four fade is seen, assume the train-of-four ratio is adequate for extubation.

D. Use quantitative train-of-four monitoring, select reversal according to block depth, and confirm a train-of-four ratio of at least 0.9 before extubation.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Safe extubation requires objective residual-block assessment and reversal matched to depth. Barash recommends quantitative TOF confirmation of at least 0.9 before extubation, so D is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 21. Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs, PDF p. 1635, passage ID 246048] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 21. Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs, PDF p. 1597, passage ID 245986]

**A — incomplete:** Sugammadex 2 mg/kg may reverse a TOF count of 2 in 2–4 minutes, but A improperly extubates without confirming the ratio. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 21. Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs, PDF p. 1597, passage ID 245986]

**B — false:** Head lift and handgrip are insensitive for residual paralysis, so B does not establish safe absence of clinically important block. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 21. Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs, PDF p. 1597, passage ID 245986]

**C — false:** Subjective absence of fade can still correspond to a TOF ratio as low as 0.3–0.4, so C does not establish adequate recovery. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 21. Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs, PDF p. 1597, passage ID 245986]

**D — correct:** Correct: Barash supports quantitative TOF monitoring, depth-appropriate reversal, and ratio at least 0.9 before tracheal extubation. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 21. Neuromuscular Blocking Drugs, PDF p. 1635, passage ID 246048]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support objective TOF monitoring, reversal depth, ratio threshold, and limitations of clinical/subjective assessment.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The key safety discriminator is quantitative TOF ratio confirmation before extubation.

## S02-Q188

Which statement integrates injection-site vascularity, dose, age, and plasma-protein binding when reducing LAST risk?

A. Consider site vascularity and anatomy, use a judicious dose, and account for age extremes and low protein binding.

B. Prevention and risk reduction are the starting point for LAST management.

C. Infants younger than 6 months have sixfold greater LAST risk.

D. Lower binding proteins increase the unbound circulating local-anesthetic fraction.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** LAST risk reduction requires integrating injection-site vascularity, dose, age, and protein binding. Barash lists all four factors together, so A is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1717, passage ID 246186]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash recommends considering tissue vascularity/anatomic site, judicious dose, extremes of age, and low plasma-protein binding when reducing LAST risk. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1717, passage ID 246186]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Prevention and risk reduction are the starting point for LAST management, but B is a general principle and omits the specific factor bundle requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1717, passage ID 246186]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Infants under six months have a higher LAST risk, but C gives one age statistic and not the integrated risk-reduction factors. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1717, passage ID 246186]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Low protein binding increases unbound anesthetic fraction, but D is one pharmacokinetic factor and not the complete site/dose/age/binding plan. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 22. Local Anesthetics, PDF p. 1717, passage ID 246186]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly states the integrated risk-reduction factors and related single-factor options.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The question requires a multi-factor prevention bundle.

## S02-Q189

Which statement addresses both ester-versus-amide metabolism and their allergy distinction?

A. Amide agents have largely supplanted ester agents in modern anesthesia.

B. Esters use plasma cholinesterase and often form allergenic PABA; common amides use hepatic CYP metabolism and true amide allergy is rare.

C. Local anesthetics block sodium channels.

D. CYP1A2 and CYP3A4 maturation affects amide toxicity risk in young infants.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for both ester-versus-amide metabolism and allergy distinction. Cote states plasma butyrylcholinesterase metabolism and PABA allergy for esters, hepatic CYP metabolism and rare true allergy for amides, so B is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 4 Pharmacogenomics, PDF p. 157, passage ID 183201] [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 193, passage ID 183374] [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1678, passage ID 190349]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Amides have supplanted esters in modern practice, but A does not state either metabolism pathway or the allergy distinction requested. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 4 Pharmacogenomics, PDF p. 157, passage ID 183201]

**B — correct:** Correct: Cote states that esters use plasma butyrylcholinesterase and form PABA associated with allergy, whereas amides use hepatic CYP enzymes and true allergy is rare. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 4 Pharmacogenomics, PDF p. 157, passage ID 183201] [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 5 Pharmacology of Drugs Used in Children, PDF p. 193, passage ID 183374] [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 40 Regional anesthesia, PDF p. 1678, passage ID 190349]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Sodium-channel blockade is the shared local-anesthetic mechanism, but C does not address ester/amide metabolism or allergy. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 4 Pharmacogenomics, PDF p. 157, passage ID 183201]

**D — true but irrelevant:** CYP maturation can affect infant amide toxicity, but D is a pediatric maturation detail and not the paired class/allergy distinction. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 4 Pharmacogenomics, PDF p. 157, passage ID 183201]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly supports metabolism, PABA, amide allergy, sodium-channel, and CYP facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The answer must address both metabolism and allergy; mechanism alone is insufficient.

## S02-Q190

A patient develops acute hypotension accompanied by bradycardia during anesthesia. Which ephedrine profile best explains its usual role in this situation?

A. Phenylephrine acts directly at alpha1 receptors and does not depend on presynaptic norepinephrine displacement.

B. Repeated ephedrine dosing produces tachyphylaxis as presynaptic norepinephrine stores are depleted.

C. Ephedrine has direct and indirect adrenergic actions, predominantly displaces norepinephrine from presynaptic terminals, and is commonly used for acute hypotension with bradycardia.

D. Ephedrine’s initial cardiovascular effects resemble epinephrine, with dose-related increases in heart rate, cardiac output, and systemic vascular resistance.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Hypotension with bradycardia calls for ephedrine’s mixed adrenergic profile. Barash states direct/indirect effects with predominant norepinephrine displacement and this common clinical role, so C is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 13. Autonomic and Cardiovascular Pharmacology, PDF p. 969, passage ID 244888]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Phenylephrine directly stimulates alpha1 receptors without norepinephrine displacement, but A describes another drug and not ephedrine’s bradycardic-hypotension role. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 13. Autonomic and Cardiovascular Pharmacology, PDF p. 970, passage ID 244890]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Tachyphylaxis follows depletion of norepinephrine stores, but B explains repeated-dose limitation and not the requested mixed-action profile. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 13. Autonomic and Cardiovascular Pharmacology, PDF p. 969, passage ID 244889]

**C — correct:** Correct: Barash describes ephedrine’s direct and indirect actions, predominant norepinephrine displacement, and usual use for hypotension accompanied by bradycardia. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 13. Autonomic and Cardiovascular Pharmacology, PDF p. 969, passage ID 244888]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Dose-related heart-rate, output, and resistance increases describe an initial epinephrine-like profile, but D does not emphasize ephedrine’s mixed mechanism and bradycardic indication. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 13. Autonomic and Cardiovascular Pharmacology, PDF p. 969, passage ID 244889]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support ephedrine mechanism, indication, tachyphylaxis, and phenylephrine comparison.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Bradycardia distinguishes ephedrine’s usual role from pure vasoconstrictor treatment.

## S02-Q191

A patient needs brief, titratable perioperative heart-rate control during anesthesia. Which beta-blocker profile best supports choosing esmolol?

A. Esmolol clearance in neonates can be twice that in older children.

B. A major potential adverse effect of esmolol is hypotension, particularly during bolus therapy.

C. Esmolol acts predominantly on the sinus and atrioventricular nodes.

D. Esmolol has rapid onset, is readily titrated to a desired endpoint, and terminates rapidly through metabolism by red blood cell and plasma esterases.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Brief titratable heart-rate control requires rapid onset, rapid termination, and easy titration. Cote states that esmolol is metabolized by red-cell and plasma esterases with this profile, so D is correct. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 16 Cardiac physiology and pharmacology, PDF p. 801, passage ID 186194]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Neonatal clearance can be higher, but A is a pediatric pharmacokinetic comparison and not the brief titratable adult-control profile. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 16 Cardiac physiology and pharmacology, PDF p. 801, passage ID 186194]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Hypotension, especially after bolus, is an esmolol adverse effect, but B does not explain why its clearance suits brief titration. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 16 Cardiac physiology and pharmacology, PDF p. 801, passage ID 186195]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Sinus and AV-node action describes esmolol’s target, but C omits rapid onset, titration, and esterase termination central to the stem. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 16 Cardiac physiology and pharmacology, PDF p. 812, passage ID 186248]

**D — correct:** Correct: Cote describes rapid onset, titratability, rapid termination, and metabolism by red-cell and plasma esterases. [Cote 7e (2024).pdf, 16 Cardiac physiology and pharmacology, PDF p. 801, passage ID 186194]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Cote passage directly supports esmolol clearance, onset, titration, adverse effect, and target facts.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The requested endpoint is brief titratable control, making kinetic termination decisive.

## S02-Q192

A patient taking a boosted protease-inhibitor regimen is scheduled for surgery and will receive fentanyl, lidocaine, rocuronium, and midazolam. Which interaction statement best anticipates the anesthetic exposure?

A. Cytochrome inhibition can increase perioperative anesthetic exposure: boosted protease inhibitors strongly inhibit CYP3A4 and increase blood levels of fentanyl, lidocaine, rocuronium, and midazolam; induction or inhibition can likewise alter opioid pharmacokinetics.

B. Fentanyl is metabolized by CYP3A4/5, and CYP3A4/5 polymorphism affects its pharmacokinetics.

C. Propofol is known as a CYP3A4 inhibitor.

D. The CYP3A4 table lists phenobarbital as a barbiturate inducer.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Boosted protease inhibitors inhibit CYP3A4 and can raise levels of several anesthetic drugs. Miller states this interaction for fentanyl, lidocaine, rocuronium, midazolam, and opioid pharmacokinetics, so A is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1089, printed p. 914, passage ID 285621]

**A — correct:** Correct: Miller states that boosted protease inhibitors strongly inhibit CYP3A4, increasing blood levels of fentanyl, lidocaine, rocuronium, and midazolam. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1089, printed p. 914, passage ID 285621]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Fentanyl CYP3A4/5 metabolism and polymorphism are relevant, but B does not address the boosted-protease-inhibitor exposure increase across the planned anesthetics. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 22. Opioids, PDF p. 706, printed p. 589, passage ID 283762]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Propofol is identified as a CYP3A4 inhibitor in the cited Miller passage, but C is not part of the planned fentanyl/lidocaine/rocuronium/midazolam interaction and does not answer the regimen question. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 21. Intravenous Anesthetics, PDF p. 621, printed p. 514, passage ID 283350]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Phenobarbital is listed as an inducer, but D is a table detail and not the inhibitor-mediated anesthetic exposure interaction asked for. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 22. Opioids, PDF p. 731, printed p. 614, passage ID 283867]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passages directly support the boosted-protease inhibitor interaction and distinguish polymorphism, propofol, and inducer details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The regimen contains multiple CYP3A4 substrates, so the broad exposure statement is the decisive option.

## S02-Q193

C. CYP2D6-inhibiting drugs can make it difficult to achieve adequate analgesia with opioid prodrugs requiring CYP2D6 activation.

D. When serotonergic psychiatric medications coexist with methylene blue or phenylpiperidine opioids, clinicians should maintain high suspicion for serotonin syndrome.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks for both serotonergic perioperative interactions and ketamine’s tricyclic-antidepressant hazard. Barash directly links meperidine/MAOI and phenylpiperidine opioids to serotonin toxicity and ketamine/tricyclics to hypertension and dysrhythmias, so B is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 785, passage ID 244577] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 14. Autonomic Nervous System Anatomy and Physiology, PDF p. 1095, passage ID 245106]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Long SSRI half-lives affect washout timing, but A does not address the two interaction mechanisms requested. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 786, passage ID 244578]

**B — correct:** Correct: Barash identifies meperidine/MAOI and phenylpiperidine-opioid serotonin toxicity and acute ketamine/tricyclic hypertension and dysrhythmias. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 785, passage ID 244577] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 14. Autonomic Nervous System Anatomy and Physiology, PDF p. 1095, passage ID 245106]

**C — true but irrelevant:** CYP2D6 inhibition can impair opioid-prodrug activation, but C addresses analgesic pharmacokinetics and not serotonin toxicity plus ketamine/tricyclic risk. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 722, passage ID 244469]

**D — true but irrelevant:** High suspicion for serotonin syndrome is appropriate, but D covers only the serotonergic side and omits the ketamine–tricyclic hazard required by the stem. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 11. Basic Principles of Clinical Pharmacology, PDF p. 786, passage ID 244578]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash passages directly support both requested interaction domains and the related washout/CYP2D6 facts.

## S02-Q194

A patient presents after an acetaminophen overdose. The ingestion time is known and the serum level is available. Which treatment plan is appropriate?

A. N-acetylcysteine has shown no benefit over normal saline hydration for prevention of contrast-induced nephropathy.

B. Acetaminophen is antipyretic and analgesic but has little, if any, antiinflammatory action.

C. For acetaminophen overdose, determine the time since ingestion, measure the serum acetaminophen level, and administer N-acetylcysteine according to the Rumack–Matthew nomogram; N-acetylcysteine is a glutathione donor.

D. Acetaminophen overdose can cause later hepatotoxicity, nephrotoxicity, and elevated aminotransferases.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Known ingestion time and serum level permit Rumack–Matthew nomogram treatment. Miller states time/level assessment and N-acetylcysteine administration, while Barash identifies it as a glutathione donor, so C is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1094, printed p. 919, passage ID 285642] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 52. Transplant Anesthesia, PDF p. 4443, passage ID 251030]

**A — true but irrelevant:** N-acetylcysteine’s unproven contrast-nephropathy benefit is unrelated to this acetaminophen overdose and does not answer the nomogram decision. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 33. Nonoperating Room Anesthesia, PDF p. 2668, passage ID 247813]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Acetaminophen is analgesic and antipyretic with little anti-inflammatory action, but B does not provide overdose assessment or antidote treatment. [Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf, 9. Peripherally Acting Analgesics, PDF p. 879, passage ID 313361]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller directs determining ingestion time, measuring serum level, and giving N-acetylcysteine via the Rumack–Matthew nomogram; Barash identifies NAC as a glutathione donor. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1094, printed p. 919, passage ID 285642] [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 52. Transplant Anesthesia, PDF p. 4443, passage ID 251030]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Later hepatotoxicity, nephrotoxicity, and aminotransferase elevation are consequences, but D does not provide the immediate treatment plan. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 29. Anesthetic Implications of Concurrent Diseases, PDF p. 1094, printed p. 919, passage ID 285642]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Miller and Barash passages directly support overdose assessment, nomogram treatment, NAC mechanism, and later toxicity.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Known time and level make the nomogram-based treatment pathway applicable.

## S02-Q204

After an epidural test dose produces no immediate change in vital signs or neurologic status, which safety principle should guide continued catheter dosing?

A. Intrathecal injection may produce lower-extremity numbness and motor block without a high spinal block.

B. Adding approximately 15 mcg of epinephrine makes intravascular placement produce a slight tachycardic or hypertensive response.

C. Intravascular injection may produce dizziness, buzzing in the ears, or numbness in the lips.

D. A test dose does not rule out unidentified intravascular or intrathecal catheter placement; continue slow dosing with intermittent aspiration, CSF/blood observation, and vigilance for rapid vital-sign, CNS, or motor changes.

**Answer: D**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** A negative immediate test dose does not eliminate unidentified intravascular or intrathecal placement. Miller recommends slow dosing, intermittent aspiration, observing CSF/blood, and vigilance for changes, so D is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Intrathecal test-dose placement can cause lower-limb block without high spinal, but A is a possible finding and not the continued safety principle after a negative test. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**B — true but irrelevant:** Epinephrine may signal intravascular placement with tachycardia/hypertension, but B is a test-dose component and not the broader continued-dosing precautions. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Dizziness, buzzing, or lip numbness may signal intravascular local anesthetic, but C is one possible symptom and not the required monitoring process. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**D — correct:** Correct: Miller says unidentified placement remains possible despite test dosing and recommends slow incremental dosing, intermittent aspiration, CSF/blood observation, and vigilance for rapid vital, CNS, or motor changes. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 58. Anesthesia for Obstetrics, PDF p. 2258, printed p. 1898, passage ID 291170]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly states the residual risk and all continued-dosing precautions.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- A negative test dose is not a guarantee; the question tests ongoing vigilance during subsequent dosing.

## S02-Q205

Neuraxial hematoma is reported more commonly after which block type?

A. Epidural block rather than subarachnoid block.

B. Only blocks involving orthopedic surgery, difficult insertion, or coagulopathy.

C. Only lesions already causing spinal ischemia or infarction.

D. Only neuraxial blocks addressed by ASRA antithrombotic guidance.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** The question asks which neuraxial block has the more common hematoma complication. Barash states that hematoma is more common after epidural than subarachnoid block, so A is correct. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2868, passage ID 248133]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash explicitly states that neuraxial hematoma is more common after epidural than subarachnoid block. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2868, passage ID 248133]

**B — false:** Risk factors include difficult insertion, coagulopathy, age, sex, orthopedic procedures, and coagulation drugs; it is not limited only to the listed circumstances, so B overrestricts the evidence. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2868, passage ID 248133]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Hematoma can compress the cord and cause ischemia or infarction, but C describes a consequence and not which block has greater incidence. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2868, passage ID 248133]

**D — true but irrelevant:** ASRA guidance addresses antithrombotic-related safety, but D concerns an authority and not the epidural-versus-subarachnoid frequency comparison. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 35. Neuraxial Anesthesia, PDF p. 2868, passage ID 248133]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Barash passage directly states the frequency comparison and related risk, consequence, and guidance information.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The lead-in asks for comparative incidence, not risk factors or consequences.

## S02-Q206

According to the current Thrombosis Canada Clinical Guide: DOACs: Perioperative Management, a patient taking apixaban is being considered for spinal or epidural anesthesia. Which plan best addresses interruption and safety assessment before proceeding?

A. For high-bleed-risk neuraxial anesthesia, stop rivaroxaban 3 days before surgery.

B. For high-bleed-risk neuraxial anesthesia, stop apixaban 3 days before the procedure and incorporate renal function plus current platelet/coagulation assessment.

C. For high-bleed-risk neuraxial procedures, stop dabigatran 3–5 days before surgery according to renal function.

D. Use no perioperative heparin bridge because DOACs have rapid offset and onset.

**Answer: B**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Neuraxial anesthesia is high bleed risk, and Miller’s neuraxial guidance lists apixaban interruption at 72 hours while emphasizing procedural risk and renal function; B is the closest textbook-supported plan. The frozen web guidance is not used. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 46. Patient Blood Management: Coagulation, PDF p. 1736, printed p. 1452, passage ID 288649] [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 999, printed p. 837, passage ID 285164]

**A — wrong condition:** Three-day interruption is the rivaroxaban schedule, but the patient takes apixaban; A names the wrong factor-Xa inhibitor. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 46. Patient Blood Management: Coagulation, PDF p. 1736, printed p. 1452, passage ID 288649]

**B — correct:** Correct: Miller’s neuraxial table lists apixaban 72 hours before catheter placement, and its preoperative discussion requires considering drug, bleeding risk, renal function, and neuraxial use. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 46. Patient Blood Management: Coagulation, PDF p. 1736, printed p. 1452, passage ID 288649] [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 999, printed p. 837, passage ID 285164]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Dabigatran’s renal-function-dependent interruption is a different drug pathway, so C does not answer apixaban management. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 999, printed p. 837, passage ID 285164]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Rapid DOAC offset may affect bridging decisions, but D omits the required 72-hour neuraxial interruption and safety assessment. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 28. Preoperative Evaluation, PDF p. 999, printed p. 837, passage ID 285164]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient after targeted textbook retrieval. The frozen evidence was living-web guidance; Miller 10e directly supports 72-hour apixaban interruption and individualized renal/procedural assessment.

## S02-Q207

After major abdominal or thoracic surgery, which analgesic strategy best supports rehabilitation and accelerated recovery while limiting opioid exposure?

A. Combine agents acting at different mechanisms and receptors to obtain synergistic analgesia and physiologic benefits.

B. Use multimodal perioperative recovery because ERAS pathways accelerate recovery and decrease hospital length of stay.

C. Use multimodal regional and nonopioid analgesia to minimize opioid exposure, facilitate rehabilitation, accelerate recovery, and shorten hospitalization.

D. Select local infiltration, peripheral nerve blocks, and neuraxial analgesia as the local-anesthetic techniques in a multimodal pathway.

**Answer: C**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** After major surgery, the desired pathway minimizes opioid exposure while facilitating rehabilitation and recovery. Miller identifies regional and nonopioid multimodal analgesia as the strategy accomplishing those outcomes, so C is correct. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 77. Acute Postoperative Pain, PDF p. 2999, printed p. 2515, passage ID 294880]

**A — true but irrelevant:** Different mechanisms can produce synergy, but A does not specifically state opioid minimization with rehabilitation and shorter hospitalization. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 77. Acute Postoperative Pain, PDF p. 2999, printed p. 2515, passage ID 294880]

**B — true but irrelevant:** ERAS pathways accelerate recovery and reduce stay, but B omits the regional/nonopioid opioid-sparing analgesia component emphasized by the lead-in. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 77. Acute Postoperative Pain, PDF p. 2999, printed p. 2515, passage ID 294880]

**C — correct:** Correct: Miller describes multimodal regional and nonopioid analgesia as minimizing opioid exposure, facilitating rehabilitation, accelerating recovery, and shortening hospitalization. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 77. Acute Postoperative Pain, PDF p. 2999, printed p. 2515, passage ID 294880]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Local infiltration, peripheral blocks, and neuraxial analgesia are component techniques, but D omits the requested outcomes and opioid-minimization purpose. [Miller 10e (2024).pdf, 77. Acute Postoperative Pain, PDF p. 2999, printed p. 2515, passage ID 294880]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Miller passage directly supports the integrated regional/nonopioid ERAS outcome statement and component details.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- The answer must integrate analgesic choice with rehabilitation and recovery outcomes.

## S02-Q208

A patient has acute postoperative pain and a functioning regional catheter. Which plan best provides opioid-sparing analgesia without withholding opioid coverage when it is needed?

A. Use NSAIDs, acetaminophen, and selective COX-2 inhibitors without considering regional continuation or opioid rescue.

D. Use multimodal opioid-sparing care with NSAIDs or acetaminophen and continue a local-anesthetic catheter, while maintaining opioid coverage as needed.

**Answer: D**

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Acute postoperative pain with a functioning regional catheter calls for multimodal opioid-sparing therapy while retaining rescue coverage. Bonica supports nonopioids, catheter continuation, and opioids as needed, so D is correct. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 32: Treatment of Pain in Patients with Addiction, PDF p. 1554, printed p. 1554, passage ID 255464]

**B — incomplete:** Opioid coverage may be necessary, but B excludes the nonopioid and regional opioid-sparing components required by the stem. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 32: Treatment of Pain in Patients with Addiction, PDF p. 1554, printed p. 1554, passage ID 255464]

**C — incomplete:** Continuing the catheter prolongs regional benefit, but C omits multimodal nonopioids and needed opioid coverage. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 32: Treatment of Pain in Patients with Addiction, PDF p. 1554, printed p. 1554, passage ID 255464]

**D — correct:** Correct: the complete plan combines NSAID/acetaminophen, continued local-anesthetic catheter benefit, and opioid coverage as needed. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 32: Treatment of Pain in Patients with Addiction, PDF p. 1554, printed p. 1554, passage ID 255464]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. The frozen Bonica passage directly supports multimodal opioid-sparing care and the need to preserve adequate coverage.

- The stem explicitly requires both opioid sparing and opioid availability when necessary.

## S02-Q209

A patient receiving chronic high-dose opioids develops worsening pain during rapid dose escalation. Which interpretation best distinguishes this pattern from simple opioid tolerance?

A. A paradoxical increase in pain during rapid escalation suggests opioid-induced hyperalgesia rather than simple loss of analgesic effect.

B. Attribute the worsening pain to neuroexcitatory effects in the RVM and PAG caused by rapid opioid reversal.

C. Diagnose ordinary analgesic tolerance because clinically meaningful tolerance occurs unpredictably in a minority of chronic opioid recipients.

D. Use spinally delivered substances because they can reverse or block antinociceptive tolerance and opioid-induced hyperalgesia.

**Answer: A**

**Rationale status:** rewritten

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Rapid opioid dose escalation with worsening pain is the paradoxical pattern of opioid-induced hyperalgesia rather than simple loss of analgesic effect. Barash states that distinction. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1538, passage ID 245887]

**A — correct:** Correct: Barash identifies paradoxical pain increase during rapid escalation as OIH. [Barash 9e (2023).pdf, 20. Opioids, PDF p. 1538, passage ID 245887]

**B — true but irrelevant:** RVM/PAG neuroexcitation follows rapid reversal, not the escalation pattern. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 5: Modulation of Spinal Nociceptive Processing, PDF p. 369, printed p. 369, passage ID 253012]

**C — true but irrelevant:** The prevalence statement about clinically meaningful tolerance is true general background, but it does not explain this stem’s paradoxical pain increase during escalation, which indicates OIH. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 79: Opioid Analgesics, PDF p. 4137, printed p. 4137, passage ID 260697]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Spinal substances may treat OIH, but D is treatment rather than diagnosis. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 5: Modulation of Spinal Nociceptive Processing, PDF p. 369, printed p. 369, passage ID 253012]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Barash and Bonica passages support OIH and alternatives.

**Editorial QA observations:**

- Paradoxical worsening during escalation is decisive.

## S02-Q210

**B — correct:** Correct: Bonica links chronic modulation with neuraxial or peripheral opioid sparing. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 5: Modulation of Spinal Nociceptive Processing, PDF p. 369, printed p. 369, passage ID 253012] [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 79: Opioid Analgesics, PDF p. 4141, printed p. 4141, passage ID 260705]

**C — true but irrelevant:** Spinal substances may reverse OIH, but C omits the chronic-modulation and local-anesthetic implication. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 5: Modulation of Spinal Nociceptive Processing, PDF p. 369, printed p. 369, passage ID 253012]

**D — true but irrelevant:** RVM/PAG effects after reversal are a different mechanism. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 5: Modulation of Spinal Nociceptive Processing, PDF p. 369, printed p. 369, passage ID 253012]

## S02-Q211

**B — true but irrelevant:** Rotation may address cross-tolerance, but B does not identify OIH or regional sparing. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 79: Opioid Analgesics, PDF p. 4141, printed p. 4141, passage ID 260705]

**C — correct:** Correct: Bonica supports OIH recognition and spinal/neuraxial opioid sparing. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 43: Cancer Pain: Principles of Management and Pharmacotherapy, PDF p. 2221, printed p. 2221, passage ID 256820] [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 79: Opioid Analgesics, PDF p. 4141, printed p. 4141, passage ID 260705]

**D — true but irrelevant:** Rodent ketamine data do not provide this complete clinical strategy. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 79: Opioid Analgesics, PDF p. 4141, printed p. 4141, passage ID 260705]

**Evidence sufficiency:** Sufficient. Frozen Bonica passages support OIH and neuraxial sparing.

- Diagnosis and localized analgesia are both required.

## S02-Q212

A patient with advanced cancer has multifocal pain and is entering palliative anesthesia planning. Which framework best integrates tumor treatment, analgesic/adjuvant therapy, nociceptive interruption, and the patient’s goals?

C. Focus cancer treatment on reducing tumor burden, adding analgesic benefit, and providing parenteral access.

D. Modify the tumor source, alter central pain perception with analgesic or adjuvant therapies, interrupt nociceptive transmission with neuraxial or neurolytic techniques, and individualize goals.

**Why the keyed answer is best:** Advanced multifocal cancer pain requires tumor-source treatment, central analgesic/adjuvant modulation, nociceptive interruption, and individualized goals. **B — true but irrelevant:** Palliative surgery may help organ function, but it is only one ancillary intervention. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 43: Cancer Pain: Principles of Management and Pharmacotherapy, PDF p. 2182, printed p. 2182, passage ID 256748]

**C — incomplete:** Tumor treatment and access are included, but C does not include central modulation, nociceptive interruption, and individualized goals. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 43: Cancer Pain: Principles of Management and Pharmacotherapy, PDF p. 2182, printed p. 2182, passage ID 256748]

**D — correct:** Correct: Bonica includes source, central therapy, neuraxial/neurolytic interruption, and goals. [Bonica’s Management of Pain 5e (2019).pdf, CHAPTER 43: Cancer Pain: Principles of Management and Pharmacotherapy, PDF p. 2182, printed p. 2182, passage ID 256748]

- Multifocal pain requires all domains.

## S02-Q260

According to Barash's Clinical Anesthesia, 9th edition, an interventional-radiology procedure will position the patient's head away from the anesthesiologist and prevent immediate airway access. Which anesthetic plan is best?

A. NORA standards require oxygen, suction, backup positive-pressure ventilation, monitoring, and immediate resuscitation equipment.

B. Provide the same standard of care at the NORA site as in the operating room.
